The two units that decide this paper

Technical Paper I is 200 marks. Discrete Mathematics (40) and Data Structures & Algorithms (60) are half of it — more than Computer Architecture or Operating Systems alone. They are also the two most formula-driven units, which makes them the most reliably scoreable: a question you can answer with a memorised formula costs you fifteen seconds, not two minutes.

This guide is built from the same question bank the trainer app quizzes you from — 385 real past questions from the MPSC sittings, grouped into the 24 topics they actually cluster into. Each topic gives you the formulas first, the traps second, and the real questions last, so you can check immediately whether the formula stuck.

How to use it: read the cram sheet until the formulas are cold, then work one topic at a time — method, cards, then the past questions with answers hidden. Tick revised as you clear each topic; the ticks persist in this browser.

Formula cram sheetprint this

Everything the past papers have actually required you to recall, on one page. If you learn nothing else, learn this.

Counting on a set of n elements

The most-reused arithmetic in Unit 1. Every one of these has been asked directly, and the wrong options are almost always the neighbouring formula in this table.

QuantityFormulaWorked at small n
Subsets of A (the power set)2ⁿn = 4 → 16
Elements of A × An²n = 3 → 9
Relations on A (subsets of A × A)2^(n²)n = 3 → 2⁹ = 512 — not (2ⁿ)² = 2^(2n)
Reflexive relations on A2^(n²−n)n = 3 → 2⁶ = 64
Symmetric relations on A2^(n(n+1)/2)n = 3 → 2⁶ = 64
Functions from an m-set to an n-setnᵐm = 3, n = 2 → 2³ = 8. Reversing to mⁿ is the standard trap
Injections (one-one) m-set → n-setP(n,m) = n!/(n−m)!m = 4, n = 5 → 5·4·3·2 = 120; zero if m > n
Surjections (onto) m-set → n-setΣₖ₌₀ⁿ (−1)ᵏ C(n,k)(n−k)ᵐm = 4, n = 3 → 81 − 48 + 3 = 36
Bijections (only when m = n)n!n = 4 → 24
Equivalence relations on A = partitions of ABell number BₙB₁=1, B₂=2, B₃=5, B₄=15, B₅=52 — memorise these five
Largest equivalence relation (A × A)n² ordered pairsn = 5 → 25
Smallest equivalence relation (identity)n ordered pairsn = 5 → 5
Contiguous substrings of a length-n stringC(n+1,2) = n(n+1)/2n = 4 → 10

Permutations, combinations and arrangements

Decide two things before reaching for a formula: does order matter, and is repetition allowed? Those answers pick the row.

SituationFormulaWorked
Order matters, no repetition (permutation)P(n,r) = n!/(n−r)!P(5,4) = 120
Order does not matter (combination)C(n,r) = n!/(r!(n−r)!)C(7,3) = 35
Order matters, repetition allowednʳ3 letters from 4 → 64
Order does not matter, repetition allowed (stars and bars)C(n+r−1, r)r = 3 from n = 4 types → C(6,3) = 20
Circular arrangements of n objects(n−1)!n = 5 → 24
A block of k items must stay together(n−k+1)! × k!'SIGNATURE' (9 distinct letters) with its 4 vowels together → 6! × 4! = 17,280
Everyone shakes hands onceC(n,2) = n(n−1)/2n = 10 → 45
Word with repeated lettersn! / (p₁! p₂! …)'LEVEL' → 5!/(2!·2!) = 30

Summation identities (for induction questions)

Section-B induction proofs come from this list.

SumClosed form
1 + 2 + … + nn(n+1)/2
1² + 2² + … + n²n(n+1)(2n+1)/6
1³ + 2³ + … + n³[n(n+1)/2]² — the square of the previous row
1 + 3 + 5 + … + (2n−1) (odd numbers)n² — the trap answer is n(n+1)/2
1 + 2 + 4 + … + 2ⁿ2^(n+1) − 1

Graph theory — the formula block

The single highest-yield table in Unit 1. Note how many answers are n − 1; the papers offer n and n+1 every time.

QuantityFormula / condition
Handshaking lemmaΣ deg(v) = 2e — so the number of odd-degree vertices is always even

Edges in a tree on n verticesn − 1

Labelled spanning trees of Kₙ (Cayley)n^(n−2) — n = 4 → 16

Rank of the incidence matrix (connected)n − 1
Edge connectivity of Kₙn − 1
Length of a Hamiltonian path on n verticesn − 1 edges
Euler circuit existsconnected and every vertex has even degree

Simple planar bound (v ≥ 3)e ≤ 3v − 6; bipartite planar e ≤ 2v − 4

Paths between two vertices of a treeexactly one

Chomsky hierarchy — memorise cold

Answers a whole cluster on its own. Type 0 is the most powerful and least restricted; Type 3 the most restricted. Candidates invert this under pressure.

TypeGrammarLanguageRecognising machineProduction form
0UnrestrictedRecursively enumerableTuring machineα → β
1Context-sensitiveContext-sensitiveLinear bounded automaton (LBA)αAβ → αγβ, |γ| ≥ 1
2Context-freeContext-freePushdown automaton (PDA)A → α (single non-terminal on the left)
3Regular / right-linearRegularFinite automaton (DFA/NFA)A → aB or A → a

Closure properties

Regular languages are closed under everything listed. Context-free languages fail on intersection and complement — that gap is what the papers test.

OperationRegularContext-free
Union✅✅
Concatenation✅✅
Kleene star✅✅
Intersection✅❌
Complement✅❌
CFL ∩ Regular—✅ always context-free (not necessarily regular)

Automata facts that recur every sitting

FactDetail
DFA is a 5-tupleM = (Q, Σ, δ, q₀, F) — no output alphabet
Mealy and Moore are 6-tuples(Q, Σ, Δ, δ, λ, q₀) — same size; they differ only in λ
Moore outputdepends on the present state only
Mealy outputdepends on the present state and the current input
NFA vs DFAdiffer only in the transition function; both are equally powerful in the languages they accept
NFA → DFA (subset construction)an NFA with k states gives a DFA with at most 2ᵏ states
NFA acceptanceaccepts if at least one path reaches a final state
Kleene's theoremregular ⟺ DFA ⟺ NFA ⟺ regular expression — all equivalent
Pumping lemmaproves non-regularity only; it is necessary, not sufficient
Infinite ≠ non-regulara* is infinite and regular; aⁿbⁿ is not
A PDA with two stacksis Turing-complete
Palindromesaccepted by an NPDA, not a DPDA; never by a DFA
CNF derivation lengtha string of length n takes exactly 2n − 1 steps
Context-sensitive languagesare always recursive (membership is decidable)

LanguageClass
(aa)*, i.e. {a^(2i)}Regular

Posets, lattices and Boolean algebra

TermDefinition / test
Partial orderreflexive + antisymmetric + transitive
Equivalence relationreflexive + symmetric + transitive
Latticea poset in which every pair has both a join (LUB, ∨) and a meet (GLB, ∧)
Maximal vs maximummaximal = nothing above it (can be several); maximum = above everything (at most one)
Chain / antichainchain = every pair comparable (totally ordered); antichain = no two comparable
Hasse diagramdrop reflexive loops and transitive edges; keep only covering relations
Distributive latticea ∨ (b ∧ c) = (a ∨ b) ∧ (a ∨ c) and its dual. The non-distributive counterexamples are the pentagon N₅ and the diamond M₃
Holds in every lattice (so cannot prove distributivity)commutativity, idempotency a ∧ a = a, absorption a ∨ (a ∧ b) = a
Complemented latticebounded, and every a has a′ with a ∨ a′ = 1 and a ∧ a′ = 0
Boolean algebraa complemented distributive lattice
Bounded latticehas a bottom 0 (GLB of all) and a top 1 (LUB of all)

Logic and Boolean identities

IdentityStatement
Implicationp → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q
Contrapositive (equivalent)p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p
Converse / inverse (not equivalent)q → p / ¬p → ¬q
Biconditionalp ↔ q ≡ (p → q) ∧ (q → p)
De Morgan¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q; ¬(p ∨ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q
Absorption (asked verbatim)p ∧ (¬p ∨ q) ≡ p ∧ q
A standard tautologyp → (q → p)
Boolean absorptionA + A′B = A + B and A + AB = A
Consensus theoremAB + A′C + BC = AB + A′C (needs the complement pair)
Half adderSum = A ⊕ B, Carry = AB
Half subtractorDifference = A ⊕ B, Borrow = A′B
SOP vs POSSOP = OR of AND terms (AB + CD); POS = AND of OR terms
'Some A are B'∃x (A(x) ∧ B(x)) — existential pairs with AND
'All A are B'∀x (A(x) → B(x)) — universal pairs with IMPLIES

Fuzzy sets

Small topic, reliably examined, entirely formula-driven — the cheapest marks in Unit 1.

Operation / termDefinition
Membership functionμ_A(x) ∈ [0,1] (a crisp set allows only {0,1})
Union (Zadeh) — the OR analogueμ_{A∪B}(x) = max(μ_A(x), μ_B(x))
Intersection (Zadeh) — the AND analogueμ_{A∩B}(x) = min(μ_A(x), μ_B(x))
Complementμ_{A′}(x) = 1 − μ_A(x)
Algebraic sum (a distractor for union)μ_A + μ_B − μ_A·μ_B
Algebraic product (a distractor for intersection)μ_A · μ_B
α-cutA_α = { x : μ_A(x) ≥ α } — a crisp set
Heighth(A) = sup μ_A(x); A is normal iff h(A) = 1
Support{ x : μ_A(x) > 0 }
Core{ x : μ_A(x) = 1 }
Cross-over pointthe x where μ_A(x) = 0.5
Fuzzy logic addressesvagueness, not randomness (that is probability)
Mamdani pipelineFuzzification → rule evaluation → aggregation → defuzzification

Group theory (peripheral but asked)

FactDetail
Group axiomsclosure, associativity, identity, inverses. Commutativity is not required — that is *abelian*
Lagrange's theoremthe order of every element divides the group order
Groups of order 4exactly two up to isomorphism: ℤ₄ (element orders 1,2,4,4) and the Klein four-group ℤ₂×ℤ₂ (1,2,2,2)
ℚ under multiplicationnot a group — 0 has no inverse
Invertible n×n matricesform the group GL(n) under multiplication
Modular inversesolve a·x ≡ 1 (mod m); e.g. 3·5 = 15 ≡ 1 (mod 7) → 5

Sorting — the facts behind the table

PointDetail
Stable setbubble, insertion, merge, counting, radix, bucket. Everything else reorders equal keys.
Not in-placemerge, counting, radix, bucket
Only common O(n log n)-average sort with an O(n²) worst casequick sort
Quick sort worst case occurs whenthe pivot is always the smallest or largest element (e.g. first-element pivot on already-sorted input)
Selection sort swapsexactly n − 1, i.e. O(n) — its *comparisons* are O(n²). Do not conflate the two
Selection sort's best casestill O(n²) — the only simple sort with no adaptive behaviour
Bubble sort's O(n) best caseconditional on the swapped flag; without it there is no O(n) case
Heap sort is O(n log n) in the worst caseonly heap construction is O(n)
Quick sort's O(log n) spaceis recursion stack, not data movement — it still counts as in-place
Lower bound for comparison sortingΩ(n log n). Counting/radix/bucket beat it only by not comparing keys
Number of passes for bubble/selection on n elementsn − 1
Bubble sort comparisons, worst casen(n−1)/2
Insertion sort comparisons, worst case(n² + n − 2)/2
Radix sort costd × (n + k) — e.g. 13 values, 10 buckets, 4-digit keys → 4 × (13+10) = 92
Worst-case ranking, best to worstmerge = heap < quick = selection = bubble = insertion
Best on nearly-sorted inputinsertion sort — O(n)
Use selection sort whenrecords are large but keys small — it makes only n−1 swaps, so swap cost dominates
Divide-and-conquer sortsmerge and quick

Array address calculation

Purely mechanical marks. The one thing that loses them is forgetting to subtract the lower bound when the array is not 0-indexed.

CaseFormula
1-D, A[i], lower bound lb, element width wBase + w × (i − lb)
2-D row-major A[i][j], n₂ = number of columnsBase + w × [ (i − lb₁) × n₂ + (j − lb₂) ]
2-D column-major A[i][j], n₁ = number of rowsBase + w × [ (j − lb₂) × n₁ + (i − lb₁) ]
k-D row-majoreach index is weighted by the sizes of all dimensions to its right
k-D column-majoreach index is weighted by the sizes of all dimensions to its left
3-D row-major A[i][j][k], extents M, N, PBase + w × [ (i−lb₁)·N·P + (j−lb₂)·P + (k−lb₃) ]
Dimension sizenᵢ = upperᵢ − lowerᵢ + 1
Total elementsproduct of all the dimension sizes. A(−1…m, 1…m) holds (m+2) × m — the negative lower bound is the trap
Which subscript varies fastestrow-major → the rightmost; column-major → the leftmost
Cost of an address computationO(k) — independent of array size
Length of a nested listcount top-level elements only: ((5,6), 0, (9,6,3,5)) has length 3

Trees — node and height relations

Read the height convention first. Counting *levels* and counting *edges* differ by one, and the papers have used both. If 'height 10' has 1023 among the options, they are counting levels; if 2047 appears, they are counting edges.

QuantityFormula
Max nodes at level L (root at level 0)2ᴸ
Max nodes in a tree of h levels2ʰ − 1 (h = 10 → 1023)
Max nodes with edge-height h2^(h+1) − 1
Min height (levels) for n nodes⌈log₂(n+1)⌉
Leaves vs degree-2 nodes in a binary treeL = D₂ + 1
Full binary tree with i internal nodeshas i + 1 leaves and 2i + 1 nodes total
Full binary tree with L leaveshas 2L − 1 nodes total (L = 10 → 19)
Distinct binary trees with n nodes (Catalan)C(2n,n)/(n+1) → 1, 2, 5, 14, 42 for n = 1…5
Minimum nodes in an AVL tree of height hN(h) = N(h−1) + N(h−2) + 1, N(0)=1, N(1)=2 → 1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20, 33, 54
AVL balance factorheight(left) − height(right) ∈ {−1, 0, +1}
B-tree of order m — children≤ m, and ≥ ⌈m/2⌉ (root exempt)
B-tree of order m — keys per node≤ m − 1, and ≥ ⌈m/2⌉ − 1
B-tree structural invariantall leaves lie at the same level — this is what separates it from a binary/AVL tree
B+ tree advantageleaves are chained in sequence, so range queries are faster; internal nodes are index-only
Height of a heap / complete tree with n nodes⌊log₂ n⌋
Preorder = postorder impliesthe tree has exactly one node
Inorder traversal of a BSTyields keys in ascending order
Complete vs full binary treecomplete = every level filled except possibly the last, which fills left to right. Full/strict = every internal node has exactly 2 children. A heap is complete, not full
Deleting a two-child BST nodereplace it with its inorder successor (or predecessor — both are standard)

Why a circular queue existsin a linear queue, dequeuing only advances front, stranding the slots behind it — the queue reads full with space free at the start
Circular queue — advancerear = (rear + 1) % n, front = (front + 1) % n
Circular queue — full(rear + 1) % n == front

FactDetail
Prim vs Kruskal on distinct weightsthey produce the same MST — the MST is unique when all edge weights are distinct

Which to preferKruskal on sparse graphs; matrix-based Prim on dense graphs

Bipartite iffthe graph has no odd-length cycle

Part 1 — Discrete Mathematics40 marks

Unit 1 of Technical Paper I.

Sets, Relations and Functions

The counting formulas here (2^n, 2^(n²), n!/(n−m)!) are the single most reused piece of arithmetic in the whole unit.

11 concept cards24 past questions

Worked method

Checking relation properties fast
  1. Reflexive: every (a,a) for a in the set must be present. One missing pair kills it.
  2. Symmetric: for every (a,b) present, (b,a) must also be present.
  3. Antisymmetric: if both (a,b) and (b,a) are present then a must equal b. A relation can be neither symmetric nor antisymmetric — that is a real option, not a trick.
  4. Transitive: for every (a,b) and (b,c), check that (a,c) is present. Work through the pairs systematically; this is where mistakes happen.
  5. Equivalence = reflexive + symmetric + transitive. Partial order = reflexive + antisymmetric + transitive. The only difference is the middle word.
Worked example

*R = {(x,y), (x,z), (z,x), (z,y)} on {x, y, z}.*

Symmetric? (x,y) is present but (y,x) is not → not symmetric.

Antisymmetric? Both (x,z) and (z,x) are present but x ≠ z → not antisymmetric.

So it is neither — which is the keyed answer, and the option most candidates never consider.

Sets, subsets, power set, cardinality

RememberPower set = one yes/no vote per element, so 2ⁿ.

A set is an unordered collection of distinct objects, and its power set P(A) is the set of all subsets of A, of which there are 2^|A|.

Key facts & formulas
  • |P(A)| = 2ⁿ when |A| = n; P(A) is never empty, since ∅ ∈ P(A).
  • The number of k-element subsets of an n-set is C(n,k) = n!/(k!(n−k)!).
  • |P(P(A))| = 2^(2ⁿ) — doubly exponential; |A| = 2 already gives 16.
  • ∅ ⊆ A for every set A, and A ⊆ A.
  • |∅| = 0 but |{∅}| = 1: {∅} is a one-element set whose only element is the empty set.
  • For |A| = n, |A × A| = n² and hence |P(A × A)| = 2^(n²).
  • Set equality is proved by double inclusion: show A ⊆ B and B ⊆ A.
Traps
  • 'How many proper subsets' normally means all except A itself, giving 2ⁿ − 1. Only answer 2ⁿ − 2 if the question explicitly says non-empty proper subsets.
  • ∅ and {∅} are different objects; {∅} has one element, ∅ has none.
  • |P(A × A)| = 2^(n²), while |P(A) × P(A)| = 2ⁿ · 2ⁿ = 2^(2n). They coincide only at n = 2 (both 16), which is exactly why papers set n = 3 or 4.
  • Element versus subset: 1 ∈ {1,2} is correct, 1 ⊆ {1,2} is not — you need {1} ⊆ {1,2}.
  • A ∈ P(A) always holds; do not read that as A ⊆ P(A).
Full explanation

Write x ∈ A for membership. A ⊆ B means every element of A lies in B; A ⊂ B (proper) additionally requires A ≠ B. Two sets are equal exactly when A ⊆ B and B ⊆ A — that mutual-inclusion argument is the standard way to prove a set identity in the conventional paper. The empty set ∅ is a subset of every set, including itself.

Cardinality |A| is the number of elements. The power set P(A) is the set whose elements are all the subsets of A. For |A| = n, choosing a subset means making n independent in/out decisions, so |P(A)| = 2ⁿ. Splitting by size gives the binomial identity Σ from k=0 to n of C(n,k) = 2ⁿ.

Worked: A = {1,2,3}. P(A) = {∅, {1}, {2}, {3}, {1,2}, {1,3}, {2,3}, {1,2,3}} — that is 8 = 2³ elements.

Exam shape: 'if |A| = 3, how many elements are in P(A × A)?' Here |A × A| = 3² = 9, so |P(A × A)| = 2⁹ = 512. The nesting is where marks are lost, not the formula.

Sets carry no order and no repetition: {1,2,2,3} = {3,2,1} = {1,2,3}.

Set operations and their algebra

RememberDe Morgan: break the bar, flip the operator.

Union, intersection, difference and complement combine sets, and they obey a fixed algebra — idempotent, commutative, associative, distributive, absorption, complement and De Morgan laws.

Key facts & formulas
  • De Morgan: (A ∪ B)′ = A′ ∩ B′ and (A ∩ B)′ = A′ ∪ B′.
  • Both distributive laws hold: A ∩ (B ∪ C) = (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ C) and A ∪ (B ∩ C) = (A ∪ B) ∩ (A ∪ C).
  • Absorption: A ∪ (A ∩ B) = A and A ∩ (A ∪ B) = A.
  • A − B = A ∩ B′, and |A − B| = |A| − |A ∩ B|.
  • A Δ B = (A − B) ∪ (B − A); it is associative, A Δ ∅ = A and A Δ A = ∅.
  • Duality principle: swapping ∪ ↔ ∩ and U ↔ ∅ turns a valid identity into another valid identity.
  • (P(U), ∪, ∩, ′, ∅, U) is a Boolean algebra — the same axioms Unit 1 later states for lattices.
Traps
  • De Morgan flips the operator, not just the bars: (A ∪ B)′ = A′ ∪ B′ is the standard wrong option.
  • Set difference is neither commutative nor associative. With A = {1}, B = ∅, C = {1}: (A − B) − C = ∅ but A − (B − C) = {1}.
  • Cancellation fails: A ∪ B = A ∪ C does not give B = C (take A = B = {1}, C = ∅). It does hold for symmetric difference.
  • Both distributive laws hold for sets, unlike arithmetic where × distributes over + but + does not over ×; papers exploit the false analogy.
  • A − B = B − A forces A = B, because the two differences are disjoint and can only be equal when both are empty.
Full explanation

Fix a universal set U. A ∪ B = {x : x ∈ A or x ∈ B}; A ∩ B = {x : x ∈ A and x ∈ B}; A − B = {x : x ∈ A and x ∉ B}; A′ = U − A. Note A − B = A ∩ B′ — rewriting a difference that way is usually the fastest route through a proof.

The algebra has the shape of a Boolean algebra, and the paper expects the textbook names: idempotent (A ∪ A = A), commutative, associative, distributive in both directions, identity (A ∪ ∅ = A, A ∩ U = A), complement (A ∪ A′ = U, A ∩ A′ = ∅), involution ((A′)′ = A), absorption (A ∪ (A ∩ B) = A) and De Morgan.

De Morgan checked directly: x ∈ (A ∪ B)′ means x is in neither A nor B, i.e. x ∈ A′ and x ∈ B′, i.e. x ∈ A′ ∩ B′. The operator flips as the bar breaks.

Duality: interchange ∪ with ∩ and U with ∅ in any valid identity and the result is again valid — that halves what you memorise.

Symmetric difference A Δ B = (A − B) ∪ (B − A) = (A ∪ B) − (A ∩ B). It is commutative and associative, has identity ∅, and A Δ A = ∅.

Cartesian product

The Cartesian product A × B is the set of all ordered pairs (a, b) with a ∈ A and b ∈ B, and |A × B| = |A| · |B|.

Key facts & formulas
  • |A × B| = |A| · |B|; for a k-fold product, multiply all k cardinalities.
  • A × B = B × A only when A = B, or A = ∅, or B = ∅.
  • A × B = ∅ if and only if A = ∅ or B = ∅.
  • A × (B ∪ C) = (A × B) ∪ (A × C); the same distribution holds over ∩ and over −.
  • Number of relations from an m-set to an n-set = 2^(mn); number of relations on an n-set = 2^(n²).
  • An n-ary relation is a subset of A₁ × A₂ × … × Aₙ — this is the formal basis of the relational data model.
  • Strictly, (A × B) × C and A × (B × C) are different sets of nested pairs, both informally identified with triples.
Traps
  • (a, b) ≠ (b, a) unless a = b — an ordered pair is not a two-element set.
  • For |A| = n, |P(A × A)| = 2^(n²), not (2ⁿ)² = 2^(2n); the two agree only at n = 2.
  • The product does distribute over union, intersection and difference — candidates often assume it fails and reject a correct option.
  • A × ∅ = ∅, not {∅} and not ∅ × something; there is simply no second coordinate to choose.
  • |A × B| counts pairs, not distinct symbols: if A and B overlap, elements are still paired, never merged.
Full explanation

An ordered pair (a, b) carries position: (a, b) = (c, d) exactly when a = c and b = d. The Cartesian product A × B = {(a, b) : a ∈ A, b ∈ B} collects every such pair.

Worked: A = {1, 2}, B = {x, y}. Then A × B = {(1,x), (1,y), (2,x), (2,y)} — four pairs, |A × B| = 2 × 2. But B × A = {(x,1), (y,1), (x,2), (y,2)} is a different set, so the product is not commutative.

Counting is where the exam value sits. |A × B| = |A| · |B|, and more generally the size of a k-fold product is the product of the sizes. Writing A² for A × A, an n-element A gives |A²| = n². A binary relation on A is by definition any subset of A × A, so the number of relations on A is |P(A × A)| = 2^(n²): for n = 3 that is 2⁹ = 512, for n = 4 it is 2¹⁶ = 65536. Similarly there are 2^(mn) relations from an m-set to an n-set.

The product distributes over the other operations: A × (B ∪ C) = (A × B) ∪ (A × C), and the same for ∩ and −.

Degenerate case: A × ∅ = ∅ × A = ∅.

Inclusion–exclusion principle

RememberAdd singles, subtract pairs, add triples: the sign alternates with how many sets you intersect.

Inclusion–exclusion counts a union by adding the sizes of the sets, subtracting all pairwise intersections, adding back all triple intersections, and continuing with alternating signs.

Key facts & formulas
  • |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B|.
  • |A ∪ B ∪ C| = |A| + |B| + |C| − |A∩B| − |A∩C| − |B∩C| + |A∩B∩C|.
  • Elements in none of the sets = |U| − |A₁ ∪ … ∪ Aₙ|.
  • Exactly two of three sets = (|A∩B| + |A∩C| + |B∩C|) − 3|A∩B∩C|.
  • Onto functions from an m-set to an n-set = Σ from k=0 to n of (−1)ᵏ C(n,k)(n−k)ᵐ — inclusion–exclusion over which images are missed.
  • Derangements: Dₙ = n! · Σ from k=0 to n of (−1)ᵏ/k!; D₄ = 9.
  • Euler's totient φ(n) = n · Π(1 − 1/p) over the distinct primes p dividing n is an inclusion–exclusion count.
Traps
  • The triple intersection is added back, not subtracted — the single most missed sign in the three-set formula.
  • 'At least one' means the union; 'none' or 'neither' means |U| minus the union. Read which is asked before computing.
  • 'Exactly one' and 'exactly two' need the weighted forms, not the raw union — subtracting the triple term once is not enough.
  • If the sets are stated to be pairwise disjoint the formula collapses to a plain sum; do not subtract intersections that are known to be empty.
  • When counting multiples in 1…N, use ⌊N/lcm⌋ for the intersections — ⌊100/2⌋·⌊100/3⌋ is meaningless here.
Full explanation

Adding |A| + |B| double-counts everything in A ∩ B, so |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B|. With three sets the pairwise subtractions remove the triple overlap three times after it was added three times, so it has to be put back once: |A ∪ B ∪ C| = |A| + |B| + |C| − |A ∩ B| − |A ∩ C| − |B ∩ C| + |A ∩ B ∩ C|.

In general |A₁ ∪ … ∪ Aₙ| = Σ(singles) − Σ(pairs) + Σ(triples) − …, the sign alternating with the number of sets intersected.

Worked: how many of 1…100 are divisible by 2, 3 or 5? ⌊100/2⌋ = 50, ⌊100/3⌋ = 33, ⌊100/5⌋ = 20; ⌊100/6⌋ = 16, ⌊100/10⌋ = 10, ⌊100/15⌋ = 6; ⌊100/30⌋ = 3. Union = 50 + 33 + 20 − 16 − 10 − 6 + 3 = 74. So 100 − 74 = 26 are divisible by none of them.

The same alternating pattern gives the count of onto functions and the derangement number, so carry the pattern rather than memorising three separate formulas.

For 'exactly k of the sets' the terms are weighted: exactly one of three = Σ|Aᵢ| − 2Σ|Aᵢ ∩ Aⱼ| + 3|A ∩ B ∩ C|.

Relations and their representation

A binary relation R from A to B is any subset of A × B, and on finite sets it is represented either as a 0/1 Boolean matrix or as a directed graph.

Key facts & formulas
  • A relation on an n-element set has an n × n Boolean matrix; there are 2^(n²) such relations.
  • The inverse relation R⁻¹ = {(b,a) : (a,b) ∈ R} has matrix M_Rᵀ and the digraph with every arrow reversed.
  • Rᵏ, the k-fold composition, has the k-th Boolean power of M_R as its matrix.
  • Boolean matrix product: entry (i,j) is OR over k of (M[i][k] AND N[k][j]); all entries stay 0 or 1.
  • In the digraph, (a, a) ∈ R appears as a self-loop at a.
  • The empty relation ∅ and the universal relation A × A are both legitimate relations on A.
  • A relation from A to B need not be a function: elements of A may have zero, one, or many partners.
Traps
  • The Boolean product uses OR and AND, so no entry ever becomes 2 — arithmetic matrix multiplication gives the wrong matrix.
  • Books differ on the composition convention: Rosen writes S ∘ R for 'R first, then S', with M_{S∘R} = M_R ⊙ M_S. Read the paper's own definition before answering.
  • A relation is not required to be total or single-valued; questions that call a two-valued mapping a 'function' are testing this.
  • Reversing arrows gives R⁻¹, not the complement of R; the complement flips 0s and 1s instead.
  • The matrix depends on the chosen ordering of elements — reorder the rows and columns consistently or the diagonal tests break.
Full explanation

A relation R from A to B is any subset of A × B; write aRb for (a, b) ∈ R. When B = A we call R a relation on A. Nothing requires every element of A to appear, and nothing requires a unique partner — that is exactly what separates a relation from a function.

Matrix representation: order A = {a₁,…,a_m} and B = {b₁,…,bₙ}. M_R is the m × n Boolean matrix whose (i, j) entry is 1 exactly when aᵢ R bⱼ. Property tests then become matrix tests: reflexive means the whole main diagonal is 1; symmetric means M_R equals its transpose; the inverse relation R⁻¹ has matrix M_Rᵀ.

Digraph representation (for a relation on A): one vertex per element, an arrow a → b for each pair in R, and a self-loop at a for each (a, a) ∈ R.

Worked: A = {1,2,3}, R = {(1,1), (1,2), (2,3)}. Row 1 of M_R is 1 1 0, row 2 is 0 0 1, row 3 is 0 0 0. The digraph has a loop at 1, an arrow 1 → 2 and an arrow 2 → 3.

Composition of relations corresponds to the Boolean matrix product, with OR playing the role of + and AND the role of ×.

Properties of relations

RememberRST = equivalence, RAT = partial order — swap Symmetric for Antisymmetric.

Reflexive, irreflexive, symmetric, antisymmetric and transitive are the five standard properties tested of a relation on a set, and each has a one-line matrix and digraph test.

Key facts & formulas
  • Reflexive relations on an n-set: 2^(n²−n); irreflexive: also 2^(n²−n).
  • Symmetric relations on an n-set: 2^(n(n+1)/2). For n = 3 that is 64.
  • Antisymmetric relations on an n-set: 2ⁿ · 3^(n(n−1)/2). For n = 3 that is 216.
  • Asymmetric = irreflexive and antisymmetric together (aRb forbids bRa outright).
  • Equivalence relation = reflexive + symmetric + transitive; partial order = reflexive + antisymmetric + transitive.
  • Transitivity holds vacuously when no chain aRb, bRc exists — a sparse relation is often transitive.
  • The number of transitive relations on an n-set has no known closed form; it is tabulated, not computed by formula.
Traps
  • 'Not reflexive' is not the same as 'irreflexive'. On {1,2}, R = {(1,1)} is neither reflexive nor irreflexive.
  • Symmetric and antisymmetric are not opposites. Any subset of the diagonal — for example the equality relation — is both; {(1,2),(2,1),(1,3)} is neither.
  • The empty relation on a non-empty set is symmetric, antisymmetric and transitive, but not reflexive. This exact option appears again and again.
  • Antisymmetry does not forbid loops; it forbids two-way arrows between distinct elements only.
  • Do not declare a relation non-transitive just because it is small — check whether a two-step chain actually exists first.
Full explanation

Let R be a relation on A. Reflexive: (a,a) ∈ R for every a. Irreflexive: (a,a) ∉ R for every a. Symmetric: aRb implies bRa. Antisymmetric: aRb and bRa together force a = b. Transitive: aRb and bRc imply aRc.

Matrix tests: reflexive means every diagonal entry is 1; irreflexive means every diagonal entry is 0; symmetric means M = Mᵀ; antisymmetric means for i ≠ j at most one of m_ij, m_ji is 1; transitive means every 1 appearing in M ⊙ M already appears in M.

Worked: on {1,2,3} take R = {(1,1), (1,2), (2,1)}. Symmetric — the 1–2 arrow runs both ways. Not reflexive, since (2,2) and (3,3) are missing. Not antisymmetric, since 1R2 and 2R1 with 1 ≠ 2. Not transitive: 2R1 and 1R2 demand (2,2), which is absent.

Counting, which these papers ask directly for a 3- or 4-element set: relations 2^(n²); reflexive 2^(n²−n); irreflexive 2^(n²−n); symmetric 2^(n(n+1)/2); antisymmetric 2ⁿ · 3^(n(n−1)/2). For n = 3 that gives 512 relations, 64 reflexive, 64 symmetric and 8 · 27 = 216 antisymmetric. There is no simple closed formula for the number of transitive relations.

Closures of relations, Warshall's algorithm

RememberWarshall: k outside, i and j inside.

The closure of a relation R with respect to a property is the smallest relation containing R that has that property, obtained by adding pairs and never removing any.

Key facts & formulas
  • r(R) = R ∪ Δ; s(R) = R ∪ R⁻¹.
  • t(R) = R ∪ R² ∪ … ∪ Rⁿ for an n-element set; higher powers add nothing new.
  • Warshall's update is M[i][j] = M[i][j] OR (M[i][k] AND M[k][j]), with k as the outer loop.
  • Warshall runs in Θ(n³) time and Θ(n²) space.
  • R* = t(R) ∪ Δ is the reflexive–transitive closure: paths of length ≥ 0.
  • The equivalence closure of R is t(s(r(R))) — reflexive, then symmetric, then transitive.
  • No antisymmetric closure exists in general, since closures may only add pairs.
Traps
  • Order of closures matters: t(s(R)) and s(t(R)) can differ, so the equivalence closure must be taken in the stated order.
  • The transitive closure needs powers up to Rⁿ; stopping at R ∪ R² misses chains of length 3 or more.
  • Swapping Warshall's loops so that k is not outermost silently produces an incomplete closure that still looks plausible.
  • A closure is the smallest superset with the property — an answer option that deletes a pair is automatically wrong.
  • Warshall's algorithm computes Boolean reachability; Floyd–Warshall computes shortest-path weights. Papers use the two names loosely, so read the matrix entries.
Full explanation

Reflexive closure: r(R) = R ∪ Δ, where Δ = {(a,a) : a ∈ A} is the diagonal.

Symmetric closure: s(R) = R ∪ R⁻¹.

Transitive closure: t(R) = R ∪ R² ∪ R³ ∪ … ∪ Rⁿ for |A| = n. Here Rᵏ is the set of pairs joined by a path of exactly k edges in the digraph. On n vertices any path can be shortened to length at most n, so the union stops at n. Equivalently, (a, b) ∈ t(R) precisely when the digraph has a directed path from a to b of length at least 1.

Warshall computes t(R) directly on the Boolean matrix:

for k = 1 to n: for i = 1 to n: for j = 1 to n: M[i][j] = M[i][j] OR (M[i][k] AND M[k][j])

k must be the outermost loop: after iteration k, M records reachability using only intermediate vertices drawn from {1,…,k}, which is the loop invariant that makes the algorithm correct. Cost is Θ(n³) time and Θ(n²) space — cheaper than forming R², R³, … separately.

The equivalence closure of R is t(s(r(R))). There is no antisymmetric closure in general, because antisymmetry can only be achieved by deleting pairs.

Equivalence relations and partitions

RememberRST → classes → partition. Bell counts: 1, 2, 5, 15, 52.

An equivalence relation is reflexive, symmetric and transitive, and it splits its set into disjoint non-empty equivalence classes that together form a partition.

Key facts & formulas
  • Equivalence relation = reflexive + symmetric + transitive.
  • [a] = {x ∈ A : xRa}, and a ∈ [a] by reflexivity, so classes are never empty.
  • Two equivalence classes are either identical or disjoint; their union is the whole set.
  • Equivalence relations on A correspond one-to-one with partitions of A.
  • Number of equivalence relations on an n-set = Bell number Bₙ: B₁ = 1, B₂ = 2, B₃ = 5, B₄ = 15, B₅ = 52.
  • Bₙ = Σ from k=1 to n of S(n,k), where S(n,k) is the Stirling number of the second kind, the number of partitions into exactly k blocks.
  • Congruence modulo n on ℤ is an equivalence relation with exactly n classes.
  • The intersection of two equivalence relations on A is again an equivalence relation.
Traps
  • The number of equivalence relations on {1,2,3,4} is 15 (B₄), not 2¹⁶ (all relations) and not 4! (bijections).
  • The union of two equivalence relations need not be transitive, so it is usually not an equivalence relation.
  • A partition's blocks must be non-empty and must cover the whole set; ∅ is never a block.
  • Classes cannot partially overlap — one shared element already forces the two classes to be equal.
  • A relation that is both an equivalence relation and antisymmetric must be the equality relation; papers offer 'both' as a plausible-looking option.
Full explanation

R on A is an equivalence relation when it is reflexive, symmetric and transitive (RST). Each a ∈ A then has a class [a] = {x ∈ A : xRa}. Reflexivity puts a into its own class, so no class is empty.

The key theorem: for any a, b the classes [a] and [b] are either identical or disjoint. Suppose c ∈ [a] ∩ [b]. Then cRa and cRb, so by symmetry and transitivity aRb, and every element related to a is then related to b. Hence the classes cover A without overlapping — they form a partition. Conversely, every partition of A defines an equivalence relation ('lies in the same block'), so equivalence relations on A and partitions of A are in one-to-one correspondence. The set of classes is written A/R, the quotient set.

Worked: congruence modulo 3 on ℤ. It is RST, and gives exactly three classes [0], [1], [2]; every integer lands in exactly one.

Counting follows from the correspondence: the number of equivalence relations on an n-element set equals the number of partitions of that set, the Bell number Bₙ. B₁ = 1, B₂ = 2, B₃ = 5, B₄ = 15, B₅ = 52. So 'how many equivalence relations on {1,2,3,4}?' answers 15.

Functions: domain, codomain, range

A function f: A → B assigns to every element of A exactly one element of B; A is the domain, B the codomain, and the range f(A) is the subset of B actually hit.

Key facts & formulas
  • Number of functions from an m-element set to an n-element set = nᵐ.
  • Range ⊆ codomain always; equality with the codomain is exactly the definition of onto.
  • Preimage f⁻¹(T) = {x : f(x) ∈ T} is defined for any function, invertible or not.
  • f⁻¹(T₁ ∪ T₂) = f⁻¹(T₁) ∪ f⁻¹(T₂), and the same for intersection — preimages respect all set operations.
  • f(S₁ ∩ S₂) ⊆ f(S₁) ∩ f(S₂), with equality guaranteed only when f is injective.
  • For a finite domain, |f(A)| ≤ |A|.
  • A function must be total and single-valued; failing either condition makes it a relation only.
Traps
  • Codomain is declared, range is computed. f: ℝ → ℝ, f(x) = x² is not onto; f: ℝ → [0, ∞), f(x) = x² is. Same rule, different answer.
  • f⁻¹ as a preimage of a set always exists; f⁻¹ as an inverse function requires bijectivity. The notation is identical, the objects are not.
  • The number of functions is nᵐ (codomain size raised to domain size), not mⁿ — the most common slip in this topic.
  • A diagram in which one domain element has two arrows, or none, is not a function; MCQs draw exactly these two failures.
  • f(S₁ ∩ S₂) = f(S₁) ∩ f(S₂) is false in general — only the ⊆ direction is safe.
Full explanation

A function f: A → B is a relation from A to B that is total (every a ∈ A has an image) and single-valued (exactly one image each). A is the domain, B the codomain, and the range or image f(A) = {f(a) : a ∈ A} is the set of values actually attained. Always f(A) ⊆ B, and the inclusion may be strict.

Worked: f: ℝ → ℝ with f(x) = x². Domain ℝ, codomain ℝ, range [0, ∞). No negative value is hit, so f is not onto — yet the rule has not changed; only the declared codomain decides. Declare f: ℝ → [0, ∞) and the same rule becomes onto. Codomain is declared; range is computed.

Image and preimage of sets: f(S) = {f(x) : x ∈ S} for S ⊆ A, and f⁻¹(T) = {x ∈ A : f(x) ∈ T} for T ⊆ B. The notation f⁻¹ used this way is defined for every function; it does not claim that an inverse function exists.

Counting: each of the m domain elements is sent independently to one of n codomain elements, so there are nᵐ functions from an m-set to an n-set. For m = 3, n = 2 that is 8.

Injective, surjective, bijective — and counting

Remembersur-jective from French sur = 'onto': it covers the codomain. In-jective keeps inputs in distinct slots.

A function is injective when distinct inputs give distinct outputs, surjective when its range is the whole codomain, and bijective when it is both.

Key facts & formulas
  • Injections from an m-set to an n-set: n!/(n−m)! when m ≤ n, and 0 when m > n. For m = 4, n = 5 the answer is 120.
  • Surjections from an m-set onto an n-set: Σ from k=0 to n of (−1)ᵏ C(n,k)(n−k)ᵐ = n! · S(m, n). For m = 4, n = 3 the answer is 36.
  • Bijections between two n-element sets: n!.
  • Total functions from an m-set to an n-set: nᵐ.
  • An injection requires |A| ≤ |B|; a surjection requires |A| ≥ |B|; a bijection requires |A| = |B| (finite sets).
  • For finite sets with |A| = |B|, injective, surjective and bijective are equivalent.
  • Pigeonhole principle: if |A| > |B| then no function A → B can be injective.
Traps
  • Injections from a 5-set to a 4-set number 0 — do not mechanically write 4!/(4−5)!, which is undefined.
  • The count of onto functions is not nᵐ minus something simple; it needs inclusion–exclusion. Guessing here loses marks under 1/3 negative marking.
  • nᵐ versus mⁿ: it is codomain size raised to the domain size. Check which set the exponent belongs to before answering.
  • The finite equivalence injective ⇔ surjective fails for infinite sets: f(n) = 2n on ℕ is injective and not onto.
  • 'Into' in older Indian phrasing does not mean 'not onto'; it only declines to assert onto. 'One-one into' is simply injective.
Full explanation

f: A → B is injective (one-one) when f(x) = f(y) forces x = y; surjective (onto) when range = codomain; bijective when both.

Counting for finite |A| = m, |B| = n — the shape these papers actually use:

Total functions: nᵐ.

Injections: choose distinct images in order, giving n(n−1)…(n−m+1) = n!/(n−m)! = P(n, m). It is 0 when m > n, by the pigeonhole principle. Worked: m = 4, n = 5 gives 5 · 4 · 3 · 2 = 120 injections.

Surjections: inclusion–exclusion over which codomain elements are missed gives Σ from k=0 to n of (−1)ᵏ C(n,k)(n−k)ᵐ, which equals n! · S(m, n). Worked: m = 4, n = 3 gives 3⁴ − 3 · 2⁴ + 3 · 1⁴ = 81 − 48 + 3 = 36.

Bijections: possible only when m = n, and then there are n! of them.

For finite sets of equal size the three notions collapse: injective ⇔ surjective ⇔ bijective. That is a counting argument — an injection from an n-set into an n-set cannot miss anything — and it fails for infinite sets: f: ℕ → ℕ with f(n) = 2n is injective but not onto.

Older Indian phrasing: 'one-one into' means injective, 'one-one onto' means bijective.

Composition and inverse functions

RememberSocks and shoes: to undo, reverse the order — (g ∘ f)⁻¹ = f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹.

The composite (g ∘ f)(x) = g(f(x)) applies f first and then g, and f has an inverse function exactly when f is a bijection.

Key facts & formulas
  • (g ∘ f)(x) = g(f(x)): the function written on the right is applied first.
  • Composition is associative but not commutative.
  • f and g injective ⇒ g ∘ f injective; f and g surjective ⇒ g ∘ f surjective; both bijective ⇒ bijective.
  • If g ∘ f is injective then f is injective; if g ∘ f is surjective then g is surjective.
  • f⁻¹ exists as a function iff f is a bijection, and then f⁻¹ ∘ f = I_A and f ∘ f⁻¹ = I_B.
  • (g ∘ f)⁻¹ = f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹ and (f⁻¹)⁻¹ = f.
  • Composition of relations generalises this and is computed by the Boolean matrix product.
Traps
  • g ∘ f means f acts first. Reading the symbols left to right and applying g first is the classic error.
  • (g ∘ f)⁻¹ = f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹, not g⁻¹ ∘ f⁻¹ — the order reverses.
  • g ∘ f injective does not make g injective, and g ∘ f surjective does not make f surjective. Only the stated one-sided implications hold.
  • f ∘ g and g ∘ f may have different domains and may not both be defined; equality of the two is a special property, not a rule.
  • The identity function I is the identity for composition, but f ∘ f = I means f is its own inverse (an involution) — it does not mean f is the identity.
Full explanation

Given f: A → B and g: B → C, the composite g ∘ f: A → C is defined by (g ∘ f)(x) = g(f(x)). The right-hand function acts first — the notation reads backwards relative to the order of application.

Worked: f(x) = x + 1 and g(x) = x² on ℝ. Then (g ∘ f)(2) = g(3) = 9, while (f ∘ g)(2) = f(4) = 5. Composition is not commutative. It is associative: h ∘ (g ∘ f) = (h ∘ g) ∘ f, so brackets may be dropped.

Inheritance of properties: if f and g are both injective, so is g ∘ f; both surjective gives surjective; both bijective gives bijective. The one-sided results are what MCQs test — if g ∘ f is injective then f must be injective (nothing follows about g), and if g ∘ f is surjective then g must be surjective (nothing follows about f).

f has an inverse function f⁻¹: B → A precisely when f is a bijection. Then f⁻¹ ∘ f = I_A, f ∘ f⁻¹ = I_B, and (f⁻¹)⁻¹ = f. For a composite the order reverses: (g ∘ f)⁻¹ = f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹.

  5. 5
    Let R be a symmetric and transitive relation on a set A, then
    1. AR is reflexive and hence an equivalence relation
    2. BR is reflexive and hence a partial order

    4. DNone of these
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    The tempting 'proof' that symmetric+transitive implies reflexive (aRb with symmetric gives bRa, then transitive gives aRa) only works for elements that already appear in some related pair; an element unrelated to anything is never forced to be reflexive. So R need not be reflexive, and it need not fail to be reflexive either — the general answer is 'none of these'.

    Consider a relation R = {(1, 1), (2, 2), (2, 3), (3, 2), (3, 4), (4, 3)}, on a set A = {1, 2, 3, 4}. The relation R is:

    Show answer

    Answer: C

    R is missing (3,3) and (4,4), so it is not reflexive. Every pair has its reverse present — (2,3)/(3,2) and (3,4)/(4,3) — so R is symmetric. But (3,2) and (2,3) are both in R while (3,3) is not, so R fails transitivity. Hence R is symmetric only.

Posets, Lattices and Induction

Small cluster, but it reappears at every sitting — usually as one Hasse-diagram / LUB-GLB question and one induction question.

6 concept cards19 past questions

Posets, comparability, Hasse diagrams

RememberHasse diagram = digraph minus loops minus shortcuts, drawn upwards.

A partially ordered set is a set with a relation that is reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive, and its Hasse diagram draws it with loops and transitively implied edges removed.

Key facts & formulas
  • Poset = reflexive + antisymmetric + transitive, written (A, ≼).
  • Standard posets: (P(S), ⊆), (ℤ⁺, |) under divisibility, (ℝ, ≤).
  • A chain is a totally ordered subset; an antichain has no two distinct comparable elements.
  • b covers a when a ≺ b and no c satisfies a ≺ c ≺ b; the Hasse diagram draws exactly the covering pairs.
  • The Hasse diagram omits all loops and all transitively implied edges, and uses vertical position instead of arrowheads.
  • Topological sort produces a linear order consistent with the partial order.
  • The number of partial orders on a labelled 2-set is 3 and on a labelled 3-set is 19 — not a simple power.
Traps
  • Divisibility is a partial order on ℤ⁺ but not on ℤ: 3 | −3 and −3 | 3 with 3 ≠ −3, so antisymmetry fails.
  • Reading a Hasse diagram as the full relation and then calling the relation non-reflexive or non-transitive is the standard trap — those edges are deliberately suppressed.
  • 'Partial' does not mean some pairs were forgotten; it means comparability is simply not required.
  • A poset need not have a top or a bottom; a Hasse diagram may have several highest vertices.
  • Strict order ≺ is irreflexive while the poset relation ≼ is reflexive; papers switch symbols mid-question.
Full explanation

A poset (A, ≼) is a set together with a relation that is reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive — the same list as an equivalence relation, with symmetry replaced by antisymmetry. Standard examples: (P(S), ⊆); (ℤ⁺, |) under divisibility; (ℝ, ≤).

Two elements a and b are comparable if a ≼ b or b ≼ a, and incomparable otherwise. 'Partial' means incomparable pairs are permitted. If every pair is comparable the poset is a chain, i.e. a total or linear order; a subset in which no two distinct elements are comparable is an antichain. In (ℤ⁺, |), 2 and 3 are incomparable while 2 and 6 are comparable.

The Hasse diagram compresses the digraph. Delete every self-loop, because reflexivity is assumed. Delete every edge implied by transitivity. Draw each remaining covering relation with the larger element higher on the page: b covers a when a ≺ b and nothing lies strictly between them. Since height encodes direction, no arrowheads are needed.

Worked: the divisors of 12 under divisibility. 1 at the bottom; 2 and 3 above it; 4 and 6 above those; 12 at the top. The edge 1–12 is not drawn — transitivity recovers it.

Extending a partial order to a compatible total order is topological sorting.

Maximal, greatest, bounds, LUB and GLB

RememberMaximal = nothing above it. Greatest = above everything.

In a poset, maximal and minimal elements have nothing strictly above or below them, greatest and least elements compare with everything, and LUB and GLB are the least upper and greatest lower bounds of a subset.

Key facts & formulas
  • Greatest and least elements are unique when they exist; maximal and minimal elements need not be.
  • Every finite non-empty poset has at least one maximal and at least one minimal element.
  • In a finite poset, a unique maximal element is the greatest element.
  • A greatest element is always maximal, but a maximal element need not be greatest.
  • LUB(B) is the least upper bound of B, unique when it exists; it need not belong to B.
  • Upper and lower bounds must lie in the poset A, but need not lie in the subset B.
  • A lattice is precisely a poset in which every pair of elements has both a LUB and a GLB.
  • In (ℤ⁺, |) the element 1 is least and there is no greatest; within the divisors of a fixed n, n is greatest.
Traps
  • Maximal ≠ greatest. In the poset {2, 3} under divisibility both elements are maximal and minimal, and there is no greatest or least element.
  • A subset may have many upper bounds but no least one — then the LUB does not exist and the poset is not a lattice.
  • 'Least element of the poset', 'minimal element' and 'lower bound of a subset' are three different questions; read which is asked.
  • The LUB need not be a member of the subset: sup{2, 3} = 6 among the divisors of 12.
  • Uniqueness of a maximal element implies greatest only for finite posets; the argument uses finiteness.
Full explanation

Work in a poset (A, ≼) with a subset B ⊆ A.

Maximal: an a ∈ A with no x ∈ A satisfying a ≺ x. Minimal: nothing strictly below.

Greatest: an element g with x ≼ g for every x ∈ A. Least: dually. Greatest and least are unique when they exist; maximal and minimal elements may be plural.

Upper bound of B: any u ∈ A with b ≼ u for all b ∈ B. LUB (supremum, join): the least such u, unique when it exists. GLB (infimum, meet): the greatest lower bound.

Worked: A = {2, 3, 4, 6, 12} ordered by divisibility. Minimal elements are 2 and 3, since nothing in A properly divides either. The only maximal element is 12, and because every element divides 12, it is also the greatest. There is no least element: 2 and 3 are incomparable. For B = {2, 3}, the upper bounds are 6 and 12, so LUB(B) = 6; B has no lower bound in A at all, so GLB(B) does not exist. Note 6 ∉ B — bounds live in A, not necessarily in B.

A finite non-empty poset always has at least one maximal and one minimal element, and if its maximal element is unique, that element is the greatest.

Lattices — definition, meet and join

Remember∨ opens upward = join = LUB = ∪. ∧ points down = meet = GLB = ∩.

A lattice is a poset in which every pair of elements has a least upper bound (join, ∨) and a greatest lower bound (meet, ∧).

Key facts & formulas
  • a ∨ b = LUB{a, b} (join); a ∧ b = GLB{a, b} (meet).
  • (P(S), ⊆) is a lattice with join = ∪ and meet = ∩.
  • (D_n, |) is a lattice with join = lcm and meet = gcd.
  • Every chain (total order) is a lattice, with join = max and meet = min.
  • Lattice axioms: idempotent, commutative, associative, plus absorption a ∨ (a ∧ b) = a and a ∧ (a ∨ b) = a.
  • The order is recoverable from either operation: a ≼ b ⇔ a ∧ b = a ⇔ a ∨ b = b.
  • Every finite lattice is bounded and complete.
  • A sublattice must be closed under the parent lattice's ∧ and ∨.
Traps
  • Being a poset is not enough. If a and b both sit below two incomparable elements c and d, then {a, b} has no LUB and the structure is not a lattice.
  • A subset that happens to be a lattice under the induced order is not automatically a sublattice; closure under the parent's meet and join is what is required.
  • Join ∨ is LUB = lcm = union; meet ∧ is GLB = gcd = intersection. Swapping the two is the most common MCQ distractor.
  • Distributivity is not part of the lattice axioms — do not assume a ∧ (b ∨ c) = (a ∧ b) ∨ (a ∧ c) for an arbitrary lattice.
  • In (D_n, |) the meet is gcd even though gcd is 'smaller': smaller under divisibility means lower in the Hasse diagram, which is what GLB requires.
Full explanation

A lattice is a poset in which every pair {a, b} has both a LUB and a GLB. Write a ∨ b (join) for the LUB and a ∧ b (meet) for the GLB. Because pairs suffice, induction extends the guarantee to every finite non-empty subset.

Standard models, worth memorising because the papers reuse them: (P(S), ⊆): a ∨ b = a ∪ b and a ∧ b = a ∩ b. (D_n, |), the divisors of n under divisibility: a ∨ b = lcm(a, b) and a ∧ b = gcd(a, b). Any chain: a ∨ b = max(a, b) and a ∧ b = min(a, b), so every totally ordered set is a lattice.

Algebraically, ∧ and ∨ are idempotent, commutative and associative, and are tied together by absorption: a ∨ (a ∧ b) = a and a ∧ (a ∨ b) = a. Those laws define a lattice without mentioning order, because the order is recoverable: a ≼ b ⇔ a ∧ b = a ⇔ a ∨ b = b.

Not every poset is a lattice. Take a and b both below c and d, with c and d incomparable and nothing above them. The upper bounds of {a, b} are c and d, neither of which is least — so a ∨ b does not exist and the poset fails.

Types of lattices and Boolean algebra

RememberBoolean = Distributive AND Complemented — both halves, never either alone.

A lattice is bounded if it has 0 and 1, complemented if every element has a complement, distributive if meet and join distribute over each other, complete if every subset has a LUB and GLB — and one that is both complemented and distributive is a Boolean algebra.

Key facts & formulas
  • Bounded lattice: has least element 0 and greatest element 1. Every finite lattice is bounded.
  • a′ is a complement of a when a ∧ a′ = 0 and a ∨ a′ = 1; a complemented lattice is bounded with every element complemented.
  • In any lattice, one distributive law implies the other; in a distributive lattice, complements are unique when they exist.
  • Boolean algebra = complemented + distributive lattice (papers may say 'self-complemented distributive lattice').
  • Complete lattice: every subset has a LUB and a GLB. Every finite lattice is complete; (ℚ, ≤) is not.
  • Modular: a ≼ c implies a ∨ (b ∧ c) = (a ∨ b) ∧ c. Every distributive lattice is modular; M₃ is modular but not distributive.
  • Birkhoff: a lattice is distributive iff no sublattice is isomorphic to M₃ (diamond) or N₅ (pentagon); it is modular iff no sublattice is N₅.
  • Every finite Boolean algebra is isomorphic to (P(S), ⊆), so it has 2ⁿ elements for some n.
  • D_n under divisibility is a Boolean algebra iff n is squarefree: D₃₀ has 8 elements and is Boolean; D₁₂ is distributive but not complemented.
Traps
  • Complemented does not imply distributive (M₃) and distributive does not imply complemented (D₁₂). Boolean requires both, and the MCQ options are built on exactly this.
  • Complements are unique only in a distributive lattice. In M₃ each of the three middle elements has two complements.
  • 'Complete' and 'bounded' are not synonyms: every complete lattice is bounded, but an infinite bounded lattice need not be complete.
  • Every lattice with 4 or fewer elements is distributive; the smallest non-distributive lattices, M₃ and N₅, each have 5 elements. Small counterexamples do not exist.
  • A chain is always distributive and, if finite, bounded — but it is complemented only when it has at most 2 elements. A 3-element chain 0 ≺ a ≺ 1 gives a no complement.
Full explanation

Bounded: the lattice has a least element 0 and a greatest element 1. Every finite lattice is bounded — take the meet and the join of everything.

Complemented: bounded, and every a has some a′ with a ∧ a′ = 0 and a ∨ a′ = 1. Complements need not be unique in general.

Distributive: a ∧ (b ∨ c) = (a ∧ b) ∨ (a ∧ c). In any lattice each distributive law implies the other, so only one need be checked. In a distributive lattice a complement, when it exists, is unique.

Complete: every subset, finite or infinite, has a LUB and a GLB. Every finite lattice is complete. (ℚ, ≤) is a lattice that is not complete — {x ∈ ℚ : x² < 2} has no rational supremum.

Boolean algebra: a lattice that is both complemented and distributive. Older Indian papers word this as 'a self-complemented distributive lattice is a Boolean algebra'. Neither half alone suffices: M₃, the three-atom diamond, is complemented but not distributive, while the divisor lattice D₁₂ is distributive but not complemented.

Every finite Boolean algebra is isomorphic to (P(S), ⊆), so its size is a power of 2. D_n is Boolean exactly when n is squarefree: D₃₀ ≅ P({2,3,5}), with 8 elements.

Mathematical induction (weak form)

RememberBase, hypothesis, step. No base, no proof.

Mathematical induction proves a statement P(n) for all integers n ≥ n₀ by verifying P(n₀) and then showing that P(k) implies P(k+1).

Key facts & formulas
  • Two obligations only: the base case P(n₀), and the implication P(k) ⇒ P(k+1) for arbitrary k ≥ n₀.
  • The induction hypothesis P(k) is assumed, not proved; the work of the step is deriving P(k+1) from it.
  • The base need not be n = 1. For 2ⁿ > n² the usual base is n = 5, because the statement is false at n = 2, 3 and 4.
  • Standard summations provable by induction: Σi = n(n+1)/2, Σi² = n(n+1)(2n+1)/6, Σi³ = [n(n+1)/2]², Σ(2i−1) = n².
  • Induction also proves divisibility results, such as 6 | n³ − n for all n ≥ 1.
  • Bernoulli's inequality (1+x)ⁿ ≥ 1 + nx for x ≥ −1 and n ≥ 1 is a standard induction exercise.
  • The principle of induction is equivalent to the well-ordering of the non-negative integers.
Traps
  • Omitting the base case can 'prove' a falsehood: the step for 1 + 2 + … + n = (n² + n + 2)/2 goes through cleanly, but n = 1 gives 2 ≠ 1, so the formula is wrong.
  • Checking n = 1, 2, 3 is verification, not induction; the step must be proved for an arbitrary k.
  • Assuming P(n) and 'deriving' P(n) is circular — the step must start at k and finish at k+1.
  • Many inequality questions are false for small n; find where the claim actually starts before writing the base case.
  • Do not substitute the closed form for the (k+1)-th term inside the step — that assumes what is being proved.
Full explanation

To prove P(n) for all integers n ≥ n₀ there are exactly two obligations. 1. Base: prove P(n₀) outright. 2. Step: for an arbitrary k ≥ n₀, assume P(k) — the induction hypothesis — and derive P(k+1). Together these give P(n) for every n ≥ n₀: the step is the rung, the base is where the ladder is planted.

Worked — prove 1 + 2 + … + n = n(n+1)/2 for all n ≥ 1. Base n = 1: LHS = 1, RHS = 1 · 2/2 = 1. Holds. Hypothesis: assume 1 + 2 + … + k = k(k+1)/2. Step: 1 + … + k + (k+1) = k(k+1)/2 + (k+1) = (k+1)(k+2)/2, which is exactly the formula at n = k+1. Hence the identity holds for all n ≥ 1.

The same three-line shape handles divisibility claims (6 divides n³ − n for all n ≥ 1) and inequalities (Bernoulli: (1+x)ⁿ ≥ 1 + nx for x ≥ −1).

Choose the base carefully. For 2ⁿ > n² the standard base is n = 5, since the claim is false at n = 2, 3 and 4. A step valid from k = 5 onwards says nothing about n = 3, and 'for which n does this hold' is testing precisely that.

Strong induction and well-ordering

RememberWeak induction climbs one rung; strong induction stands on the whole ladder below.

Strong induction proves P(k+1) by assuming P(n₀) through P(k) all hold, and the well-ordering principle states that every non-empty set of non-negative integers has a least element.

Key facts & formulas
  • Strong induction hypothesis: P(n₀) ∧ P(n₀+1) ∧ … ∧ P(k), used to prove P(k+1).
  • Weak induction, strong induction and the well-ordering principle are logically equivalent over the integers.
  • Strong induction suits recursions that reach back more than one step: prime factorisation, Fibonacci-style bounds, binary representation, divide-and-conquer correctness.
  • Every integer n ≥ 2 has a prime factorisation — the standard strong-induction proof.
  • Well-ordering principle: every non-empty subset of ℕ has a least element.
  • Proof by minimal counterexample: assume the counterexample set is non-empty, take its least member, contradict.
  • The division algorithm — existence of q and r with a = bq + r, 0 ≤ r < b — is proved from well-ordering.
  • ℤ, ℚ⁺ and ℝ⁺ are not well-ordered under ≤; ℚ⁺ and ℝ⁺ have no least positive element.
Traps
  • Strong induction is not stronger in what it can prove — it proves exactly the same theorems as weak induction, just more conveniently.
  • A recurrence whose step reaches back two terms needs two base cases; one base case leaves a gap, and this is the standard broken Fibonacci proof.
  • Strong induction still requires its base case(s) to be checked separately — at the bottom the 'all previous cases' hypothesis is vacuous.
  • Well-ordering asserts a least element, not a greatest; a non-empty subset of ℕ need not have a largest element.
  • ℤ is not well-ordered under ≤ — the negative integers have no least element. Papers offer 'every non-empty set of integers has a least element' as a distractor.
Full explanation

Strong induction (also called complete induction or the second principle) changes only the hypothesis: to prove P(k+1) you may assume P(n₀), P(n₀+1), …, P(k) all hold, rather than P(k) alone. Reach for it whenever the step reaches back further than one step — recursive definitions, prime factorisation, binary representation, correctness of divide-and-conquer algorithms.

Worked — every integer n ≥ 2 is a product of primes. Base: 2 is itself prime. Step: take n > 2 and assume the claim for every integer from 2 up to n−1. If n is prime we are done. Otherwise n = ab with 2 ≤ a, b < n; the hypothesis applies to both a and b, and concatenating their factorisations factorises n. Ordinary induction cannot run this argument, because a and b are not n−1.

The well-ordering principle states that every non-empty subset of the non-negative integers has a least element. It is logically equivalent to both forms of induction. It powers proof by minimal counterexample: assume the set of n for which P(n) fails is non-empty, let m be its least member, and derive a contradiction from the fact that P holds below m.

Well-ordering is specific to ℕ: ℤ, ℚ⁺ and ℝ⁺ all fail it under ≤.

Past questions from this topic (19)
  1. 1
    A relation R on a set S is called a partial order (making (S, R) a poset) if R is:
    1. AReflexive, symmetric and transitive
    2. BReflexive, antisymmetric and transitive
    3. CIrreflexive, symmetric and transitive
    4. DSymmetric and transitive only
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A partial order must satisfy three properties: reflexivity (every element relates to itself), antisymmetry (if a relates to b and b relates to a, then a = b), and transitivity (if a relates to b and b relates to c, then a relates to c). Reflexive + symmetric + transitive instead defines an equivalence relation, which partitions a set into disjoint classes rather than ordering it, so option A describes a different structure entirely.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_1

  2. 2
    Using mathematical induction, the sum of the first n odd natural numbers, 1 + 3 + 5 + ... + (2n - 1), equals:
    1. An(n + 1) / 2
    2. Bn²
    3. C2n - 1
    4. Dn(n - 1)
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Base case n=1: the sum is just 1, and 1² = 1, so it holds. Inductive step: assume the sum of the first k odd numbers is k² (the inductive hypothesis). The sum of the first k+1 odd numbers is then k² + (2k+1), since (2k+1) is the (k+1)-th odd number, and k² + 2k + 1 = (k+1)² exactly. This confirms the formula for k+1, completing the induction. n(n+1)/2 is instead the sum of the first n natural numbers, not odd numbers.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_10

  3. 3
    In the poset (S, |) where S = {2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12} and | denotes divisibility, which statement is correct?
    1. A12 is the maximum element of the poset
    2. B8 and 12 are both maximal elements, and the poset has no maximum element
    3. C2 is a maximal element
    4. DThe poset is a total order (chain)
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A maximal element has nothing above it in the order; a maximum element must be comparable to and above every other element. Here 8 does not divide 12 and 12 does not divide 8, so they are incomparable — both are maximal, but neither dominates the other, so no maximum exists. This distinction between 'maximal' and 'maximum' is a classic poset exam point. Since 8 and 12 are incomparable, the poset is also not a chain.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_2

  4. 4
    A Hasse diagram represents a finite poset by:
    1. AShowing every ordered pair in the relation, including reflexive loops and transitive edges
    2. BOmitting reflexive loops and any edge implied by transitivity, drawing only the covering relations with lower elements placed below higher ones
    3. CShowing only symmetric relations between elements
    4. DListing every element in one totally ordered sequence
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A Hasse diagram simplifies the full relation graph of a poset for readability: reflexive loops (a relates to itself) are dropped since they're implied, and edges that follow from transitivity (if a<b<c, the a-c edge is omitted since it's implied by a-b and b-c) are removed, leaving only direct 'covering' relations. Elements are drawn with higher elements physically above lower ones, so direction doesn't need arrows.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_3

  5. 5
    A poset (L, ≤) is called a lattice if:
    1. AEvery pair of elements has a unique least upper bound (join) only
    2. BEvery pair of elements has both a least upper bound (join, ∨) and a greatest lower bound (meet, ∧)
    3. CEvery element in L has a complement
    4. DL is totally ordered (a chain)
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A lattice requires both binary operations to exist and be well-defined for every pair of elements: the join (supremum, smallest element that is ≥ both) and the meet (infimum, largest element that is ≤ both). Having only a join makes it a join-semilattice, not a full lattice. Complementation (option C) is an extra property of a special class called complemented lattices, not a requirement for every lattice, and a chain (option D) is only one special case of a lattice.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_4

  6. 6
    A lattice (L, ∨, ∧) is called distributive if, for all a, b, c ∈ L, it satisfies:
    1. Aa ∨ (b ∧ c) = (a ∨ b) ∧ (a ∨ c)
    2. Ba ∨ b = b ∨ a (commutativity) only
    3. Ca ∧ a = a (idempotency) only
    4. Da ∨ (a ∧ b) = a (absorption) only
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Distributivity is an extra, not-always-true property: a ∨ (b ∧ c) = (a ∨ b) ∧ (a ∨ c), and equivalently a ∧ (b ∨ c) = (a ∧ b) ∨ (a ∧ c). Options B, C and D (commutativity, idempotency, absorption) are identities that hold in every lattice by definition, so they can't be used to distinguish a distributive lattice from a non-distributive one. The classic non-distributive examples are the pentagon lattice N5 and the diamond lattice M3.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_5

  7. 7
    A bounded lattice L (with least element 0 and greatest element 1) is called complemented if:
    1. AEvery element a has at least one complement a' such that a ∨ a' = 1 and a ∧ a' = 0
    2. BL has exactly two elements
    3. CL is a chain
    4. DEvery element is its own complement
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    In a complemented lattice, every element must pair with some complement that 'fills the gap' to the top (their join is the greatest element 1) and shares nothing with it at the bottom (their meet is the least element 0). A two-element chain {0,1} happens to be complemented, but that's a specific tiny example, not the general definition. A longer chain is generally not complemented since interior elements have no complement.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_6

  8. 8
    In a finite bounded lattice, the meet (greatest lower bound) of the entire set of elements is called the:
    1. ASupremum or top element (denoted 1)
    2. BInfimum or bottom element (denoted 0)
    3. CMaximal element
    4. DJoin-irreducible element
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Taking the meet (greatest lower bound) of every element in a bounded lattice always yields the unique smallest element of the lattice, conventionally denoted 0 and called the bottom or infimum. Symmetrically, taking the join of every element yields the top element 1 (the supremum). 'Maximal' is a local, possibly non-unique notion from general posets, while 'join-irreducible' refers to an element that cannot be expressed as the join of two strictly smaller elements.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_7

  9. 9
    A poset in which every two elements are comparable (i.e., for any a, b, either a ≤ b or b ≤ a) is called a:
    1. ALattice
    2. BChain (a totally ordered set, or linear order)
    3. CAntichain
    4. DSemilattice
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A chain, or totally ordered set, is a poset with no incomparable pairs — you can always line up its elements in a single sequence. The opposite extreme, an antichain, is a poset where no two distinct elements are comparable at all. Every chain happens to be a lattice (the join/meet of two comparable elements is just the larger/smaller one), but not every lattice is a chain, so 'lattice' alone is not the precise answer.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_POSET_8

  10. 10
    To prove a statement P(n) true for all n ≥ n0 using the principle of strong (complete) induction, the inductive step requires you to:
    1. AAssume only P(n0) is true and directly prove P(n0 + 1)
    2. BAssume P(k) is true for one arbitrarily fixed k and prove P(n0)
    3. CAssume P(k) is true for every k with n0 ≤ k ≤ n, and use that to prove P(n + 1)
    4. DAssume P(n + 1) is true and derive P(n) from it
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Strong induction differs from ordinary (weak) induction in what the inductive hypothesis is allowed to use. Ordinary induction's step assumes only the single previous case P(n) to prove P(n+1). Strong induction is permitted to assume that P holds for every case from the base up through n, which is essential when P(n+1) genuinely depends on more than just its immediate predecessor — the classic example being proving every integer greater than 1 has a prime factorization.

  11. 11
    The less than relation, <, on real is
    1. AA partial ordering since it is asymmetric and reflexive
    2. BA partial ordering since it is anti-symmetric and reflexive
    3. CNot a partial ordering because it is not asymmetric and not reflexive
    4. DNot a partial ordering because it is not anti-symmetric and not reflexive
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    '<' is not reflexive (a<a is always false), which alone disqualifies it from being a partial order; it is actually antisymmetric (vacuously) and transitive. Both printed options pair the right conclusion with a shaky reason (claiming < isn't antisymmetric), so the question is imprecisely worded — (d) is the conventionally keyed choice despite that flaw.

    1. AA partial ordering
    2. BAn equivalence relation
    3. CTransitive and symmetric only
    4. DTransitive and anti-symmetric only
    Show answer

    Answer: A
