Graph Theory

The heaviest cluster in Unit 1. Almost every question is one formula away from the answer — memorise the table, don't reason from first principles under time pressure.

12 concept cards30 past questions

Worked method

Rank and nullity — telling them apart
  1. Count n = vertices, e = edges, k = connected components.
  2. Rank = n − k. It counts *vertices*, so it is the spanning-forest edge count.
  3. Nullity (circuit rank, cyclomatic number) = e − n + k. It counts the *independent cycles* — the edges left over once a spanning forest is removed.
  4. Sanity check: rank + nullity = e. If your two numbers do not add to the edge count, one of them is wrong.
Worked example

A graph with 7 vertices, 9 edges and 2 components.

Rank = n − k = 7 − 2 = 5. Nullity = e − n + k = 9 − 7 + 2 = 4.

Check: 5 + 4 = 9 = e. ✓

Graph basics: vertices, edges, graph types

RememberUndirected halves the directed count: n(n−1)/2 versus n(n−1).

A graph G = (V, E) is a set of vertices V together with a set of edges E, each edge joining a pair of vertices.

Key facts & formulas
  • A simple graph has no loops and no parallel edges.
  • Maximum edges in a simple undirected graph on n vertices: n(n−1)/2.
  • Maximum edges in a simple digraph on n vertices: n(n−1).
  • In a digraph the edge (u, v) is ordered, so (v, u) is a distinct edge.
  • Order of a graph = number of vertices; size = number of edges.
  • A loop contributes 2 to the degree of its vertex in an undirected graph.
Traps
  • Every 'maximum number of edges' formula silently assumes a simple graph — a multigraph has no upper bound at all.
  • A loop adds 2 to the degree, not 1; this quietly breaks handshaking arithmetic in MCQs.
  • 'Order' means the vertex count, not the edge count — the paper uses it that way and offers the edge count as a distractor.
  • Null graph (vertices, no edges) and trivial graph (one vertex) are different things and both appear as options.
  • n(n−1)/2 is the undirected count and n(n−1) the directed one; the question wording decides which.
Full explanation

In an undirected graph an edge is the unordered pair {u, v}; in a directed graph (digraph) it is the ordered pair (u, v), so u→v and v→u are two different edges. A loop is an edge from a vertex to itself. Parallel (multiple) edges are two or more edges with the same endpoints. A simple graph has no loops and no parallel edges; a multigraph permits parallel edges. Almost every formula the paper asks for — Kn has n(n−1)/2 edges, a tree has n−1 edges — assumes a simple graph, so read the question for the word 'simple'. The order of G is |V| = n and the size of G is |E| = m; Indian textbooks use those two words in exactly that way. A null graph has no edges; a trivial graph is a single vertex with no edges. The maximum number of edges in a simple undirected graph on n vertices is n(n−1)/2, and in a simple digraph it is n(n−1), because each ordered pair may appear once. Worked count: with n = 5 an undirected simple graph tops out at 5·4/2 = 10 edges and a digraph at 20. A weighted graph attaches a number to each edge; every shortest-path and minimum-spanning-tree question is set on a weighted graph.

Degree, handshaking lemma, degree sequence

RememberEvery edge shakes two hands, so degrees total twice the edges.

The degree of a vertex is the number of edge-ends meeting it, and the handshaking lemma states that the degrees of all vertices sum to twice the number of edges.

Key facts & formulas
  • Handshaking lemma: Σ deg(v) = 2|E|.
  • The number of odd-degree vertices in any graph is even.
  • A k-regular graph on n vertices has nk/2 edges.
  • A k-regular graph with k odd requires n to be even.
  • In a digraph, Σ in-degree = Σ out-degree = |E|.
  • Degree sequence is an isomorphism invariant but not a complete one.
  • Maximum degree in a simple graph on n vertices is n−1.
Traps
  • Σ deg = 2|E|, not |E| — halve the degree sum to get the edge count.
  • Equal degree sequences do not imply isomorphism: C6 and two disjoint triangles both give (2,2,2,2,2,2) and are not isomorphic.
  • Any candidate degree sequence with an odd sum is impossible — spot this instantly in 'which is a valid degree sequence' questions.
  • A sequence containing an entry ≥ n is invalid for a simple graph on n vertices.
  • In a digraph, 'degree' may mean in-degree, out-degree, or their sum; check which the question means.
Full explanation

Write deg(v) for the degree of v; a loop counts twice. The handshaking lemma is Σ over v of deg(v) = 2|E|, because every edge contributes one to the degree of each of its two endpoints, so the total is exactly twice the edge count. Two corollaries the papers use constantly: the sum of all degrees is always even, and the number of vertices of odd degree is always even. Worked example: a 3-regular (cubic) graph on 10 vertices has 3·10/2 = 15 edges, and no 3-regular graph exists on an odd number of vertices, because 3n would be odd. More generally a k-regular graph on n vertices has nk/2 edges. In a digraph each vertex has an in-degree and an out-degree, and Σ in-degree = Σ out-degree = |E|. The degree sequence is the list of degrees, conventionally written non-increasing, e.g. (3, 3, 2, 2, 2). It is an isomorphism invariant, so two graphs with different degree sequences cannot be isomorphic — but equal sequences prove nothing. Not every list is a valid degree sequence: it must have an even sum, no entry may exceed n−1 in a simple graph, and the Havel–Hakimi / Erdős–Gallai conditions settle the rest.

Complete, regular, bipartite and planar graphs

RememberK5 and K3,3 are the two forbidden shapes behind every planarity question.

Named families of graphs — complete Kn, k-regular, bipartite, complete bipartite K(m,n) and planar — each with a standard edge count or drawing property the exam asks for directly.

Key facts & formulas
  • Kn has n(n−1)/2 edges and is (n−1)-regular.
  • Chromatic number of Kn is n.
  • K(m,n) has m+n vertices and mn edges.
  • A graph is bipartite iff it contains no odd-length cycle.
  • Euler's formula for a connected planar graph: v − e + f = 2.
  • Simple connected planar graph with v ≥ 3 satisfies e ≤ 3v − 6.
  • K5 and K(3,3) are the two minimal non-planar graphs (Kuratowski's theorem).
Traps
  • e ≤ 3v − 6 is necessary but not sufficient: K(3,3) has e = 9 ≤ 12 and is still non-planar.
  • K(m,n) has mn edges, not m+n and not (m+n)(m+n−1)/2.
  • Bipartite forbids odd cycles only — C6 is bipartite, C5 is not.
  • Euler's formula v − e + f = 2 requires connectedness; use 1 + k otherwise.
  • K5 is the smallest complete non-planar graph, but K4 is planar despite looking crossed in the usual drawing.
Full explanation

The complete graph Kn joins every pair of distinct vertices by exactly one edge. It has n(n−1)/2 edges, is (n−1)-regular, and has chromatic number n. K4 has 6 edges and K5 has 10. A graph is k-regular when every vertex has degree k. A bipartite graph splits V into two disjoint sets V1 and V2 with every edge running between the sets and none inside a set; equivalently, a graph is bipartite if and only if it contains no cycle of odd length. The complete bipartite graph K(m,n) joins every vertex of an m-set to every vertex of an n-set: m+n vertices, mn edges, chromatic number 2. A planar graph can be drawn in the plane with no edges crossing. For a connected planar simple graph Euler's formula gives v − e + f = 2, where f counts faces including the unbounded outer one; with k components it becomes v − e + f = 1 + k. Two consequences used to disprove planarity: e ≤ 3v − 6 for v ≥ 3, and e ≤ 2v − 4 when the graph is also triangle-free. K5 (v = 5, e = 10 > 3·5−6 = 9) and K(3,3) (triangle-free, e = 9 > 2·6−4 = 8) are the two minimal non-planar graphs. Kuratowski's theorem: a graph is planar iff it has no subgraph homeomorphic to K5 or K(3,3).

Subgraphs, cycle graphs and complements

RememberInduced = 'you must take the edges that come with the vertices'.

A subgraph is a graph formed from a subset of another graph's vertices and edges; an induced subgraph must additionally keep every edge whose endpoints both lie in the chosen vertex set.

Key facts & formulas
  • An induced subgraph on S contains every edge of G with both endpoints in S.
  • A spanning subgraph keeps every vertex of G.
  • Cn has n vertices, n edges, and is 2-regular.
  • |E(G)| + |E(complement of G)| = n(n−1)/2.
  • G ≅ H if and only if their complements are isomorphic.
  • A graph with m edges has 2^m spanning subgraphs.
  • Self-complementary graphs require n ≡ 0 or 1 (mod 4); C5 is the smallest with n > 1.
Traps
  • Subgraph and induced subgraph are not the same: omitting an edge between two chosen vertices is a legal subgraph but not an induced one.
  • 'Cyclic graph' may mean the cycle graph Cn or simply 'contains a cycle' — read the options before choosing.
  • The complement of Kn is the null graph on n vertices, not Kn again.
  • Complement edge count depends on n through n(n−1)/2, not on |E(G)| alone.
  • A spanning subgraph need not be connected; only a spanning tree is required to be.
Full explanation

H = (V', E') is a subgraph of G = (V, E) when V' ⊆ V, E' ⊆ E, and every edge in E' has both endpoints in V'. A spanning subgraph keeps all of V (all vertices, some edges). The induced subgraph G[S] on a vertex set S takes S together with every edge of G whose both endpoints lie in S — you may not drop such an edge. That is the distinction the papers test: any subset of edges gives a subgraph, but only the complete set of internal edges gives an induced subgraph. The cycle graph Cn (n ≥ 3) has n vertices in a single cycle: n edges, 2-regular, connected. A graph is cyclic if it contains at least one cycle and acyclic if it contains none; a connected acyclic graph is a tree. The complement Ḡ of a simple graph G is on the same vertex set and contains exactly the edges not in G, so |E(G)| + |E(Ḡ)| = n(n−1)/2. Worked: a 5-vertex graph with 4 edges has a complement with 10 − 4 = 6 edges. Also G ≅ H if and only if Ḡ ≅ H̄, which is a useful isomorphism shortcut. A self-complementary graph has G ≅ Ḡ, hence n(n−1)/4 edges, which forces n ≡ 0 or 1 (mod 4); C5 is the smallest non-trivial example.

Walks, trails, paths, circuits and cycles

RememberTrail spares Edges, Path spares Vertices — T-E, P-V.

A walk is any alternating sequence of vertices and edges; a trail forbids repeated edges, a path forbids repeated vertices, and a closed version of each gives a circuit or a cycle.

Key facts & formulas
  • Length of a walk or path is its number of edges.
  • A path of length k passes through k+1 vertices.
  • Trail = no repeated edge; path = no repeated vertex.
  • Every path is a trail and every trail is a walk; the converses fail.
  • The longest path in a simple graph on n vertices has n−1 edges.
  • If a u–v walk exists, a u–v path exists.
Traps
  • Length counts edges: 'a path through 5 vertices' has length 4, not 5.
  • 'Circuit' means closed trail in Rosen but closed non-repeating-vertex walk in Deo — do not assume one convention.
  • A trail may revisit vertices; only a path may not. Options often swap the two definitions.
  • Euler questions concern trails and circuits (edges); Hamiltonian questions concern paths and cycles (vertices). Mixing them is the classic error.
  • A closed walk of length 0, and a single loop, are edge cases some texts exclude from 'cycle'.
Full explanation

A walk is v0, e1, v1, …, ek, vk where each ei joins v(i−1) and vi; vertices and edges may repeat. Its length is k, the number of edges — always count edges, never vertices. A walk is open if v0 ≠ vk and closed if v0 = vk. A trail is a walk with no repeated edge, though vertices may repeat. A path is a walk with no repeated vertex, which automatically forbids repeated edges, so every path is a trail. A cycle is a closed walk of length at least 3 in which no vertex repeats except the shared start and end. Textbooks genuinely disagree on 'circuit': Rosen and most Western texts use circuit = closed trail (edges distinct, vertices may repeat), whereas Narsingh Deo — the standard Indian reference for this syllabus — defines a circuit as a closed walk in which no vertex except the endpoints repeats, i.e. what others call a cycle. If the option list distinguishes them, follow the definitions the paper itself gives. A path of length k visits k+1 vertices. In a simple graph on n vertices the longest possible path has n−1 edges and the longest cycle has n edges. If a u–v walk exists then a u–v path exists, obtained by deleting the repeated segment.

Connectivity, components, vertex and edge connectivity

RememberKappa (vertices) ≤ Lambda (edges) ≤ delta (min degree) — alphabet order κ, λ then δ.

A graph is connected if a path joins every pair of vertices; vertex connectivity and edge connectivity measure how many vertices or edges must be deleted to break that property.

Key facts & formulas
  • Whitney's inequality: κ(G) ≤ λ(G) ≤ δ(G).
  • κ(Kn) = λ(Kn) = n−1.
  • A connected graph on n vertices has at least n−1 edges.
  • A graph with n vertices and k components has at least n−k edges.
  • An edge is a bridge if and only if it lies on no cycle.
  • A simple graph with more than (n−1)(n−2)/2 edges is necessarily connected.
  • Strong connectivity (digraph) needs directed paths both ways; weak connectivity does not.
Traps
  • Edge connectivity of Kn is n−1, not n and not n(n−1)/2.
  • The inequality order is κ ≤ λ ≤ δ; reversing it is a standard distractor.
  • Removing a cut vertex also removes all its incident edges; removing a bridge leaves both endpoints in place.
  • κ(Kn) = n−1 is a convention, since Kn cannot actually be disconnected by deleting vertices.
  • λ = δ often but not always; do not assume the bounds are tight for a given graph.
Full explanation

The maximal connected subgraphs of a graph are its connected components. A connected graph on n vertices needs at least n−1 edges, and a graph with n vertices and k components needs at least n−k. Conversely a simple graph with more than (n−1)(n−2)/2 edges must be connected. Vertex connectivity κ(G) is the smallest number of vertices whose removal disconnects G (or reduces it to a single vertex). Edge connectivity λ(G) is the smallest number of edges whose removal disconnects G. Whitney's inequality chains them to the minimum degree δ(G): κ(G) ≤ λ(G) ≤ δ(G). For the complete graph Kn no vertex deletion ever disconnects it, so by convention κ(Kn) = n−1; and λ(Kn) = n−1 as well, because isolating one vertex means cutting all n−1 of its edges — this exact value is an MPSC favourite. A cut vertex (articulation point) is a vertex whose removal increases the number of components, and a bridge (cut edge) is the edge analogue; an edge is a bridge if and only if it lies on no cycle. For digraphs, strongly connected means a directed path exists both ways between every ordered pair of vertices, while weakly connected only requires the underlying undirected graph to be connected.

Euler paths and Euler circuits

RememberEuler = Edges, and Even degrees. Two odd vertices are the two open ends.

An Euler trail uses every edge of a graph exactly once, and an Euler circuit is a closed Euler trail; both are decided purely by the parity of vertex degrees.

Key facts & formulas
  • Euler circuit exists iff the graph is connected and every vertex has even degree.
  • Open Euler trail exists iff the graph is connected and exactly two vertices have odd degree.
  • An open Euler trail must begin and end at the two odd-degree vertices.
  • Digraph Euler circuit exists iff connected and in-degree = out-degree at every vertex.
  • Kn has an Euler circuit iff n is odd.
  • An Euler trail may visit a vertex many times; it uses each edge exactly once.
  • Fleury's and Hierholzer's algorithms construct Euler trails.
Traps
  • All-even degrees is sufficient only together with connectedness — a disconnected all-even graph has no Euler circuit.
  • Zero odd vertices gives a circuit; exactly two gives an open trail. Do not confuse the two answers.
  • Exactly one odd vertex is impossible by handshaking, and four odd vertices means no Euler trail at all.
  • Euler is about edges and Hamiltonian about vertices: K4 is Hamiltonian but has no Euler trail, since all four degrees are 3.
  • Loops add 2 to a degree and so can never break the parity condition.
Full explanation

A connected graph has an Euler circuit if and only if every vertex has even degree. It has an open Euler trail (not a circuit) if and only if exactly two vertices have odd degree, and that trail must start at one odd vertex and finish at the other. Any other count of odd vertices — which by handshaking must be even, so 4, 6, 8 — means no Euler trail exists at all. The reason is simple: each visit to an intermediate vertex enters on one edge and leaves on another, consuming degree in pairs; only the two endpoints of an open trail escape this pairing. Isolated vertices are ignored, but the edge-bearing part must be connected. The Königsberg bridges form a multigraph on four vertices of degrees 3, 3, 3 and 5 — four odd vertices, so no Euler trail, which is Euler's 1736 result and the origin of the subject. For a digraph an Euler circuit exists iff the graph is connected when direction is ignored and in-degree equals out-degree at every vertex. Kn has an Euler circuit exactly when n is odd, since every degree is n−1. Fleury's algorithm constructs a trail by never crossing a bridge unless forced; Hierholzer's method does it in linear time.

Hamiltonian paths and circuits

RememberHamiltonian visits Houses (vertices) once; Euler walks Edges once.

A Hamiltonian path visits every vertex of a graph exactly once, and a Hamiltonian circuit is a closed one returning to its start.

Key facts & formulas
  • A Hamiltonian path in a graph on n vertices has n−1 edges, so its length is n−1.
  • A Hamiltonian circuit has n edges.
  • Dirac: n ≥ 3 and every degree ≥ n/2 implies Hamiltonian.
  • Ore: deg(u) + deg(v) ≥ n for all non-adjacent u, v implies Hamiltonian.
  • Kn with n ≥ 3 has (n−1)!/2 distinct Hamiltonian circuits.
  • A graph with a cut vertex has no Hamiltonian circuit.
  • Deciding Hamiltonicity is NP-complete, while the Euler test is linear time.
Traps
  • The length of a Hamiltonian path is n−1 (edges), not n (vertices) — this exact wording appears in past papers.
  • Dirac and Ore are sufficient, not necessary: C5 fails Dirac (degrees 2 < 2.5) yet is Hamiltonian.
  • Hamiltonian and Eulerian are independent properties; neither implies the other.
  • K(m,n) is Hamiltonian only for m = n, so K(2,3) is not.
  • A Hamiltonian path may leave edges unused; only Euler trails must consume every edge.
Full explanation

In a connected graph on n vertices a Hamiltonian path passes through all n vertices and therefore uses exactly n−1 edges, so its length is n−1; a Hamiltonian circuit uses n edges and has length n. Unlike Euler's clean parity test, no simple necessary-and-sufficient criterion is known: deciding Hamiltonicity is NP-complete, which is precisely why the exam asks why no easy rule exists. What we have are sufficient conditions. Dirac's theorem: a simple graph with n ≥ 3 in which every vertex has degree at least n/2 is Hamiltonian. Ore's theorem: if deg(u) + deg(v) ≥ n for every pair of non-adjacent vertices u and v, the graph is Hamiltonian. Both are sufficient only — failing them proves nothing. A useful necessary condition works the other way: if deleting k vertices leaves more than k components, the graph is not Hamiltonian, so any graph with a cut vertex has no Hamiltonian circuit. Kn is Hamiltonian for n ≥ 3 and contains (n−1)!/2 distinct Hamiltonian circuits. K(m,n) is Hamiltonian only when m = n. The Petersen graph is the standard counterexample used in vivas: it has Hamiltonian paths but no Hamiltonian circuit.

Graph colouring and chromatic number

RememberOne edge forces two colours; one odd cycle forces three.

A proper colouring assigns colours to vertices so that no two adjacent vertices share one, and the chromatic number χ(G) is the fewest colours for which this is possible.

Key facts & formulas
  • A graph with one or more edges has χ ≥ 2, i.e. it is at least 2-chromatic.
  • χ(G) = 1 if and only if G has no edges.
  • χ(Kn) = n; χ(Cn) = 2 for n even and 3 for n odd.
  • χ(G) ≤ 2 iff G is bipartite iff G has no odd cycle.
  • Greedy bound: χ(G) ≤ Δ(G) + 1.
  • Four colour theorem: every planar graph is 4-colourable (Appel and Haken, 1976).
  • Vizing's theorem: Δ ≤ χ'(G) ≤ Δ + 1 for a simple graph.
Traps
  • 'At least 2-chromatic' needs only one edge to exist; a graph of isolated vertices is 1-chromatic.
  • Four colours suffice for planar graphs only — K5 needs 5 colours and is non-planar.
  • χ ≤ Δ + 1 is an upper bound, and it is tight for Kn, so Δ alone is not the answer.
  • Chromatic number colours vertices; chromatic index colours edges. Read which the question asks.
  • χ(Cn) flips with parity: C4 needs 2 colours, C5 needs 3.
Full explanation

Basic values first, because the paper asks for them directly. χ(G) = 1 if and only if G has no edges, so any graph with one or more edges is at least 2-chromatic — that is the exact MPSC phrasing. χ(Kn) = n. χ(Cn) = 2 when n is even and 3 when n is odd. A graph is bipartite iff χ ≤ 2, equivalently iff it has no odd cycle, so any tree with at least one edge is 2-chromatic. Bounds: χ(G) ≥ ω(G), the number of vertices in the largest clique, and χ(G) ≤ Δ(G) + 1 where Δ is the maximum degree — this upper bound falls straight out of greedy colouring, since a vertex has at most Δ coloured neighbours. Brooks' theorem sharpens it to χ ≤ Δ for connected graphs that are neither a complete graph nor an odd cycle. The four colour theorem states that every planar graph is 4-colourable; it was conjectured in 1852 and proved by Appel and Haken in 1976 with computer assistance, the first major theorem so proved. The weaker five colour theorem has a short hand proof. The chromatic index χ'(G) colours edges so that edges sharing a vertex differ, and Vizing's theorem bounds it by Δ ≤ χ' ≤ Δ + 1 for simple graphs.

Adjacency matrix, adjacency list, incidence matrix

RememberIncidence rows are vertices, columns edges, rank one less than the vertex count when connected.

The three standard ways to store a graph: an n×n adjacency matrix, per-vertex adjacency lists, and an n×m incidence matrix of vertices against edges.

Key facts & formulas
  • Adjacency matrix space is Θ(n²); adjacency list space is Θ(n + m).
  • The (i, j) entry of A^k counts walks of length k from i to j.
  • The adjacency matrix of an undirected graph is symmetric.
  • The incidence matrix is n×m: rows are vertices, columns are edges.
  • Every column of an undirected incidence matrix contains exactly two 1s.
  • Rank of A(G) over GF(2) is n−1 for a connected graph, and n−k with k components.
  • BFS and DFS cost O(n + m) with lists but O(n²) with a matrix.
Traps
  • The rank of the incidence matrix of a connected graph is n−1, not n and not m.
  • Incidence is n×m (vertices × edges) while adjacency is n×n; distractors swap the dimensions.
  • A^k counts walks, not paths — walks are allowed to repeat vertices.
  • Row sum of the adjacency matrix equals the vertex degree for a simple graph; loop conventions (diagonal 1 or 2) differ between texts, so loops break this.
  • The adjacency matrix wastes Θ(n²) space even on a sparse graph, so the expected answer for 'best representation of a sparse graph' is the adjacency list.
Full explanation

The adjacency matrix A is n×n with A[i][j] = 1 when an edge joins i to j (or the weight, or the count of parallel edges). For an undirected graph A is symmetric. Space is Θ(n²) whatever the edge count, edge lookup is O(1), and listing a vertex's neighbours costs O(n). A key exam property: the (i, j) entry of A^k is the number of distinct walks of length k from i to j. An adjacency list stores for each vertex the list of its neighbours: space Θ(n + m), neighbour iteration proportional to degree, edge lookup O(deg). Use lists for sparse graphs and matrices for dense ones — BFS and DFS run in O(n + m) on lists but O(n²) on a matrix. The incidence matrix A(G) of an undirected graph is n×m: one row per vertex, one column per edge, with entry 1 when that vertex is an endpoint of that edge. Every column therefore holds exactly two 1s (a loop is the exception). Over GF(2) the rank of A(G) is n−1 for a connected graph on n vertices, and n−k for a graph with k components; this is the standard result MPSC asks for. The rows are dependent because every column sums to 0 modulo 2, so the rows themselves sum to the zero vector.

Graph isomorphism and invariants

RememberInvariants can only say NO; only a bijection can say YES.

Two graphs are isomorphic when a bijection between their vertex sets preserves adjacency in both directions, i.e. they are the same graph with the vertices relabelled.

Key facts & formulas
  • Isomorphism preserves both adjacency and non-adjacency.
  • Isomorphic graphs agree on |V|, |E|, degree sequence, girth, χ, κ, λ and component count.
  • A single differing invariant disproves isomorphism; matching invariants never prove it.
  • C6 and two disjoint triangles share the degree sequence (2,2,2,2,2,2) but are not isomorphic.
  • G ≅ H if and only if their complements are isomorphic.
  • The number of labelled simple graphs on n vertices is 2^(n(n−1)/2).
  • Graph isomorphism is neither known to be in P nor known to be NP-complete.
Traps
  • Equal vertex and edge counts are necessary but never sufficient.
  • Equal degree sequences are necessary but never sufficient.
  • To prove isomorphism you must supply the mapping; to disprove it one broken invariant suffices.
  • Subgraph isomorphism is a different problem and is NP-complete.
  • A map that sends edges to edges but creates extra adjacencies is a homomorphism, not an isomorphism.
Full explanation

Formally G ≅ H if there is a bijection f: V(G) → V(H) such that u and v are adjacent in G if and only if f(u) and f(v) are adjacent in H. No polynomial-time algorithm is known for general graph isomorphism, so exam questions are settled with invariants — properties that any isomorphism must preserve. To prove two graphs are NOT isomorphic, exhibit a single invariant on which they differ: number of vertices; number of edges; degree sequence; number of connected components; number of cycles of each length, especially the girth; vertex and edge connectivity; chromatic number; planarity; bipartiteness; or whether their complements are isomorphic. Check them in that order, cheapest first. What you cannot do is argue the other way: matching invariants never prove isomorphism. The standard counterexample is two 2-regular graphs on six vertices — C6 and the disjoint union of two triangles — which share vertex count, edge count and the degree sequence (2,2,2,2,2,2), yet one is connected and the other is not. To prove isomorphism you must actually exhibit the bijection or argue structurally. The number of labelled simple graphs on n vertices is 2^(n(n−1)/2); the count up to isomorphism is far smaller and has no closed form.

Graph homomorphism

RememberHOMOmorphism keeps edges only; ISOmorphism keeps everything (iso = same).

A graph homomorphism from G to H is a map on vertices that sends every edge of G to an edge of H, without any requirement to be injective, surjective, or to preserve non-edges.

Key facts & formulas
  • Homomorphism condition: uv ∈ E(G) implies f(u)f(v) ∈ E(H).
  • A homomorphism need not be injective or surjective.
  • A homomorphism G → Kk exists iff G is k-colourable, so χ(G) is the least such k.
  • A homomorphism G → K2 exists iff G is bipartite.
  • An isomorphism is a bijective homomorphism whose inverse is also a homomorphism.
  • Homomorphisms compose: G → H and H → K give G → K.
Traps
  • Homomorphism preserves edges only; isomorphism preserves edges and non-edges. This is the single most-tested difference.
  • Homomorphic is not homeomorphic — homeomorphism is about edge subdivision and appears in Kuratowski's theorem.
  • Two adjacent vertices can never be merged by a homomorphism into a loop-free graph.
  • A homomorphism G → H does not imply one from H → G; the relation is not symmetric.
  • Existence of a homomorphism into Kk shows G is k-colourable, but the smallest such k is χ(G), not the k you happened to use.
Full explanation

Formally f: V(G) → V(H) is a homomorphism when uv ∈ E(G) implies f(u)f(v) ∈ E(H). Edges must go to edges; non-edges are unconstrained. That one-way condition is the whole difference from isomorphism: an isomorphism is a bijective homomorphism whose inverse is also a homomorphism, so it preserves non-adjacency as well. Because adjacent vertices must map to adjacent vertices and H has no loops, no two adjacent vertices of G may collapse onto the same vertex of H. That gives the key identity the exam wants: a homomorphism G → Kk exists if and only if G is k-colourable, since the k vertices of Kk act as k colours and the edge condition says exactly that adjacent vertices receive different colours. Hence χ(G) is the least k admitting a homomorphism into Kk, and G is bipartite iff a homomorphism G → K2 exists. It follows that no homomorphism exists from a graph containing a triangle into a triangle-free graph. Homomorphisms compose, and 'G → H and H → G' defines homomorphic equivalence, which is strictly weaker than isomorphism. Beware a near-homonym on this syllabus: homeomorphic graphs are those obtainable from each other by subdividing edges with degree-2 vertices, a different idea used in Kuratowski's planarity theorem.

  24. 24
    Which of the following statements for a simple graph is correct?
    1. AEvery path is a trail
    2. BEvery trail is a path
    3. CEvery trail is a path as well as every path is a trail
    4. DNone of these
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Every path is automatically a trail, since a path forbids repeated vertices and, in a simple graph, that already rules out repeated edges. The reverse direction fails: a trail only forbids repeated edges and can revisit a vertex (for example a closed trail returning to its start), so it need not be a path — making 'every trail is a path' the false statement.

    Stored answer was (b); corrected to (a) by the 2026-09-02 answer-key audit, upheld by two independent adjudicators.

    derived · highMPSC CSE Paper I (year not recorded)TECH1_CSE_028

  25. 25
    An undirected graph G with n vertices and e edges is represented by adjacency list. What is the time required to generate all the connected components?
    1. AO(n)
    2. BO(e)
    3. CO(e+n)
    4. DO(e²)
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Using an adjacency list, a DFS/BFS-based scan visits every vertex once and scans each vertex's edge list once, giving O(n+e) time to find all connected components.

    derived · unratedMPSC CSE Paper I (year not recorded)TECH1_CSE_030

Trees, Spanning Trees and Graph Algorithms

Unit 1 asks the *properties* (n−1 edges, Cayley's formula); Unit 3 asks the same algorithms for their *complexity*. Learn them once, answer both.

7 concept cards18 past questions

Trees: definition and properties

RememberTree: n vertices, n−1 edges, exactly 1 path between any pair.

A tree is a connected acyclic graph, and a tree on n vertices always has exactly n−1 edges and exactly one path between any two of its vertices.

Key facts & formulas
  • A tree on n vertices has exactly n−1 edges.
  • Exactly one path joins any two vertices of a tree.
  • Every edge of a tree is a bridge.
  • Every tree with n ≥ 2 vertices has at least two leaves.
  • Adding any one new edge to a tree creates exactly one cycle.
  • A forest with n vertices and k components has n−k edges.
  • A tree with at least one edge is bipartite and 2-chromatic.
Traps
  • Having n−1 edges alone does not make a tree: a triangle plus an isolated vertex has 4 vertices and 3 edges and is not one. Connectedness or acyclicity must also hold.
  • The number of paths between any pair of nodes in a tree is 1, not n−1.
  • A tree contains no cycle at all, so options giving it a girth of 3 are distractors.
  • Forest edge count is n−k, not n−1.
  • Every non-leaf vertex of a tree is a cut vertex, so a tree with n ≥ 3 is never 2-connected.
Full explanation

For a graph T on n vertices the following statements are equivalent, and any one of them may be quoted as the definition: T is connected and acyclic; T is connected with exactly n−1 edges; T is acyclic with exactly n−1 edges; every pair of vertices is joined by exactly one path; T is connected but deleting any edge disconnects it; T is acyclic but adding any new edge creates exactly one cycle. The fourth item answers the paper's question about 'the number of paths between any pair of nodes in a tree': exactly one. The edge count is the other standard question — a tree with 10 vertices has 9 edges. Every edge of a tree is a bridge, and every non-leaf vertex is a cut vertex. A leaf (pendant vertex) has degree 1, and every tree with n ≥ 2 has at least two leaves; that follows from handshaking, since if all n vertices had degree at least 2 the degree sum would be at least 2n, exceeding 2(n−1) = 2|E|. A forest is a disjoint union of trees, and a forest with n vertices and k trees has n−k edges. A rooted tree adds a distinguished root and with it the parent/child and level structure used in data-structure questions.

Spanning trees and Cayley's formula

RememberCayley: n to the power n minus two — check it on K3 giving 3.

A spanning tree of a connected graph is a subgraph that is a tree and contains every vertex, and the complete graph Kn has n^(n−2) distinct labelled spanning trees.

Key facts & formulas
  • A spanning tree of a connected graph on n vertices has exactly n−1 edges.
  • Cayley's formula: Kn has n^(n−2) labelled spanning trees.
  • K3 has 3 spanning trees and K4 has 16.
  • Circuit rank (nullity) is m − n + 1 for a connected graph, and m − n + k with k components.
  • Kirchhoff's matrix-tree theorem: any cofactor of L = D − A counts the spanning trees.
  • The MST is unique when all edge weights are distinct.
  • A spanning tree exists if and only if the graph is connected.
Traps
  • Cayley's formula is n^(n−2), not n^(n−1) and not (n−1)^(n−2).
  • Cayley counts labelled trees; the count of unlabelled trees is much smaller and has no closed form.
  • Circuit rank m − n + 1 counts the edges removed; the edges kept always number n−1.
  • An MST minimises total weight, not edge count — every spanning tree already has n−1 edges.
  • Distinct weights guarantee a unique MST, but a graph with tied weights may still have a unique one, so the implication does not reverse.
Full explanation

A spanning tree has n vertices and n−1 edges whatever the parent graph looks like. One exists if and only if G is connected; a disconnected graph instead has a spanning forest with n−k edges for k components. The number of edges that must be removed from a connected graph to leave a spanning tree is m − n + 1, called the cycle rank, circuit rank or nullity of G; correspondingly the rank of G is n − 1, matching the rank of its incidence matrix. Cayley's formula gives the number of distinct labelled spanning trees of Kn as n^(n−2). Check the small cases: n = 2 gives 2^0 = 1; n = 3 gives 3^1 = 3, since you drop any one of the triangle's three edges; n = 4 gives 4^2 = 16. Equivalently n^(n−2) counts labelled trees on n vertices. For a general graph, Kirchhoff's matrix-tree theorem gives the spanning-tree count as any cofactor of the Laplacian L = D − A, where D is the diagonal degree matrix and A the adjacency matrix. A minimum spanning tree is a spanning tree of least total edge weight; it is unique whenever all edge weights are distinct, and both Kruskal's and Prim's algorithms produce one.

Kruskal's algorithm for minimum spanning trees

RememberKruskal = cheapest edge anywhere; keeps a forest until the last join.

Kruskal's algorithm builds a minimum spanning tree by scanning all edges in non-decreasing weight order and accepting each edge that does not close a cycle.

Key facts & formulas
  • Kruskal sorts edges and adds the cheapest edge that does not create a cycle.
  • Complexity O(m log m), equivalently O(m log n), dominated by the sort.
  • Cycle detection uses a union–find (disjoint-set) structure.
  • The intermediate structure is a forest, not a single connected tree.
  • It halts after n−1 edges have been accepted.
  • On disconnected input it yields a minimum spanning forest.
  • Correctness rests on the cut property.
Traps
  • Kruskal's intermediate result is a forest while Prim's is always one connected tree — this is the standard discriminator question.
  • An edge is rejected because its endpoints already share a component, not merely because it is heavy.
  • The expected complexity answer is O(m log m) or O(m log n); O(n²) is Prim's array form, not Kruskal's.
  • Kruskal needs no start vertex; Prim does.
  • Kruskal is greedy yet provably optimal here — do not reject it on the grounds that greedy algorithms often fail.
Full explanation

Sort the m edges by weight, then scan them in that order, adding an edge to the growing forest whenever its two endpoints lie in different components and discarding it when they already lie in the same one, which would create a cycle. Stop once n−1 edges have been accepted. The cycle test is done with a disjoint-set (union–find) structure: find the roots of the two endpoints and union them if the roots differ. Complexity is O(m log m), dominated by the sort; since m ≤ n² this is equivalently O(m log n), plus near-constant amortised union–find work. Worked trace on four vertices with edges AB = 1, CD = 2, BC = 3, AC = 4, BD = 5: accept AB, accept CD, then accept BC, which joins the two fragments — three edges on four vertices, so we stop, total weight 6; AC and BD would each close a cycle. Kruskal is globally greedy: it considers the cheapest edge anywhere in the graph, so its intermediate structure is a forest of disconnected fragments rather than one tree. It also runs happily on disconnected input, producing a minimum spanning forest. Correctness follows from the cut property: for any partition of the vertices, a lightest edge crossing the partition belongs to some MST.

Prim's algorithm for minimum spanning trees

RememberPrim = one tree grows outward; Kruskal = many fragments merge.

Prim's algorithm builds a minimum spanning tree by growing one connected tree from an arbitrary start vertex, repeatedly adding the cheapest edge that leaves the tree.

Key facts & formulas
  • Prim grows one connected tree, always adding the cheapest edge crossing from S to the rest.
  • O(n²) with an adjacency matrix and array scan; O(m log n) with a binary heap; O(m + n log n) with a Fibonacci heap.
  • Prim suits dense graphs; Kruskal suits sparse graphs.
  • Prim requires a connected graph; Kruskal does not.
  • Prim and Kruskal always return the same total weight.
  • Prim's structure mirrors Dijkstra's, but its key is a single edge weight rather than an accumulated distance.
Traps
  • Prim's key is the weight of one edge to the tree; Dijkstra's key is the total distance from the source. Swapping them is the classic error.
  • Prim's partial result is always connected, so 'Prim may produce disconnected fragments' is false.
  • Different start vertices can give different MSTs when weights tie, but never a different total weight.
  • On dense graphs the O(n²) implementation beats the O(m log n) heap version, so the 'better complexity' depends on density.
  • Run on a disconnected graph, Prim silently returns the MST of the start vertex's component only.
Full explanation

Maintain a set S of vertices already in the tree. Repeatedly pick the cheapest edge with exactly one endpoint in S, add that edge and its outside endpoint to S, and continue until S = V, at which point n−1 edges have been added. The result is a minimum spanning tree with the same total weight as Kruskal's, and the same tree when all weights are distinct. Complexity depends on the structure holding candidate edges: O(n²) with a simple array scan over an adjacency matrix, O(m log n) with a binary heap over adjacency lists, and O(m + n log n) with a Fibonacci heap. So the O(n²) form is preferred on dense graphs where m is near n², while Kruskal's O(m log m) is preferred on sparse graphs. The differences the papers test: Prim holds a single connected tree at every step whereas Kruskal holds a forest; Prim needs a starting vertex and Kruskal does not; Prim uses a priority queue keyed on vertices while Kruskal uses a sort plus union–find; Prim requires a connected graph, whereas Kruskal degrades gracefully to a spanning forest. Correctness is again the cut property, applied to the cut between S and V minus S. Prim's is also called the Jarník–Prim algorithm, and its loop structure is nearly identical to Dijkstra's.

Dijkstra's single-source shortest path

RememberDijkstra freezes whatever it picks — a later negative edge could have unfrozen it.

Dijkstra's algorithm computes the shortest-path distances from one source vertex to every other vertex in a graph whose edge weights are all non-negative.

Key facts & formulas
  • Dijkstra solves single-source shortest paths for non-negative edge weights.
  • O(n²) with an array, O((n + m) log n) with a binary heap, O(m + n log n) with a Fibonacci heap.
  • Once a vertex is extracted its distance is final — the greedy invariant.
  • It fails on negative edge weights even when no negative cycle exists.
  • Bellman–Ford runs in O(nm), handles negative edges, and detects negative cycles.
  • On an unweighted graph, BFS is the O(n + m) equivalent.
  • Dijkstra works fine on cyclic graphs, provided weights are non-negative.
Traps
  • Dijkstra fails on any negative edge, not merely on negative cycles — the negative-cycle limit belongs to Bellman–Ford.
  • Dijkstra returns shortest paths, not a minimum spanning tree; the shortest-path tree usually differs from the MST.
  • Adding a constant to every edge to remove negatives is wrong: it penalises paths with more edges and can change which path is shortest.
  • It is single-source, not all-pairs; running it from every vertex is a valid but different answer from Floyd–Warshall's O(n³).
  • Relaxation never reopens a finalised vertex, which is the precise mechanism by which the negative-edge example fails.
Full explanation

Keep a tentative distance d[v], set to 0 for the source s and infinity elsewhere. Repeatedly extract the unvisited vertex u with the smallest d[u], mark it final, and relax each outgoing edge: if d[u] + w(u, v) < d[v], set d[v] = d[u] + w(u, v) and record u as v's predecessor. Complexity is O(n²) with a linear scan, O((n + m) log n) with a binary heap, and O(m + n log n) with a Fibonacci heap. The algorithm is greedy: the moment a vertex is extracted, its distance is declared final and never revisited. That is exactly why negative edge weights break it. Worked counterexample on three vertices with A→B = 2, A→C = 5 and C→B = −4: Dijkstra extracts B with d = 2 and freezes it, but the true shortest path A→C→B costs 5 − 4 = 1. The greedy invariant assumes that extending a path can only increase its cost, which a negative weight violates. Bellman–Ford handles negative edges in O(nm) and detects negative cycles; Johnson's algorithm reweights the graph so Dijkstra can be reused. If a negative cycle is reachable, no shortest path exists at all. On an unweighted graph, BFS gives the same answer in O(n + m).

Floyd–Warshall and Warshall's algorithm

RememberWarshall answers Whether you can get there; Floyd answers how Far.

Floyd–Warshall computes all-pairs shortest path distances in Θ(n³) by dynamic programming over intermediate vertices, and Warshall's algorithm is the same recurrence over booleans, giving the transitive closure.

Key facts & formulas
  • Floyd–Warshall: Θ(n³) time, Θ(n²) space, all-pairs shortest paths.
  • Recurrence: d[i][j] = min(d[i][j], d[i][k] + d[k][j]), with k as the outermost loop.
  • It handles negative edge weights and detects negative cycles via a negative diagonal entry.
  • Warshall's transitive closure: t[i][j] = t[i][j] OR (t[i][k] AND t[k][j]).
  • Both are dynamic-programming algorithms, not greedy ones.
  • Initialisation: 0 on the diagonal, edge weights where edges exist, infinity elsewhere.
Traps
  • k must be the outermost of the three loops; putting i or j outside gives wrong answers on some graphs.
  • Floyd–Warshall handles negative edges but not negative cycles — it only detects them.
  • The Θ(n³) cost holds regardless of sparsity, so on a sparse graph n runs of Dijkstra can be faster.
  • Warshall's algorithm gives 0/1 reachability while Floyd's gives distances; papers pair the two names as distractors.
  • An undirected graph with any negative edge automatically contains a negative cycle, so 'Floyd–Warshall handles negative undirected edges' is ill-posed.
Full explanation

Let d_k[i][j] be the shortest i-to-j distance using only vertices 1..k as intermediates. Then d_k[i][j] = min(d_(k−1)[i][j], d_(k−1)[i][k] + d_(k−1)[k][j]). Initialise the matrix with the edge weights, infinity for absent edges, and 0 on the diagonal. The implementation is three nested loops with k as the outermost — and k must be outermost, which is the commonest exam error, because the recurrence needs all pairs settled for intermediates 1..k−1 before k is considered. Complexity is Θ(n³) time and Θ(n²) space, independent of the edge count, so it beats n runs of Dijkstra on dense graphs. It handles negative edge weights correctly. It does not handle negative cycles, but it detects them: some diagonal entry d[i][i] becomes negative. Warshall's algorithm replaces min and + by OR and AND over booleans and computes the transitive closure, i.e. the reachability relation of a digraph: t[i][j] = t[i][j] OR (t[i][k] AND t[k][j]), also Θ(n³). The closure matrix answers only 'is there any directed path from i to j'. To recover actual paths from Floyd–Warshall, keep a predecessor matrix updated whenever the route through k wins, then walk it backwards.

Depth-first and breadth-first search

RememberBFS: Broad, uses a queue. DFS: Deep, uses a stack.

BFS explores a graph level by level using a queue while DFS goes as deep as possible along one branch before backtracking, using a stack; both visit every reachable vertex once in O(n + m).

Key facts & formulas
  • Both run in O(n + m) with adjacency lists and O(n²) with an adjacency matrix.
  • BFS uses a queue; DFS uses a stack or recursion.
  • BFS gives shortest paths by edge count in unweighted graphs; DFS does not.
  • DFS underlies topological sort, cycle detection, strongly connected components, and bridge/articulation-point finding.
  • BFS level 2-colouring tests bipartiteness.
  • Both build a spanning tree of the source's connected component.
  • In DFS on an undirected graph there are no cross edges — only tree edges and back edges.
Traps
  • BFS = queue and DFS = stack; the swap is the single most common MCQ trap in this area.
  • BFS gives shortest paths only for unweighted or unit-weight graphs; weights require Dijkstra.
  • One BFS or DFS reaches only the source's component, so a full traversal must loop over all vertices.
  • DFS stack depth is O(n) in the worst case, so 'DFS always uses less memory than BFS' is false.
  • Traversal order depends on the neighbour ordering in the adjacency list; if the question does not state it, the visiting order is not unique.
Full explanation

BFS enqueues the source, then repeatedly dequeues a vertex and enqueues its unvisited neighbours, so it visits all vertices at distance 1, then all at distance 2, and so on. DFS uses a stack — explicitly, or implicitly through recursion — descending one branch until it is exhausted, then backtracking. Both cost O(n + m) with adjacency lists and O(n²) with an adjacency matrix. Order example on the graph with edges A–B, A–C, B–D, C–D, taking neighbours alphabetically: BFS from A gives A, B, C, D; DFS from A gives A, B, D, C. What each is good for is the examinable half. BFS finds the shortest path in an unweighted graph, because the level at which a vertex is first reached is its hop distance from the source; it also tests bipartiteness by 2-colouring alternate levels. DFS is the tool for cycle detection, topological sorting of a DAG, finding strongly connected components (Kosaraju's and Tarjan's algorithms), and locating cut vertices and bridges. On space, BFS's queue can hold O(n) vertices — the widest level — while DFS's stack is bounded by the longest root-to-current path. Both produce a spanning tree of the component containing the source, together with classified non-tree edges.

  10. 10
    When inorder traversing a tree resulted E A C K F H D B G, the preorder traversal would return
    1. AFAEKCDBHG
    2. BFAEKCDHGB
    3. CEAFKHDCBG
    4. DFEAKDCHBG
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A preorder is consistent with a given inorder only if, at every step, the elements following the root in the preorder split into exactly the left and right inorder blocks. Taking F as the root (inorder EACK | F | HDBG), only FAEKCDHGB splits as AEKC / DHGB and continues to reconstruct all the way down; the other three contradict at the first or second level. So inorder plus these four candidates does pin the answer down.

    Recovered from page 5 of the source PDF, which was missing from the supplied OCR text. Answer verified by running the preorder/inorder reconstruction against all four options - exactly one is consistent.

    Which of the following is/are the operations performed by kruskal's algorithm in a graph G i) sort the edges of G in increasing order by length ii) keep a subgraph S of G initially empty iii) builds a tree one vertex at a time
    1. Ai, and ii only
    2. Bii and iii only
    3. Ci and iii only
    4. Dall i, ii and iii
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Kruskal's algorithm sorts all edges by increasing weight and starts with an empty subgraph, so statements (i) and (ii) both hold.   16. 16
    The Floyd-Warshall all-pairs shortest path algorithm for finding the shortest distances between nodes in a graph is an example of:
    1. AA Dynamic Programming
    2. BA Greedy Algorithm
    3. CA divide and conquer technique
    4. DBranch and bound technique
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Floyd-Warshall builds up all-pairs shortest paths by considering intermediate vertices incrementally and reusing previously computed sub-results, which is the defining characteristic of dynamic programming.

    derived · unratedMPSC CSE Paper I (year not recorded)TECH1_CSE_070

  17. 17
    Which data structures find their applications in BFS and DFS Traversal mechanisms on a Tree respectively?
    1. AGraph & Stack
    2. BQueue & Stack
    3. CQueue & Graph
    4. DNone of these
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    BFS explores level by level using a FIFO queue to remember which nodes to visit next, while DFS goes deep along one branch using a LIFO stack (explicit or via recursion) to backtrack.

Finite Automata and Regular Languages

Definition-and-table territory. The NFA→DFA state-count question (2^n) and the Mealy-vs-Moore distinction recur every year.

9 concept cards40 past questions

Worked method

NFA → DFA by subset construction
  1. The DFA's states are sets of NFA states. Start from the ε-closure of the NFA start state.
  2. For each unprocessed DFA state (a set S) and each input symbol a, compute the union of δ(q, a) over all q ∈ S, then take the ε-closure of that union. That set is the next DFA state.
  3. Add any new set to the worklist. Repeat until no new sets appear.
  4. A DFA state is accepting if the set contains at least one NFA accepting state.
  5. For the *how many states* variant, you do not build anything: an NFA with k states has at most 2ᵏ subsets, so the bound is 2ᵏ. Count k carefully — if the stem says 'k states excluding the start state', then k+1 is the total.
Worked example

*An NFA has 4 states. What is the maximum number of DFA states?*

2⁴ = 16. Note this is an upper bound — the reachable subset count is often far smaller, but the exam asks for the maximum.

Alphabets, strings, languages, Kleene star

An alphabet is a finite set of symbols, a string is a finite sequence of symbols drawn from it, and a language is any set of strings over that alphabet.

Key facts & formulas
  • Sigma* always contains epsilon; Sigma+ = Sigma* minus {epsilon}
  • Number of strings of length n over an alphabet of size k is k^n
  • Sigma* is countably infinite even though Sigma itself is finite
  • A language is any subset of Sigma*, including the empty set and Sigma* itself
  • Concatenation is associative; epsilon is the identity for concatenation
  • The empty language (no strings) and the language {epsilon} (one empty string) are different objects
Traps
  • Mixing up Sigma* (includes epsilon) with Sigma+ (excludes epsilon)
  • Treating the empty language {} and {epsilon} as the same thing — one has zero strings, the other has one
  • Assuming Sigma* is finite because Sigma is finite — it is not, strings have unbounded length
Full explanation

Everything in automata theory sits on this vocabulary. An alphabet Sigma is a finite, nonempty set of symbols, e.g. Sigma = {0,1}. A string (or word) is a finite concatenation of symbols from Sigma; the empty string, written epsilon, has length 0 and is a string over every alphabet. Sigma* denotes the Kleene star of Sigma: the set of all strings of any finite length over Sigma, including epsilon. It is built as the union Sigma^0 union Sigma^1 union Sigma^2 union ..., where Sigma^0 = {epsilon}. Sigma+ (Kleene plus) is Sigma* minus {epsilon}, i.e. all strings of length at least 1. A language L is simply any subset of Sigma*, from the empty language (no strings) to Sigma* itself (every string). Concatenation of strings is associative and epsilon is its identity element. For a finite alphabet of size k, there are exactly k^n distinct strings of length n, but Sigma* as a whole is always countably infinite, since strings can be arbitrarily long. This is the base layer everything else (DFA, regex, grammars) is defined on top of.

DFA — the 5-tuple

A deterministic finite automaton is a 5-tuple M = (Q, Sigma, delta, q0, F) that reads an input string one symbol at a time, following exactly one transition per symbol, and accepts if it ends in a final state.

Key facts & formulas
  • Exactly five components: (Q, Sigma, delta, q0, F)
  • delta: Q x Sigma -> Q is total and single-valued (one next state per input)
  • There is no output alphabet in the DFA tuple — it only accepts or rejects
  • Exactly one start state q0; F subset of Q can be empty or equal to Q
  • Missing transitions in a diagram are formally completed with an implicit trap/dead state
  • L(M) = set of strings that drive delta-hat from q0 into some state in F
Traps
  • "Which is NOT part of the finite automata 5-tuple" — the answer is the output alphabet; DFA has none
  • Confusing DFA's delta (returns one state) with NFA's delta (returns a set of states)
  • Assuming a DFA can have more than one start state — only one q0 is allowed by definition
  • Forgetting delta must be total; an "undefined" transition in a hand-drawn diagram is not the same as it being formally undefined in the tuple
Full explanation

Q is a finite set of states. Sigma is the input alphabet. delta: Q x Sigma -> Q is the transition function — total (defined for every state-symbol pair) and single-valued (exactly one next state, never zero, never many). q0 is the single start state. F subset of Q is the set of accepting (final) states. Extend delta to strings as delta-hat: delta-hat(q, epsilon) = q, and delta-hat(q, wa) = delta(delta-hat(q,w), a). The language accepted is L(M) = { w in Sigma* : delta-hat(q0, w) is in F }. Note carefully what is absent: there is no output alphabet in this tuple — a DFA only accepts or rejects, it does not produce output symbols. If a textbook diagram seems to have a missing transition for some (state, symbol) pair, the formal model still requires delta to be total, so an implicit dead/trap (non-accepting, self-looping) state is assumed to keep it well-defined. F can legally be empty (language is empty) or equal to Q (every string accepted).

NFA and NFA with epsilon moves

A nondeterministic finite automaton allows zero, one, or several transitions on a symbol from a given state, and an NFA with epsilon moves additionally allows a transition that consumes no input at all.

Key facts & formulas
  • delta: Q x Sigma -> 2^Q for an NFA — the result is a set of states, possibly empty
  • Acceptance means at least one computation path reaches an accepting state, not all paths
  • NFA-epsilon adds transitions on epsilon that consume zero input symbols
  • ECLOSE(q), the epsilon-closure of q, always includes q itself
  • NFA, NFA-epsilon, and DFA are equally expressive — all define the regular languages
  • NFAs are often exponentially smaller than an equivalent DFA (see subset construction)
Traps
  • Believing an NFA can accept languages no DFA can — false, they are equally powerful, only size differs
  • Forgetting to epsilon-close the start state itself before beginning simulation or conversion
  • Reading "nondeterministic" as "random" — it means "accept if any path accepts", with no probabilities involved
Full explanation

An NFA is the tuple (Q, Sigma, delta, q0, F), but delta: Q x Sigma -> 2^Q maps a state and symbol to a set of possible next states (possibly empty). A string is accepted if THERE EXISTS at least one computation path from q0, consistent with the input, that ends in an accepting state — not that every path must succeed. This "exists a path" rule is the whole meaning of nondeterminism here; it is not random choice, it is existential branching that the acceptance definition resolves in the automaton's favour if any branch works. An NFA-epsilon extends delta to Q x (Sigma union {epsilon}) -> 2^Q, allowing a state change without reading a symbol. The epsilon-closure of a state q, written ECLOSE(q), is the set of all states reachable from q using only epsilon transitions, and by convention always includes q itself. Despite looking more powerful, NFA, NFA-epsilon, and DFA all define exactly the same class of languages — the regular languages. What nondeterminism buys is succinctness: NFAs are often far smaller than the equivalent DFA, and constructions like Thompson's construction (regex to automaton) naturally produce an NFA-epsilon rather than a DFA.

NFA to DFA: subset construction

Subset construction (the powerset construction) converts an n-state NFA into an equivalent DFA whose states are sets of NFA states, with at most 2^n reachable DFA states in the worst case.

Key facts & formulas
  • Worst-case DFA size after subset construction is 2^n for an n-state NFA
  • DFA start state = ECLOSE({q0}) of the NFA
  • Transition on symbol a from DFA state S = ECLOSE(union of delta(q,a) for all q in S)
  • A DFA state is accepting if it contains ANY NFA accepting state, not all of them
  • The empty subset, if reachable, becomes the DFA's dead/trap state
  • Only reachable subsets are actually constructed, so real output is usually far smaller than 2^n
Traps
  • Assuming all 2^n subsets always get built — only reachable ones are, so the true DFA can be much smaller
  • Forgetting to re-apply epsilon-closure after every symbol transition when the NFA has epsilon moves
  • Marking a DFA state final only when ALL its NFA states are final — the correct rule is ANY one is final
Full explanation

Each DFA state is a subset S of the NFA's state set Q, representing "all NFA states the machine could currently be in, given the input read so far." The DFA start state is ECLOSE({q0}). For a DFA state S and symbol a, the transition goes to ECLOSE(union over q in S of delta(q,a)) — collect where every state in S could go on a, then epsilon-close the result. A DFA state is accepting if it contains at least one NFA accepting state (any, not all). Worked example: an NFA with states {q0,q1} where q0 on 'a' nondeterministically goes to both q0 and q1. Reachable DFA states end up being subsets like {q0} and {q0,q1}, up to a maximum of 2^2 = 4 possible subsets (including the empty set, which serves as the DFA's dead/trap state when no NFA state is reachable). In practice only the subsets actually reachable from the start are built ("lazy" subset construction), so real DFAs are usually much smaller than the full 2^n bound; the bound is a worst case, achieved by specially constructed languages, not typical.

State equivalence & DFA minimization

Two DFA states are equivalent if, for every possible remaining input string, continuing from either one leads to the same accept-or-reject verdict; minimization merges every class of equivalent states into a single state.

Key facts & formulas
  • p ~ q iff no string distinguishes them across ALL possible continuations, not just one
  • Step 1 of minimization: remove states unreachable from the start state
  • Step 2: initial partition is F versus Q-F, since accepting/non-accepting states are never equivalent
  • Refine partitions by transitions until no group splits further; merge each stable group
  • The Myhill-Nerode theorem guarantees a unique minimum DFA (up to state renaming) for each regular language
  • This standard algorithm applies to DFAs; NFA minimization is a different, harder problem with no unique answer
Traps
  • Skipping the unreachable-state removal step, which can leave the DFA under-minimized
  • Checking only one or two sample strings to decide equivalence — the definition requires ALL strings
  • Assuming the same table-filling method minimizes an NFA the same way it minimizes a DFA
Full explanation

Formally, states p and q are equivalent (p ~ q) iff for ALL strings w in Sigma*, delta-hat(p,w) is in F exactly when delta-hat(q,w) is in F — this must hold for every possible continuation, not just one convenient string. The standard algorithm (table-filling / partition refinement) works from a Myhill-Nerode style argument: (1) remove states unreachable from q0, since minimization is only meaningful over reachable states; (2) start with two groups, F and Q-F, since an accepting and a non-accepting state can never be equivalent; (3) repeatedly split a group if some input symbol sends its members to states that are already known to be in different groups; (4) stop when no group splits further — the surviving groups are the equivalence classes. Merge each class into one state to get the minimum DFA. The Myhill-Nerode theorem guarantees this minimum DFA is unique for a given regular language, up to renaming states. Note this table-filling algorithm is specifically for DFAs; it does not directly minimize NFAs, whose minimum form is not even guaranteed unique.

Pumping lemma & limits of DFAs

The pumping lemma says every regular language has a length p such that any string of length at least p can be split into xyz with |xy| <= p, |y| >= 1, and xy^i z still in the language for every i >= 0; it is used only to prove a language is NOT regular.

Key facts & formulas
  • Pumping lemma applies to every regular language, with some pumping length p
  • Split constraints: |xy| <= p, |y| >= 1 (y nonempty), and xy^iz in L for all i >= 0
  • a^n b^n is the standard textbook example proved non-regular via pumping
  • A DFA cannot recognise palindromes of unbounded/arbitrary length — no finite memory to compare far-apart symbols
  • A DFA CAN recognise palindromes of one fixed, bounded length — that is a different, finite problem
  • Pumping lemma only proves non-regularity; it can never be used to prove a language IS regular
Traps
  • Trying to use the pumping lemma to prove a language regular — it is strictly a non-regularity tool
  • Picking a string for the proof that happens to still pump correctly, producing a false "proof"
  • Reading "can a DFA recognise a palindrome" without noting whether length is bounded or arbitrary — arbitrary length is the intended, answer-is-no case in this exam's phrasing
Full explanation

Because a DFA has only finitely many states, reading a long enough string must revisit some state — that repeated loop is the "y" piece, and looping it any number of times (i = 0, 1, 2, ...) must still be accepted, since the automaton cannot distinguish how many times it went around the loop. Using it as a proof: assume the language IS regular, obtain its pumping length p, choose an adversarial string of length >= p, then show that EVERY valid way of splitting it into xyz fails to keep xy^iz in the language for some i — this contradiction proves the language is not regular. Two classic exam examples: a^n b^n is not regular, because pumping the a-block (or b-block) alone breaks the equal-count requirement; and palindromes of unbounded length are not regular, because a finite-state machine has no way to remember an arbitrarily long prefix to compare it against the suffix later — this is exactly why a DFA cannot recognise general palindromes. A DFA CAN recognise palindromes of one fixed, bounded length, since that only needs finitely many cases; the impossibility is specific to unbounded length. The lemma is a one-way tool: satisfying the pumping property does not prove regularity.

Regular expressions

A regular expression is an algebraic notation over union, concatenation, and Kleene star that describes exactly the class of regular languages, the same class recognised by DFAs and NFAs.

Key facts & formulas
  • Base regular expressions: empty language, epsilon, and each single symbol
  • Operators: union (+), concatenation, Kleene star; precedence is star > concatenation > union
  • Kleene's theorem: regular expressions, DFAs, and NFAs define exactly the same language class
  • Regular expressions denote Type 3 (regular) languages in the Chomsky hierarchy
  • Regular expressions cannot express a^n b^n or balanced parentheses — those need a stack (context-free)
  • Practical programming-language regex (with backreferences/lookahead) is strictly more powerful than the formal definition
Traps
  • "Regular expressions are Type __ language" — the answer is Type 3, a very commonly repeated MCQ; do not answer Type 2
  • Assuming engine regex (PCRE-style, with backreferences) is equivalent to formal regular expressions — it is not, it's more powerful
  • Misapplying precedence, e.g. treating ab* as (ab)* instead of the correct a(b*)
Full explanation

Base cases: the empty language, epsilon, and each symbol a in Sigma are themselves regular expressions. Compound expressions are built with union (written + or |), concatenation (juxtaposition), and Kleene star (*), with precedence star highest, then concatenation, then union — so ab* means a(b*), not (ab)*. Kleene's theorem is the key result: regular expressions, DFAs, and NFAs all define precisely the same language class, the regular languages; conversion works both ways — Thompson's construction turns a regex into an NFA-epsilon, and state elimination turns a finite automaton back into a regex. This three-way equivalence (regex = automaton = Type 3 grammar) is the reason a very common exam fact is stated as: regular expressions denote Type 3 languages in the Chomsky hierarchy, not Type 2. Example: (0+1)*011 denotes all binary strings that end in 011. Regular expressions cannot express languages that need unbounded counting or matching, such as a^n b^n or balanced parentheses — those require a stack, i.e. they are context-free, one level up the hierarchy. Note that practical regex engines in programming languages (with backreferences, lookahead) are strictly more powerful than this formal definition.

Moore machine

RememberMoore's output is a badge worn by the State; it doesn't care what input just arrived.

A Moore machine is a finite-state transducer whose output is attached to each state, so the output depends only on the current state, never directly on the input symbol being read.

Key facts & formulas
  • 6-tuple: (Q, Sigma, Delta, delta, lambda, q0), with lambda: Q -> Delta
  • Output is a function of the current state only — never the input symbol
  • Output string length is input length + 1, because the start state emits before any input is read
  • Output changes only when the state changes — glitch-free with respect to input timing
  • Every Moore machine has an equivalent Mealy machine, and vice versa
Traps
  • "The output of a Moore machine depends on ___" — answer: the present state only, never the input symbol
  • Forgetting the output-length-is-n+1 rule when tracing a Moore machine example by hand
  • Swapping Moore and Mealy under exam pressure — Moore ties output to state, Mealy ties output to transition
Full explanation

Formally a 6-tuple (Q, Sigma, Delta, delta, lambda, q0), where Delta is the output alphabet, delta: Q x Sigma -> Q is the usual transition function, and lambda: Q -> Delta is the output function that assigns one output symbol to each state. Because the start state q0 itself emits an output before any input symbol is even read, the output string produced for an input of length n has length n+1 — one output per state visited, including the initial one. Contrast this immediately with the Mealy machine (next card), where output rides on the transition rather than the state. Since output is fixed per state, a Moore machine's output only changes exactly when the state changes, so there is no possibility of an output glitch mid-transition — this stability is Moore's main practical advantage, at the cost of sometimes needing more states than an equivalent Mealy design (to represent the same combination of "which state" and "what output should show" separately for different incoming paths). Every Moore machine has an equivalent Mealy machine and vice versa; recognition power over inputs alone (ignoring output) is the same as a finite automaton.

Mealy machine & Moore equivalence

RememberMealy's output rides the Move (the edge); Moore's output sits on the state, one extra output overall.

A Mealy machine is a finite-state transducer whose output is produced on each transition, depending on both the current state and the input symbol just read, so its output string has the same length as its input.

Key facts & formulas
  • Mealy output function: lambda: Q x Sigma -> Delta, depends on state AND input symbol
  • Mealy output string length equals input string length exactly, unlike Moore's n+1
  • Mealy transition diagrams label each edge "input/output"
  • Moore -> Mealy conversion never increases the number of states
  • Mealy -> Moore conversion can increase state count, since states with different incoming outputs must split
  • Neither Mealy nor Moore adds recognising power beyond a finite automaton — outputs are separate from acceptance
Traps
  • "Output of Mealy machine can be represented as ___" — answer: a function of present state and present input, i.e. lambda(q, a)
  • Assuming both conversion directions preserve state count — only Moore-to-Mealy is guaranteed not to increase it
  • Mixing up output-string-length rules: Mealy = same length as input, Moore = one longer
Full explanation

Formally a 6-tuple (Q, Sigma, Delta, delta, lambda, q0), but here lambda: Q x Sigma -> Delta labels each edge, typically drawn as "input/output" on the transition arrow. Because exactly one output symbol is produced per input symbol consumed, the output length equals the input length exactly — unlike a Moore machine's n+1. Conversion Moore -> Mealy: for every transition into a state, use that state's Moore output as the Mealy output on the incoming edge; this never increases the number of states. Conversion Mealy -> Moore is the harder direction: a state that is reached by edges carrying different outputs must be split into separate copies, one per distinct output, so the state count can increase. The two machine types are equivalent in the transducer sense (any behaviour one realises the other can too, once you account for the one-step output alignment difference), and neither is more powerful than a DFA for pure language recognition — outputs are an add-on, not extra recognising power. A very commonly tested phrasing: the output of a Mealy machine can be represented as a function of the present state and present input, i.e. lambda(state, input).

  4. 4
    The regular expression 0*(10)* denotes the same set as
    1. A(1*0)*1*
    2. B0 + (0 + 10)*
    3. C(0 + 1)*10(0 +1)*
    4. DNone of the above
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    0*(10)* only ever produces a 1 immediately followed by exactly one 0, so it can never generate two 1's in a row (e.g. '11') or a lone '0' block appearing after the '(10)*' run has started (e.g. '0100'). That rules out (a) (1*0)*1*, which is the classical identity for the set of ALL binary strings — a string containing '11' is in (a) but not in this language. It also rules out (b) 0 + (0 + 10)*, which, unlike this expression, permits a lone '0' block interleaved with '10' blocks. And it rules out (c) (0+1)*10(0+1)*, since a plain string of only 0's contains no '10' substring at all. No listed option matches the literal expression as printed in the source paper, so the answer is (d), none of the above.

    Only the Paper-I-syllabus questions from this paper were imported (104 of 149). Stored answer was (a); corrected to (d) by the 2026-09-02 answer-key audit, upheld by two independent adjudicators.

  13. 13
    An FSM (Finite State Machine) can be considered to be a TM (Turing Machine)
    1. Aof finite tape length, without rewinding capability and unidirectional tape movement
    2. Bof finite tape length, rewinding capability and unidirectional tape movement
    3. Cof finite tape length, without rewinding capability and bidirectional tape movement
    4. Dof finite tape length, rewinding capability and bi-directional tape movement
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    A simple FSM reads its input once, left to right, with no ability to move back or reread — equivalent to a Turing machine with a finite tape, no rewind, and unidirectional movement.

  36. 36
    If NFA of 6 states excluding the initial state is converted into DFA, maximum possible number of states for the DFA is ?contested key
    1. A64
    2. B32
    3. C128
    4. D127
    Show answer
    Do not memorise this answer

    The key says 64 (2⁶), but the stem says 6 states excluding the initial state — 7 states in all, so the subset-construction bound is 2⁷ = 128, which is offered as option C. The stored explanation itself says 2^(number of states). Learn the rule (2^k for k total NFA states), not this question's letter.

    Bank's answer: A

    Subset construction can, in the worst case, produce one DFA state for every subset of the NFA's total state set, i.e. 2^(number of states) states.

    If the NFA has 6 states 'excluding the initial state,' the total distinct states (including the initial one) would be 7, giving a worst case of 2^7=128 (option C) rather than 2^6=64 (the marked answer, option A) — the question's wording is ambiguous about whether 6 already includes all states.
