Formal Grammars and the Chomsky Hierarchy

The Type 0–3 ↔ machine ↔ language table is worth memorising cold; it alone answers most questions in this cluster.

6 concept cards24 past questions

Formal grammar: the 4-tuple

A formal grammar is a 4-tuple G = (V, T, P, S) that generates a language by repeatedly rewriting a string of symbols using production rules, starting from a designated start symbol.

Key facts & formulas
  • Exactly four components: (V, T, P, S)
  • V (nonterminals) and T (terminals) are always disjoint finite sets
  • P is a finite set of production rules, each of the form alpha -> beta
  • S is a nonterminal in V, the designated start symbol of every derivation
  • L(G) = { w in T* : S derives w in zero or more steps } — the language is a result, not part of the tuple
  • Alternative notation (N, Sigma, P, S) means exactly the same structure as (V, T, P, S)
Traps
  • "Which is NOT part of the grammar's 4-tuple" — a common wrong-answer bait is inserting "the language L"; L is generated by G, it is not one of the four components
  • Forgetting V and T must be disjoint — a symbol cannot be both a terminal and a nonterminal
  • Assuming S can be used only once in a derivation — recursive productions can reintroduce S or other nonterminals repeatedly
Full explanation

V is the finite set of nonterminal (variable) symbols, T is the finite set of terminal symbols, and V and T are always disjoint. P is a finite set of production rules of the form alpha -> beta, each rule saying a piece of the current string matching alpha may be rewritten as beta. S is a distinguished nonterminal in V, the start (sentence) symbol from which every derivation begins. A derivation applies rules from P one at a time: write => for a single rewrite step and =>* for the reflexive-transitive closure (zero or more steps). The language generated is L(G) = { w in T* : S =>* w } — every terminal string reachable from S by some sequence of rule applications. Note some textbooks use the notation G = (N, Sigma, P, S), with N for nonterminals and Sigma for terminals — same four components, different letters, so do not be thrown by a different textbook's symbol choice. The generated language L is a consequence of the grammar, not a fifth component of the tuple itself.

Chomsky hierarchy overview

RememberTM - LBA - PDA - FA for Types 0 - 1 - 2 - 3, in that order.

The Chomsky hierarchy classifies grammars, and the languages they generate, into four strictly nested types — Type 0 (unrestricted) down to Type 3 (regular) — each type recognised by a matching kind of automaton.

Key facts & formulas
  • Type 0 = unrestricted grammar, recognised by a Turing machine
  • Type 1 = context-sensitive grammar, recognised by a linear bounded automaton
  • Type 2 = context-free grammar, recognised by a pushdown automaton
  • Type 3 = regular grammar, recognised by a finite automaton
  • Strict nesting: regular subset of context-free subset of context-sensitive subset of recursively enumerable
  • Lower type numbers (toward 3) mean MORE restricted grammars and SMALLER language classes, not the reverse
Traps
  • Inverting the direction — Type 0 is the least restricted and most powerful, Type 3 the most restricted and least powerful; easy to flip under pressure
  • Assigning context-sensitive grammars to a pushdown automaton — that pairing belongs to context-free (Type 2), not Type 1
  • Treating the type-to-automaton pairing as something to reason out live in the exam rather than a fixed table to memorise cold
Full explanation

The pairing to memorise as a fixed table: Type 0, unrestricted grammar, recognised by a Turing machine; Type 1, context-sensitive grammar, recognised by a linear bounded automaton; Type 2, context-free grammar, recognised by a pushdown automaton; Type 3, regular grammar, recognised by a finite automaton. The classes nest strictly: regular (Type 3) is a proper subset of context-free (Type 2), which is a proper subset of context-sensitive (Type 1), which is a proper subset of recursively enumerable / Type 0. Going from Type 0 down to Type 3, the productions allowed become progressively MORE restricted, and correspondingly the languages that can be generated become a smaller, simpler class: Type 0 productions have essentially no shape restriction (only that the left side contains at least one nonterminal); Type 1 requires the right side to be at least as long as the left side (non-contracting); Type 2 requires the left side to be a single nonterminal; Type 3 further restricts the right side to a very limited linear form. This card is the map — each type gets its own dedicated card with the exact production shapes.

Type 3 regular grammars & FA link

A Type 3 (regular) grammar restricts every production to a single nonterminal on the left, and on the right to either one terminal alone, or one terminal paired with at most one nonterminal in a fixed position (right-linear or left-linear).

Key facts & formulas
  • Right-linear production form: A -> aB or A -> a (nonterminal, if any, comes after the terminal)
  • Left-linear production form: A -> Ba or A -> a (nonterminal, if any, comes before the terminal)
  • Regular grammars, finite automata, and regular expressions all define the same language class
  • Grammar-to-NFA conversion: nonterminals become states, A -> aB becomes an a-labelled transition A to B
  • A language is regular if and only if some Type 3 grammar generates it
  • Mixing left-linear and right-linear rules in one grammar can produce a non-regular language
Traps
  • Assuming any grammar with short right-hand sides is automatically regular — the position and count of the nonterminal on the right must match the right-linear or left-linear pattern exactly
  • Treating a mixed left/right-linear grammar as regular by default — consistency of form is required
  • Forgetting the terminal-only production A -> a needed to actually terminate a derivation and reach an accepting state
Full explanation

Right-linear form: A -> aB or A -> a (the nonterminal, if present, always comes last, after the terminal). Left-linear form: A -> Ba or A -> a (the nonterminal, if present, always comes first). A grammar must be consistently one form or the other; freely mixing left-linear and right-linear rules in the same grammar can generate a language that is not regular, so "short productions" alone do not guarantee regularity. The formal bridge to finite automata: every regular grammar converts mechanically into an NFA — each nonterminal becomes a state, a rule A -> aB becomes a transition from state A to state B on symbol a, and a rule A -> a becomes a transition to a (new or existing) accepting state — and the reverse conversion works the same way from a DFA's transitions back into productions. Together with regular expressions (via Kleene's theorem), this gives three equivalent views of exactly the same language class: regular grammar, regular expression, and finite automaton. A language is regular precisely when some Type 3 grammar can generate it.

Type 2 CFG, parse trees & ambiguity

A Type 2 (context-free) grammar allows any production A -> beta where A is a single nonterminal and beta is any string of terminals and nonterminals, applied regardless of what surrounds A — hence "context-free."

Key facts & formulas
  • CFG production form: A -> beta, with A a single nonterminal and beta any string over nonterminals and terminals
  • Parse tree leaves, read left to right, give the derived (yielded) string
  • A string is ambiguous in a grammar if it has two or more distinct parse trees / leftmost derivations
  • Whether an arbitrary CFG is ambiguous is undecidable in general — no algorithm always answers it
  • CFGs are recognised by pushdown automata
  • Classic ambiguous example: E -> E+E | E*E | id gives two parse trees for id+id*id, since precedence is undefined
Traps
  • Confusing "this grammar is ambiguous" with "this language is inherently ambiguous" — some languages have both ambiguous and unambiguous grammars for them
  • Assuming every CFG can be rewritten to remove ambiguity — some languages are inherently ambiguous, with no unambiguous grammar at all
  • Finding one leftmost derivation and concluding the grammar is unambiguous without checking whether a second, different leftmost derivation also exists
Full explanation

A parse tree (derivation tree) has the start symbol as its root, nonterminals as internal nodes, and terminals as leaves; reading the leaves left to right gives the string the tree derives. A leftmost derivation always expands the leftmost remaining nonterminal first; a rightmost derivation expands the rightmost first — both are just orderings of the same rule applications. A grammar is ambiguous if some string in its language has two or more distinct parse trees, equivalently two distinct leftmost derivations. The textbook example is E -> E+E | E*E | id for the string id+id*id: one parse tree groups it as (id+id)*id and another as id+(id*id), giving genuinely different meanings from the same grammar, because the grammar itself encodes no precedence or associativity. Ambiguity is a real practical problem for language and compiler design, since it leaves meaning undefined until some external rule (precedence table) is imposed, and deciding whether an arbitrary CFG is ambiguous is undecidable in general — there is no algorithm that always answers this question. CFGs are recognised by pushdown automata and can express nested, recursive structures like balanced parentheses that regular grammars cannot.

PDA: deterministic vs nondeterministic

A pushdown automaton (PDA) is a finite automaton augmented with an unbounded stack, and it recognises exactly the context-free languages; a deterministic PDA (DPDA) is strictly weaker than a nondeterministic PDA (NPDA).

Key facts & formulas
  • PDA = finite control plus one unbounded stack; recognises exactly the context-free languages
  • PDA acceptance can be defined by final state or by empty stack — the two definitions are equivalent in power
  • DPDA is STRICTLY weaker than NPDA — deterministic context-free languages (DCFL) are a proper subset of context-free languages
  • ww^R (even palindromes, no centre marker) is a standard example an NPDA accepts but no DPDA can
  • a^n b^n IS accepted by a DPDA, since no guessing about the midpoint is required
  • DCFLs correspond to languages parsed by LR-style parsers, relevant to compiler design
Traps
  • Assuming "PDA = DFA + stack" means determinism costs nothing here too — unlike DFA/NFA, DPDA really is weaker than NPDA
  • Picking a DPDA when a question asks for a machine that accepts an arbitrary/general context-free language — only an NPDA is guaranteed sufficient
  • Lumping all stack-based languages together as equally hard — a^n b^n is DPDA-recognisable, while ww^R without a marker is not
Full explanation

A PDA's transition reads the current state, the current input symbol (or epsilon), and the symbol on top of the stack, and produces a new state plus a stack update (push, pop, or replace the top symbol). The stack gives genuinely unbounded memory, unlike a DFA's fixed set of states, which is exactly what is needed to match nested or unbounded-depth structure such as balanced parentheses or a^n b^n (push on a's, pop on b's, accept if the stack empties exactly when input ends). Acceptance can be defined either by ending in a final state or by ending with an empty stack — the two definitions are proven equivalent in power. The critical exam distinction: an NPDA accepts every context-free language, but a DPDA accepts only a proper subset of them, called the deterministic context-free languages (DCFL); so DPDA is strictly weaker than NPDA, unlike the DFA/NFA case where determinism costs nothing. The standard example separating them is ww^R (even-length palindromes with no centre marker) over {0,1}: a nondeterministic machine can guess where the midpoint is and start popping, but a deterministic machine has no way to know when to switch from pushing to popping without a marker, so no DPDA can accept it. DCFLs correspond to the languages parseable by LR-style parsers, which is why compilers care about this distinction.

Type 1/0 grammars & closure properties

A Type 1 (context-sensitive) grammar restricts productions to be non-contracting and is recognised by a linear bounded automaton, while a Type 0 (unrestricted) grammar has no such restriction and is recognised by a full Turing machine.

Key facts & formulas
  • Context-sensitive productions are non-contracting: |alpha| <= |beta|, with S -> epsilon allowed only if S never appears on any right-hand side
  • Context-sensitive grammar is recognised by a linear bounded automaton (LBA) — tape length is bounded by input length
  • Type 0 (unrestricted) grammars are recognised by a full Turing machine, and generate the recursively enumerable languages
  • Context-free languages ARE closed under union, concatenation, and Kleene star
  • Context-free languages are NOT closed under intersection or complement
  • Regular languages ARE closed under union, intersection, complement, concatenation, and Kleene star
Traps
  • "Context Sensitive Grammar can be recognized by a ___" — answer: linear bounded automaton, not Turing machine (that pairing is Type 0)
  • "Context Free Grammar is not closed under ___" — answer: intersection and complement, a very frequently repeated question
  • Assuming CFLs are closed under intersection because regular languages are — false; CFL intersect CFL need not be context-free (e.g. constructions resembling a^n b^n c^n)
Full explanation

The commonly tested, simplified definition of a context-sensitive production is non-contracting: |alpha| <= |beta| for every rule alpha -> beta, meaning the string never gets shorter during a derivation (one exception is allowed: S -> epsilon, only if S never appears on the right side of any rule). A linear bounded automaton (LBA) is a Turing machine restricted to use only the tape space occupied by the input itself, no extra tape — and this exact pairing, "context-sensitive grammar is recognised by a linear bounded automaton," is a heavily repeated exam fact. Type 0 grammars drop every restriction on production shape (only requiring at least one nonterminal on the left) and are recognised by an unrestricted Turing machine; the languages they generate are exactly the recursively enumerable languages, where a machine may run forever without halting on a non-member string. Closure properties, another favourite exam area: regular languages are closed under union, intersection, complement, concatenation, and Kleene star. Context-free languages ARE closed under union, concatenation, and Kleene star, but are NOT closed under intersection or complement — a classic counterexample uses two CFLs whose intersection encodes a language like a^n b^n c^n, which is not context-free.

    4. DContext-free languages

    Answer: C

    The class of languages accepted by finite automata is exactly the regular languages, by Kleene's theorem.

    1. An

    3. C2n
    4. D2n
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    In CNF every rule is A→BC or A→a, so deriving a string of length n needs n terminal-producing steps and n-1 binary-branching steps (a binary tree with n leaves has n-1 internal nodes), for a total of 2n-1 steps.

Fuzzy Sets

Small, self-contained, and reliably examined — cheap marks. max/min for union/intersection, 1−μ for complement.

1 concept cards15 past questions

Fuzzy sets — membership & properties

RememberCore = complete (mu = 1); Support = some (mu > 0).

A fuzzy set assigns every element a graded degree of membership between 0 and 1 via a membership function, rather than the strict all-or-nothing (0 or 1) membership of an ordinary crisp set.

Key facts & formulas
  • Membership function mu_A(x) is in [0,1]; a crisp set restricts this to exactly {0,1}
  • Support = {x : mu_A(x) > 0}; core = {x : mu_A(x) = 1} (core may be empty)
  • Height h(A) = supremum of mu_A(x) over all x in the universe
  • A fuzzy set A is called normal when h(A) = 1, and subnormal when h(A) < 1
  • Alpha-cut A_alpha = {x : mu_A(x) >= alpha} is a crisp set; larger alpha gives a smaller (nested) cut
  • Standard (Zadeh) fuzzy union uses max, intersection uses min, complement is 1 - mu_A(x)
Traps
  • "The fuzzy set A is called normal when h(A) = ___" — answer is 1, not 0 and not 0.5
  • Confusing support (mu > 0, can be large) with core (mu = 1 exactly, can even be empty for a subnormal set)
  • Assuming fuzzy union/intersection use sum or product of memberships — the standard definition uses max/min, not arithmetic operations that could exceed 1
  • Treating fuzzy memberships like a probability distribution — they need not sum to 1 across all elements
Full explanation

A fuzzy set A over a universe X is defined by its membership function mu_A: X -> [0,1], giving each x a degree of belonging rather than a binary yes/no; a crisp set is the special case where mu(x) is restricted to exactly {0,1}. Key structural terms: the support of A is {x in X : mu_A(x) > 0}, every element with any nonzero membership at all; the core of A is {x in X : mu_A(x) = 1}, elements with full membership (the core can be empty). The height h(A) is the supremum of mu_A(x) over all x; A is called a normal fuzzy set when h(A) = 1 (at least one element attains full membership), and subnormal when h(A) < 1. An alpha-cut A_alpha = {x : mu_A(x) >= alpha} is a crisp set obtained by thresholding at level alpha; as alpha increases, A_alpha shrinks (nested, monotonic). Basic operations mirror ordinary set theory but use max/min in place of union/intersection: mu_(A union B)(x) = max(mu_A(x), mu_B(x)); mu_(A intersect B)(x) = min(mu_A(x), mu_B(x)); mu_(complement of A)(x) = 1 - mu_A(x). Example: a "tall people" fuzzy set might give a person of 5'9" a membership of 0.6 rather than a hard yes or no.

Past questions from this topic (15)
  1. 1
    In classical (crisp) set theory, an element's membership in a set is restricted to {0, 1}. In fuzzy set theory, the membership function μA(x) instead takes values in:
    1. A{0, 1} only, exactly as in crisp sets
    2. BThe closed interval [0, 1], any real number between 0 and 1 inclusive
    3. CThe interval [-1, 1]
    4. DAny positive integer
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    This is the foundational idea of fuzzy sets, introduced by Lotfi Zadeh: instead of an element being strictly in or out of a set, it can belong partially, with a membership grade anywhere in [0, 1]. A grade of 0 means not a member at all, 1 means full membership, and values in between (e.g., 0.7) express degrees of belonging — useful for modeling vague concepts like 'tall' or 'hot' that don't have sharp boundaries.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_1

  2. 2
    In a Mamdani-type fuzzy inference system used for approximate reasoning, the typical processing stages occur in which order?
    1. ADefuzzification, then fuzzification, then rule evaluation
    2. BFuzzification, then rule evaluation (inference), then aggregation of rule outputs, then defuzzification
    3. CRule evaluation, then fuzzification, then aggregation, with no final output step
    4. DOnly fuzzification is needed; no defuzzification step is ever required
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A Mamdani fuzzy inference system first fuzzifies crisp numeric inputs into fuzzy membership grades, then evaluates each if-then rule against those grades (inference), then aggregates the fuzzy outputs of all fired rules into one combined fuzzy output set, and finally defuzzifies that combined set (e.g., via the centroid method) back into a single crisp output number that a real system can act on.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_10

  3. 3
    A fuzzy set A defined on a universe of discourse X is formally represented as:
    1. AA = {x | x ∈ X}
    2. BA = {(x, μA(x)) | x ∈ X, μA(x) ∈ [0,1]}
    3. CA = {x | μA(x) = 1}
    4. DA set defined only by a sharp, crisp boundary
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A fuzzy set is defined as a collection of ordered pairs, each consisting of an element x from the universe and its corresponding membership grade μA(x) in [0, 1]. Option C would only capture the elements with full (crisp) membership, discarding all the partial-membership information that makes the set 'fuzzy' in the first place, and option D describes a classical crisp set, the opposite of a fuzzy one.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_2

  4. 4
    For two fuzzy sets A and B on the same universe X, the standard Zadeh definition of the union A ∪ B has membership function:
    1. AμA∪B(x) = μA(x) + μB(x)
    2. BμA∪B(x) = min(μA(x), μB(x))
    3. CμA∪B(x) = max(μA(x), μB(x))
    4. DμA∪B(x) = μA(x) · μB(x)
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Zadeh's standard fuzzy union takes the maximum of the two membership grades at each point, mirroring how a classical union includes an element if it belongs to either set. Simple addition (option A) can push values above 1, which is invalid since membership must stay within [0, 1], so max is used instead. Min (option B) is instead the standard definition for intersection, not union.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_3

  5. 5
    For two fuzzy sets A and B on the same universe X, the standard Zadeh definition of the intersection A ∩ B has membership function:
    1. AμA∩B(x) = max(μA(x), μB(x))
    2. BμA∩B(x) = min(μA(x), μB(x))
    3. CμA∩B(x) = μA(x) + μB(x) - μA(x)·μB(x)
    4. DμA∩B(x) = 1 - μA(x)
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Zadeh's standard fuzzy intersection takes the minimum of the two membership grades, mirroring how classical intersection requires membership in both sets — an element's combined degree of belonging can be no higher than its weakest membership in either set. Option D describes the complement operation, not intersection, and option C is an alternative ('algebraic sum') formula sometimes used for union in other fuzzy logic families, not the standard Zadeh intersection.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_4

  6. 6
    The standard Zadeh complement of a fuzzy set A, denoted A', has membership function:
    1. AμA'(x) = 1 - μA(x)
    2. BμA'(x) = 1 / μA(x)
    3. CμA'(x) = μA(x) - 1
    4. DμA'(x) = -μA(x)
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    The fuzzy complement subtracts an element's membership grade from 1, so that full membership (1) becomes zero membership in the complement, and vice versa, while a grade of exactly 0.5 remains 0.5 in the complement (perfectly ambiguous). This mirrors the classical set complement (an element not in A) while keeping the result within the valid [0, 1] range, unlike the other options, which can produce values outside [0, 1] or undefined results.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_5

  7. 7
    Let fuzzy sets A = {(x1, 0.3), (x2, 0.6), (x3, 0.9)} and B = {(x1, 0.5), (x2, 0.4), (x3, 1.0)} be defined on the same universe. Using the standard Zadeh union, what is the membership value of x2 in A ∪ B?
    1. A1.0
    2. B0.6
    3. C0.4
    4. D0.24
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    The Zadeh union takes the maximum membership grade at each point: μA∪B(x2) = max(μA(x2), μB(x2)) = max(0.6, 0.4) = 0.6. Option C (0.4) would be the result if you mistakenly applied intersection (min) instead of union, and option D (0.24) would result from multiplying the two grades, which is not the standard union operator.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_6

  8. 8
    The α-cut (or α-level set) of a fuzzy set A, denoted Aα, is defined as:
    1. AThe set of all x ∈ X such that μA(x) ≥ α — a crisp (ordinary) subset of X
    2. BThe set of all x such that μA(x) equals α exactly
    3. CThe complement of A scaled by the factor α
    4. DA fuzzy set with membership function α · μA(x)
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    An α-cut converts a fuzzy set into an ordinary crisp set by keeping exactly those elements whose membership grade meets or exceeds the threshold α, and discarding membership-grade information beyond that yes/no cutoff. This is a key bridge between fuzzy and crisp set theory: as α increases from 0 to 1, the α-cuts shrink, and any fuzzy set can be fully reconstructed from the nested family of all its α-cuts.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_7

  9. 9
    A fuzzy relation R between two universes X and Y is best defined as:
    1. AA crisp (ordinary) subset of the Cartesian product X × Y
    2. BA fuzzy subset of X × Y, i.e., a mapping μR: X × Y → [0, 1] assigning a degree of relatedness to each pair (x, y)
    3. CA function that maps every element of X to exactly one element of Y with no membership grades involved
    4. DDefined only when X and Y are the same universe
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Just as a fuzzy set generalizes a crisp set by allowing partial membership, a fuzzy relation generalizes a crisp relation by allowing each pair (x, y) to be related to a degree in [0, 1] rather than a strict yes/no. This is used to model statements like 'x is much larger than y' where 'much larger' is itself a matter of degree, and it applies equally whether X and Y are the same or different universes.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_8

  10. 10
    Which statement correctly distinguishes fuzzy logic from classical (Boolean) two-valued logic?
    1. AFuzzy logic allows only truth values of 0 or 1, exactly like Boolean logic
    2. BFuzzy logic allows a continuum of truth values between 0 (completely false) and 1 (completely true), enabling reasoning about degrees of truth and vagueness rather than only crisp true/false
    3. CFuzzy logic cannot represent linguistic terms such as 'warm' or 'tall'
    4. DFuzzy logic is simply a subset of probability theory used only for modeling random events
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Fuzzy logic extends Boolean logic by allowing propositions to be partially true, which is exactly what makes it suitable for approximate reasoning about vague, linguistic concepts ('somewhat warm', 'fairly tall'). It is important to note fuzzy logic addresses vagueness/imprecision in meaning, not randomness/uncertainty about outcomes, which is what probability theory addresses — the two are philosophically distinct despite both using numbers in [0, 1].

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_FUZZY_9

    1. Ah(A)=0
    2. Bh(A)<0
    3. Ch(A)=1
    4. Dh(A)<1
    Show answer

Group Theory (peripheral)

Off the printed syllabus but present in the legacy papers — learn the four axioms and move on.

0 concept cards5 past questions

Past questions from this topic (5)
  1. 1
    G {e, a, b, c} is an abelian group with 'e' as identity element. The order of the other elements arecontested key
    1. A2, 2, 3
    2. B3, 3, 3
    3. C2, 2, 4
    4. D2, 3, 4
    Show answer
    Do not memorise this answer

    No abelian group of order 4 has element orders 2, 2, 4: ℤ₄ gives 2, 4, 4 and the Klein four-group gives 2, 2, 2. Lagrange only rules out the options containing 3. The keyed multiset is not realisable.

    Bank's answer: C

    By Lagrange's theorem, every element's order must divide the group order 4, so orders can only be 1, 2, or 4 — ruling out any option containing a 3. Only (c) 2,2,4 is consistent with this, though it doesn't cleanly match either standard order-4 group (Z4 gives 2,4,4; the Klein four-group gives 2,2,2), so treat with some caution.

    Only the Paper-I-syllabus questions from this paper were imported (104 of 149).

    derived · mediumInspector of Legal Metrology, March 2010 · Paper IILM2010_P1_047

  2. 2
    Which of the following statement is FALSE ?
    1. AThe set of rational numbers is an abelian group under addition
    2. BThe set of rational integers is an abelian group under addition
    3. CThe set of rational numbers form an abelian group under multiplication
    4. DNone of these
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Rationals under multiplication fail to form a group because 0 has no multiplicative inverse; rationals and integers are fine abelian groups under addition, so (c) is the false statement.

    Only the Paper-I-syllabus questions from this paper were imported (104 of 149).

    derived · highInspector of Legal Metrology, March 2010 · Paper IILM2010_P1_048

  3. 3
    Which of the following statement is TRUE?
    1. AThe set of all rational negative numbers forms a group under multiplication
    2. BThe set of all non-singular matrices forms a group under multiplication
    3. CThe set of all matrices forms a group under multiplication
    4. DBoth (b) and (c) are true
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Non-singular (invertible) matrices of a fixed size form a group under multiplication (GL(n)): closed, has inverses, has identity I. Negative rationals aren't closed under multiplication (negative*negative=positive), and singular matrices lack inverses, so 'all matrices' isn't a group either.

    Only the Paper-I-syllabus questions from this paper were imported (104 of 149).

    derived · highInspector of Legal Metrology, March 2010 · Paper IILM2010_P1_049

  4. 4
    Which one of the following in NOT necessarily a property of a Group?
    1. ACommutativity
    2. BAssociativity
    3. CExistence of inverse for every element
    4. DExistence of identity
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    The group axioms require closure, associativity, an identity element and inverses for every element, but not commutativity -- a group without commutativity is simply called non-abelian.

  5. 5
    How many different non-isomorphic Abelian groups of order 4 are there?
    1. A2
    2. B3
    3. C4
    4. D5
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Up to isomorphism there are exactly 2 abelian groups of order 4: the cyclic group Z4 and the Klein four-group Z2×Z2.

Part 2 — Data Structures and Algorithms60 marks

Unit 3 of Technical Paper I.

Algorithm Analysis and Asymptotic Notation

Frame the whole unit: nearly every other cluster ends in a complexity question, so fix O / Ω / Θ and the growth ordering first.

7 concept cards14 past questions

Worked method

Reading complexity off a loop nest
  1. A loop running i from 1 to n with a constant-work body is O(n).
  2. Nested independent loops multiply: two nested n-loops give O(n²). Sequential loops add, and the larger term wins.
  3. A loop whose counter is multiplied or divided each iteration (i = i * 2, i = i / 2) runs O(log n) times.
  4. An inner loop bounded by the outer counter — for j = 1 to i — gives 1 + 2 + … + n = n(n+1)/2, which is O(n²).
  5. Drop constants and lower-order terms: 3n² + 5n + 7 is O(n²).
  6. For recursive code, write the recurrence and apply the master theorem: T(n) = aT(n/b) + f(n) compares f(n) against n^(log_b a).
Worked example

*for (i = 1; i <= n; i++) for (j = 1; j <= n; j = j * 2) …*

The outer loop runs n times; the inner loop doubles j, so it runs log₂ n times. Total: O(n log n).

Algorithm: definition & properties

RememberFDIOE: Finiteness, Definiteness, Input, Output, Effectiveness.

An algorithm is a finite, well-defined sequence of unambiguous steps that transforms given input into desired output in a finite amount of time.

Key facts & formulas
  • Five standard properties: finiteness, definiteness, input, output, effectiveness.
  • Finiteness distinguishes an algorithm from a general program or process (e.g. an OS loop, which need not terminate).
  • Definiteness requires each step to have exactly one clear, unambiguous meaning.
  • Effectiveness requires each operation to be basic/primitive enough to be carried out exactly and in finite time.
  • Input requirement allows zero or more inputs; output requirement mandates at least one output.
Traps
  • Claiming an algorithm needs "at least one input" — the standard definition permits zero inputs; only output is mandatory to be at least one.
  • Equating "algorithm" with "program" — every algorithm can be written as a program, but not every program is an algorithm, since programs (e.g. an OS) may run indefinitely and violate finiteness.
  • Treating efficiency as one of the five required properties — it is a desirable quality measured separately by complexity analysis, not part of the formal definition.
Full explanation

Standard texts list five properties every algorithm must satisfy. Finiteness: the algorithm must terminate after a finite number of steps — this is what separates an algorithm from a general process or program, since a program (such as an operating system's main loop) need not terminate. Definiteness: every step must be precisely and unambiguously specified, with exactly one interpretation, so two people following it reach the same result. Input: the algorithm takes zero or more well-specified inputs — zero is allowed, since some algorithms (e.g., "print hello") need none. Output: it produces one or more outputs that bear a defined relationship to the input — at least one output is mandatory. Effectiveness: every operation must be basic enough to be carried out exactly, in principle by hand with pencil and paper, in a finite amount of time — it cannot rely on an operation that is itself infeasible. These five properties are commonly tested directly, often as "which of the following is NOT a required property," with efficiency being the usual false option: efficiency is desirable and is what complexity analysis measures, but it is not one of the defining properties of an algorithm.

Time vs space complexity trade-off

Time complexity measures how an algorithm's running time grows with input size, space complexity measures how much extra memory it needs, and the two can often be traded against each other.

Key facts & formulas
  • Time complexity: growth rate of running time with input size n, typically stated with Big-O.
  • Space complexity: growth rate of memory used (usually auxiliary space, excluding the input itself) with n.
  • Space-time trade-off: extra memory (caching, precomputed tables, hashing) can reduce time, and vice versa.
  • Memoized recursive Fibonacci: O(n) time and O(n) extra space, versus naive recursive Fibonacci's O(2^n) time.
  • Space complexity of a recursive algorithm usually must include the call stack, not just explicit variables.
Traps
  • Reporting only time complexity for a recursive algorithm and forgetting the call stack's space cost — naive recursive Fibonacci uses O(n) stack space despite its exponential time.
  • Assuming more space automatically means faster time — some memory-heavy structures (e.g. an unbalanced tree) don't improve time complexity at all.
  • Treating "space complexity" as the memory needed to hold the input — it conventionally refers to EXTRA space used beyond the input.
Full explanation

Both complexities are typically expressed as functions of input size n and reported using asymptotic notation. Time complexity counts basic operations as a function of n; space complexity typically counts auxiliary space — memory used beyond the input itself, which for recursive algorithms includes the call stack. The space-time trade-off is the principle that an algorithm can often be made faster by using more memory (caching results, precomputed lookup tables, hashing) or made to use less memory at the cost of extra recomputation. The canonical teaching example is Fibonacci: the naive recursive version recomputes the same subproblems repeatedly, costing O(2^n) time while using only O(n) stack space; memoizing it (storing each computed Fibonacci value the first time it is needed) brings time down to O(n) at the cost of O(n) extra storage for the memo table. Another common instance: a hash table gives O(1) average-case lookup using extra memory for the hash structure, versus binary search on a sorted array giving O(log n) lookup with no extra structure beyond the array itself. Exam questions often ask you to state both complexities for the same algorithm, not just time.

Asymptotic notation: O, Omega, Theta

RememberO = at most, Omega = at least, Theta = exactly, o = strictly less than.

Asymptotic notation describes how a function grows as its input approaches infinity, ignoring constant factors and lower-order terms: Big-O gives an upper bound, Big-Omega a lower bound, Big-Theta a tight bound, and little-o a strict upper bound.

Key facts & formulas
  • Big-O: f(n) = O(g(n)) means f grows no faster than g (upper bound) — exists c, n0 such that f(n) <= c.g(n) for all n >= n0.
  • Big-Omega: f(n) = Omega(g(n)) means f grows at least as fast as g (lower bound) — exists c, n0 such that f(n) >= c.g(n) for all n >= n0.
  • Big-Theta: f(n) = Theta(g(n)) means f and g grow at the same rate (tight bound) — both O and Omega hold.
  • little-o: f(n) = o(g(n)) is a strict upper bound — holds for EVERY constant c, not just some c, meaning f grows strictly slower than g.
  • Example: 2n^2 + 3n + 1 is Theta(n^2), since it is both O(n^2) and Omega(n^2).
Traps
  • Using O() when Theta() is meant — casual usage of "O(n log n)" often really means Theta(n log n); the exam distinguishes them, and O alone only claims an upper bound.
  • Confusing the quantifiers — O, Omega, Theta each require only "there EXISTS a constant c", whereas little-o requires the bound to hold for EVERY constant c > 0.
  • Believing Big-Omega always describes best case and Big-O always describes worst case — bound type (O/Omega/Theta) and case type (best/average/worst) are orthogonal classifications of the same or different functions.
Full explanation

Formally, f(n) = O(g(n)) if there exist positive constants c and n0 such that 0 <= f(n) <= c.g(n) for all n >= n0 — f grows no faster than g. f(n) = Omega(g(n)) if there exist c, n0 such that 0 <= c.g(n) <= f(n) for all n >= n0 — f grows at least as fast as g. f(n) = Theta(g(n)) if both the O and Omega relationships hold (possibly with different constants) — f and g grow at the same rate. f(n) = o(g(n)) is stricter: for EVERY constant c > 0 (not just some c), there exists n0 such that f(n) < c.g(n) for all n >= n0, equivalent to the limit of f(n)/g(n) going to 0 as n approaches infinity — f grows strictly slower than g. Worked example: for f(n) = 3n^2 + 5n, we have f(n) = O(n^2), f(n) = Omega(n^2), and hence f(n) = Theta(n^2); also f(n) = o(n^3) since 3n^2+5n grows strictly slower than n^3. These definitions describe bounds on growth rate and are independent of whether the function being bounded represents a best, average, or worst case running time.

Best, average and worst case analysis

Best, average and worst case describe an algorithm's performance under the most favourable, typical, and least favourable inputs respectively; worst case is quoted most often because it gives a guaranteed upper bound regardless of the actual input.

Key facts & formulas
  • Best case: performance on the input requiring the least work (e.g., linear search finding the target on the first comparison, O(1)).
  • Worst case: performance on the input requiring the most work; gives an upper-bound guarantee (e.g., linear search target absent or last, O(n)).
  • Average case: expected performance under an assumed input distribution (often uniform), requiring probabilistic analysis.
  • Worst case is quoted most often because it is a guarantee independent of input distribution and is usually easier to derive exactly.
  • Quicksort: average/best case O(n log n), worst case O(n^2), triggered by consistently unbalanced partitioning (e.g. naive pivoting on sorted data).
Traps
  • Treating "worst case" and "Big-O" as synonyms — Big-O is a bound TYPE, while best/average/worst is a CASE; a Big-O bound can be stated for the best case too.
  • Forgetting that an average-case figure depends on an assumed probability distribution over inputs, which may not match real-world usage patterns.
  • Assuming quicksort's O(n^2) worst case is a rare theoretical corner case — a sorted or reverse-sorted array with naive first/last-element pivoting reliably triggers it, a standard exam scenario.
Full explanation

Take linear search for a value in an array of n elements as the standard teaching example: best case is O(1), when the target is found at the very first position; worst case is O(n), when the target is at the last position or absent entirely; average case, assuming the target is equally likely to be at any position, works out to about n/2 comparisons, still Theta(n). Quicksort is the other classic example: with balanced partitioning it runs in O(n log n) in both the best and average case, but with a consistently poor pivot choice (e.g., always picking the first element on an already-sorted array) it degrades to O(n^2) in the worst case. Worst case is the figure usually quoted in exams and in real-time or safety-critical system design because it is a guarantee that holds no matter what the input is, whereas average case requires assuming a probability distribution over inputs that may not reflect reality, and is often harder to derive rigorously. Best case is rarely useful as a design guarantee since it only describes the most convenient input, not a typical or guaranteed one.

Analysing loops for complexity

Analysing an algorithm's complexity from its code means counting how many times each loop body executes as a function of input size n, adding costs for sequential loops and multiplying costs for nested loops.

Key facts & formulas
  • Sequential loops: complexities add, and the sum is dominated by the largest term — two O(n) loops in sequence remain O(n).
  • Nested loops with independent bounds (both to n): multiply — O(n) x O(n) = O(n^2).
  • Triangular nested loop (inner bound depends on outer index, e.g. j from 1 to i): total iterations = n(n+1)/2 = Theta(n^2).
  • A loop whose control variable is multiplied/divided by a constant each iteration runs Theta(log n) times.
  • Connected components / BFS-DFS traversal on an adjacency LIST: O(n + e); the same traversal on an adjacency MATRIX: O(n^2) — the representation changes the complexity class.
Traps
  • Assuming any nested loop is automatically O(n^2) without checking whether the inner bound actually depends on n independently of the outer index.
  • Miscounting a triangular loop as O(n) instead of recognising the arithmetic series sums to Theta(n^2).
  • For "generate all connected components with n vertices, e edges" questions, defaulting to O(n^2) by mentally picturing nested loops over vertex pairs, instead of recognising that an adjacency-LIST-based BFS/DFS visits each edge and vertex once, giving O(n+e).
  • Confusing "number of loop iterations" with "total operations executed" when the loop body itself contains a further loop or a costly function call.
Full explanation

A single loop running from 1 to n with a constant-time body costs O(n). Two independent sequential loops, each O(n), still sum to O(n) overall, since asymptotic addition is dominated by the largest term. Nested loops where the inner loop always runs n times regardless of the outer index multiply: O(n) x O(n) = O(n^2), e.g. for(i=1..n) for(j=1..n) sum++ performs exactly n^2 increments, Theta(n^2). A triangular nested loop, where the inner bound depends on the outer index (for i=1..n, for j=1..i), still sums to 1+2+...+n = n(n+1)/2 = Theta(n^2) — same order as a full nested loop, a common exam trap. A loop whose control variable is multiplied or divided by a constant each iteration (i = i*2 while i < n) runs only Theta(log n) times, and nesting an O(n) loop inside such a log-n outer loop gives Theta(n log n), the pattern behind efficient sorting analysis. A key representation-dependent case: generating all connected components of a graph with n vertices and e edges. Traversing via BFS/DFS on an adjacency LIST visits each vertex and each edge once, giving O(n + e); the same task on an adjacency MATRIX costs O(n^2), since checking every possible neighbour of every vertex is unavoidable regardless of actual edge count.

Recurrence relations and the Master theorem

A recurrence relation expresses a recursive algorithm's running time in terms of its running time on smaller inputs, and the Master theorem gives a direct formula for solving common recurrences of the form T(n) = aT(n/b) + f(n).

Key facts & formulas
  • General divide-and-conquer recurrence: T(n) = aT(n/b) + f(n), where a = number of subproblems, n/b = size of each subproblem, f(n) = divide/combine cost.
  • The Master theorem compares f(n) against the watershed function n^(log_b a) to choose one of three cases.
  • Case 1 (f(n) grows slower): T(n) = Theta(n^(log_b a)).
  • Case 2 (f(n) grows at the same rate): T(n) = Theta(n^(log_b a) x log n) — this is merge sort's Theta(n log n).
  • Case 3 (f(n) grows faster, plus a regularity condition): T(n) = Theta(f(n)).
  • Binary search's T(n) = T(n/2) + Theta(1) solves to Theta(log n); merge sort's T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Theta(n) solves to Theta(n log n).
Traps
  • Applying the Master theorem to a recurrence that doesn't fit its exact form, e.g. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n log n, which falls between case 2 and case 3 and needs a different method (recursion-tree analysis or the Akra-Bazzi generalisation).
  • Ignoring the regularity condition required for case 3 — without it, the case-3 conclusion does not automatically follow.
  • Mixing up quicksort's recurrences — its balanced best/average case is T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Theta(n) (giving Theta(n log n)), while its unbalanced worst case is T(n) = T(n-1) + Theta(n) (giving Theta(n^2)).
  • Miscomputing log_b a as an integer when it isn't — e.g. T(n) = 3T(n/2) + Theta(n) gives log_2 3 (approximately 1.585), a non-integer exponent that is easy to round incorrectly.
Full explanation

Recurrences are read straight off recursive code: binary search halves its input each call, giving T(n) = T(n/2) + Theta(1); merge sort splits into two halves and does linear-time merging, giving T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Theta(n). The Master theorem solves T(n) = aT(n/b) + f(n), for a >= 1 and b > 1, by comparing f(n) to the watershed function n^(log_b a). Case 1: if f(n) grows polynomially slower than n^(log_b a), then T(n) = Theta(n^(log_b a)) — cost dominated by the recursive calls. Case 2: if f(n) = Theta(n^(log_b a)) (same growth rate), then T(n) = Theta(n^(log_b a) x log n). Case 3: if f(n) grows polynomially faster than n^(log_b a) and a regularity condition holds, then T(n) = Theta(f(n)) — cost dominated by the top-level work. Worked examples: merge sort, a=2, b=2, log_b a = 1, f(n) = Theta(n^1), matches case 2, giving Theta(n log n); binary search, a=1, b=2, log_b a = 0, f(n) = Theta(n^0) = Theta(1), also matches case 2, giving Theta(log n).

Divide and conquer strategy

RememberDivide, Conquer, Combine — DCC.

Divide and conquer solves a problem by splitting it into smaller instances of the same problem, solving those recursively, and combining their solutions.

Key facts & formulas
  • The three phases are divide, conquer (recurse), combine.
  • General recurrence: T(n) = a*T(n/b) + f(n), compared against n^(log_b a) by the Master theorem.
  • Merge sort, quick sort, binary search, Strassen's matrix multiplication and the closest-pair problem are standard divide-and-conquer examples.
  • Binary search is divide and conquer with only one sub-problem and no combine step.
  • Strassen's algorithm runs in about O(n^2.81) versus O(n^3) for the naive method.
  • Divide and conquer recursion consumes stack space proportional to the recursion depth, typically O(log n) when splits are balanced.
Traps
  • Divide and conquer is not dynamic programming. Divide and conquer sub-problems are disjoint; dynamic programming exists precisely because sub-problems overlap and are memoised.
  • It is not the greedy method either, which makes one locally optimal choice and never recurses on alternatives.
  • Balanced splitting is what gives log n depth. Quick sort is still divide and conquer when the split is 1 : n-1, but the depth becomes n and the cost O(n^2).
  • The Master theorem does not cover every recurrence; the gap cases (for example f(n) = n log n against n^(log_b a) = n) fall between cases 2 and 3 and need another method.
  • Recursion by itself is not divide and conquer. Reducing n to n-1, as in naive factorial or in the T(n) = T(n-1) + n of quick sort's worst case, gives no logarithmic saving.
Full explanation

The three steps are divide, conquer and combine. Divide splits the input into sub-problems, conquer solves them recursively down to a base case small enough to solve directly, and combine assembles the sub-solutions. Which step does the real work varies: merge sort divides trivially and works in the combine (the merge); quick sort works in the divide (the partition) and combines for free.

Cost is expressed as a recurrence T(n) = a*T(n/b) + f(n), where a is the number of sub-problems, n/b their size, and f(n) the cost of dividing plus combining. The Master theorem compares f(n) with n^(log_b a): if f(n) is polynomially smaller the answer is Theta(n^(log_b a)); if they match, Theta(n^(log_b a) * log n); if f(n) is polynomially larger and satisfies the regularity condition, Theta(f(n)).

Worked cases. Merge sort: a = 2, b = 2, f(n) = Theta(n); n^(log2 2) = n matches f, so Theta(n log n). Binary search: a = 1, b = 2, f(n) = O(1); n^0 = 1 matches, so Theta(log n). Strassen's matrix multiplication: a = 7, b = 2, giving Theta(n^(log2 7)) which is about n^2.81, beating the naive n^3.

    In dynamic programming, the technique of storing the previously calculated values is called ___________
    1. ASaving value property
    2. BStoring value property
    3. CMemoization
    4. DMapping
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Memoization is the standard term for caching results of already-computed subproblems so each is solved once; the other three options are not real technical terms.

  2. 2
    Which of the following problems should be solved using dynamic programming?
    1. AMergesort
    2. BBinary search
    3. CLongest common subsequence
    4. DQuicksort
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Longest common subsequence has overlapping subproblems and optimal substructure, so a DP table over prefix pairs solves it in O(mn). Mergesort, quicksort and binary search split into disjoint subproblems and are divide-and-conquer, not DP.

  3. 3
    If a problem can be solved by combining optimal solutions to non-overlapping problems, the strategy is called ____________
    1. ADynamic programming
    2. BGreedy
    3. CDivide and conquer
    4. DRecursion
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Non-overlapping subproblems combined into a whole is exactly divide and conquer; dynamic programming is the strategy reserved for the case where the subproblems do overlap.

  4. 4
    Consider the following functions: f(n)=2^n, g(n)=n!, h(n)=n^log n Which of the following Statement about the asymptotic behavior of f(n),g(n) and h(n) is true?
    1. Af(n)=O(g(n));g(n)=O(h(n))
    2. Bf(n)=Ω(g(n));g(n)=O(h(n))
    3. Cg(n)=O(f(n));h(n)=O(f(n))
    4. Dh(n)=O(f(n));g(n)=Ω(f(n))
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    h(n) = n^log n = 2^((log n)^2) and (log n)^2 = o(n), so h(n) = O(2^n) = O(f(n)); and n! = 2^Theta(n log n) grows faster than 2^n, so g(n) = Omega(f(n)). The true ordering is h < f < g, which only option D states correctly.

    official keyProgrammer under Public Health Engineering Department, July 2018 — Technical Paper IPROG2018_P1_034

Arrays, Addressing and Strings

The address-calculation formula is the most mechanical mark in the paper — and the easiest to drop by forgetting the lower bound.

8 concept cards22 past questions

Worked method

Computing an element's address
  1. Write down four things: Base address, element width w, the lower bound(s) of each dimension, and the number of columns (for row-major) or rows (for column-major).
  2. Normalise every index by subtracting its lower bound. This is the step that is skipped under time pressure and it is the one that costs the mark.
  3. Row-major (C, Pascal — rows stored end to end): Base + w × [ (i − lb₁) × ncols + (j − lb₂) ].
  4. Column-major (FORTRAN — columns stored end to end): Base + w × [ (j − lb₂) × nrows + (i − lb₁) ].
  5. Multiply by w only at the end, once you have the element offset.
Worked example

*A[5][4] with lower bounds 1, base address 1000, w = 2 bytes, stored row-major. Find the address of A[4][3].*

Number of columns = 4. Normalised indices: i − 1 = 3, j − 1 = 2.

Offset in elements = 3 × 4 + 2 = 14. Address = 1000 + 2 × 14 = 1028.

The same element column-major: rows = 5, offset = 2 × 5 + 3 = 13, address = 1000 + 26 = 1026.

ADT vs data structure, linear/dynamic

An abstract data type (ADT) specifies what operations a data collection supports and how they behave, while a data structure is the concrete implementation that stores the data and realises those operations.

Key facts & formulas
  • ADT defines operations and their semantics (the interface); a data structure defines storage and algorithms (the implementation).
  • Stack, Queue, List, Set, Map are ADTs; array, linked list, hash table are data structures that can implement them.
  • Linear structures: each element has at most one predecessor and one successor (array, linked list, stack, queue).
  • Non-linear structures: an element can connect to multiple elements (tree, graph).
  • Static structure: size fixed at creation, usually contiguous memory (array).
  • Dynamic structure: size varies at run time as memory is allocated/freed (linked list, dynamic array).
Traps
  • Calling an array an ADT — it is a concrete data structure; "List" is the ADT it can implement.
  • Treating linear/non-linear as being about physical memory layout — it is actually about logical connectivity between elements.
  • Assuming static vs dynamic and linear vs non-linear are the same axis — they are independent; a linked list is linear yet dynamic.
  • Confusing "static" (fixed size once created) with "static memory allocation" (compile-time/global) — a heap-allocated array of fixed size is still a static structure in the DS sense.
Full explanation

An ADT is a logical/mathematical model: it says a Stack supports push, pop, peek with LIFO behaviour, but says nothing about how that is stored. A data structure is the actual storage layout and code that implements an ADT — a Stack can be implemented with an array or with a linked list, and both are valid, interchangeable implementations of the same ADT. Data structures are also classified along two independent axes. Linear vs non-linear describes logical connectivity: in a linear structure every element has at most one predecessor and one successor (array, linked list, stack, queue); in a non-linear structure an element can connect to several others (tree, graph). Static vs dynamic describes size behaviour: a static structure has its size fixed when created, typically stored contiguously (array); a dynamic structure can grow or shrink at run time as memory is allocated and freed (linked list, dynamic array/vector). These two classifications are orthogonal — an array is linear and static, a linked list is linear and dynamic, a graph can be represented dynamically or with a fixed-size adjacency matrix.

Arrays: 1-D address formula

RememberBase + index x width.

An array is a static, linear data structure holding a fixed number of same-type elements in contiguous memory, so any element's address can be computed directly from its index.

Key facts & formulas
  • Random/direct access to any array element is O(1) because the address is computed, not searched for.
  • General formula: Addr(A[i]) = Base + (i - lowerBound) x size_of_element.
  • For a 0-based int array (4 bytes/element) with Base = 1000: Addr(A[i]) = 1000 + 4i.
  • Insertion or deletion in the middle of an array is O(n) because later elements must shift to keep the array contiguous.
  • Arrays occupy contiguous memory, which is precisely what enables this constant-time address computation.
Traps
  • Forgetting to subtract the lower bound when the array's indices don't start at 0 (e.g., a 1-based or otherwise offset array).
  • Confusing "number of elements in the array" with "size of one element" (w) inside the formula.
  • Assuming array access is O(1) even when the index itself must first be found by searching — the O(1) applies only once the index is already known.
Full explanation

Because array elements sit one after another in memory with no gaps, the address of element A[i] can be calculated arithmetically instead of being searched for. If Base is the memory address of the first element, w is the size in bytes of one element, and lower is the smallest valid index, then Addr(A[i]) = Base + (i - lower) x w. For a 0-indexed array this simplifies to Addr(A[i]) = Base + i x w. Worked example: an int array (w = 4 bytes) with Base = 1000 and 0-based indexing. Addr(A[5]) = 1000 + 5 x 4 = 1020. If the same array were instead declared with indices running 1..n (lower = 1), the address of the element holding the same value would be Addr(A[6]) = 1000 + (6-1) x 4 = 1020 — same physical slot, different index because of the different lower bound. This direct computation is why arrays give O(1) random access, in contrast to structures like linked lists where reaching the i-th element requires walking i links from the head.

Row-major vs column-major order

RememberRow-major: the rightmost index moves fastest, like reading a row left to right. Column-major: the leftmost index moves fastest.

A two-dimensional array can be laid out in memory in row-major order (row by row) or column-major order (column by column), each giving a different formula for an element's address.

Key facts & formulas
  • Row-major: Addr(A[i][j]) = Base + [(i - lr) x N + (j - lc)] x w, where N = number of columns.
  • Column-major: Addr(A[i][j]) = Base + [(j - lc) x M + (i - lr)] x w, where M = number of rows.
  • C, C++, Java, Pascal store arrays in row-major order; Fortran, MATLAB, R store them in column-major order.
  • For a 3x4 matrix, Base = 1000, w = 2, 0-indexed: row-major address of A[2][1] = 1018; column-major address of the same element = 1010.
  • The two formulas differ only in which dimension's size (N or M) multiplies the outer index.
Traps
  • Swapping N and M — row-major multiplies the row index by the number of COLUMNS (N); column-major multiplies the column index by the number of ROWS (M).
  • Forgetting to subtract each dimension's lower bound when the array does not start at index 0.
  • Assuming row-major is the universal default — Fortran-family languages default to column-major, a classic distractor.
  • Swapping i and j inside the offset term, which silently gives the address of a transposed-position element.
Full explanation

Row-major order (used by C, C++, Java, Pascal) stores every element of row 0, then every element of row 1, and so on. For a matrix with rows indexed 0..M-1 and N columns, Addr(A[i][j]) = Base + [(i x N) + j] x w, where N is the number of columns and w is the element size. Column-major order (used by Fortran, MATLAB, R) stores every element of column 0, then column 1, and so on: Addr(A[i][j]) = Base + [(j x M) + i] x w, where M is the number of rows. Worked example: a 3x4 matrix (rows 0-2, columns 0-3), Base = 1000, w = 2 bytes. Row-major address of A[2][1]: N = 4, so Addr = 1000 + (2x4 + 1) x 2 = 1000 + 18 = 1018. Column-major address of the same element A[2][1]: M = 3, so Addr = 1000 + (1x3 + 2) x 2 = 1000 + 10 = 1010 — same logical element, two different physical addresses depending on storage order. When indices don't start at 0, subtract the respective lower bounds before multiplying, exactly as in the 1-D formula.

Multidimensional array addressing

For arrays of three or more dimensions, an element's address generalises the row-major/column-major idea: each index is weighted by the sizes of all dimensions that vary faster than it.

Key facts & formulas
  • General row-major offset = sum over dimensions of (index - lower bound) x product of sizes of all faster-varying (later) dimensions.
  • Column-major is the mirror rule: multiply by the product of sizes of all slower-varying (earlier) dimensions instead.
  • Address computation for a k-dimensional array takes O(k) time, independent of the array's total size.
  • The 2-D row-major and column-major formulas are the k=2 special case of this general formula.
  • Total number of elements in a k-D array = n1 x n2 x ... x nk (product of all dimension sizes).
Traps
  • Getting the direction of the product backwards — row-major multiplies by sizes of dimensions to the RIGHT, not the left.
  • Memorising a separate formula per dimension count instead of the general "product of faster-varying dimension sizes" rule, which breaks down on unusual index orders in exam questions.
  • Forgetting that a dimension declared as l..u has size (u - l + 1), not u, when computing the product terms.
Full explanation

For a k-dimensional array A[d1]...[dk] stored in row-major order, with dimension sizes n1..nk (each ni = upper_i - lower_i + 1) and element size w, the address is Addr = Base + w x [ sum over p=1..k of (i_p - l_p) x (product of n_q for all q > p) ]. In words: the offset contributed by index i_p is multiplied by the sizes of every dimension to its right, because those vary faster and are stored more tightly nested. Column-major mirrors this by multiplying instead by the sizes of every dimension to the LEFT. Worked example: a 3-D array A[2][3][4] (dimension sizes 2, 3, 4), 0-indexed, w = 4 bytes, Base unspecified. Offset of A[1][2][3] = ((1 x 3) + 2) x 4 + 3 = (3+2) x 4 + 3 = 20 + 3 = 23 elements, so Addr = Base + 23 x 4 = Base + 92. The 2-D row-major/column-major formulas are simply the k=2 special case of this general rule, and computing the address always takes O(k) arithmetic steps regardless of how large the array is.

Sparse matrices: triplet & linked form

A sparse matrix is one in which the vast majority of elements are zero, so it is stored compactly by recording only the non-zero entries instead of the full row-major/column-major array.

Key facts & formulas
  • Triplet representation: array of (row, column, value) tuples; index 0 conventionally stores (numRows, numCols, numNonZero).
  • Triplet storage needs roughly 3 x (number of non-zero elements) units, versus rows x columns for dense storage.
  • Linked (orthogonal list) representation gives each non-zero node a pointer to the next non-zero in its row and the next non-zero in its column.
  • Linked representation supports cheap insertion/deletion of a non-zero element once neighbouring nodes are known, unlike a triplet array which may need shifting.
  • Neither representation gives O(1) random access to an arbitrary A[i][j] the way a dense array does.
Traps
  • Assuming triplet representation still gives O(1) access to A[i][j] — it doesn't; finding a specific element requires searching unless the triplets are sorted and indexed.
  • Calling any matrix with some zeros "sparse" — the term is reserved for matrices where non-zero elements are a small minority.
  • Assuming linked representation is unconditionally better — its per-element pointer overhead only pays off when the matrix's non-zero pattern changes frequently; for a static sparse matrix, triplets are simpler and more compact.
Full explanation

Storing a sparse matrix as a plain 2-D array wastes memory on zeros and wastes time scanning them. The triplet (3-tuple) representation stores only non-zero entries as (row, column, value) triples, usually in an array or list, often with an initial triple recording (total rows, total columns, number of non-zero entries) as a header. This is compact but makes operations like transpose or multiplication more code than the dense-matrix versions, and finding a specific element requires scanning (or a sorted search) rather than direct addressing. The linked (orthogonal list) representation instead gives each non-zero element a node holding its row, column, and value, plus two pointers: one to the next non-zero element in the same row, and one to the next non-zero element in the same column. This supports efficient row-wise or column-wise traversal and cheap insertion/deletion of a non-zero entry once its neighbouring nodes are known, which matters when the matrix's non-zero pattern changes often (e.g., iterative numerical methods). The choice between the two is a classic space-vs-flexibility trade-off: triplets are simpler and more compact; linked lists cost more memory per entry (multiple pointers) but handle dynamic updates better.

Packing and space-efficient storage

Packing is storing data using fewer bits or bytes than a naive layout would need — by combining several small fields into one word, or exploiting known structure like symmetry — trading extra pack/unpack computation for reduced memory use.

Key facts & formulas
  • Packing exploits known structure (redundancy, symmetry, small value ranges) to store data in less space than a naive layout.
  • Symmetric n x n matrix packing needs about n(n+1)/2 elements (upper or lower triangle only) instead of n^2.
  • Bit-field packing stores several small values within the bits of one word or byte (flags, colour channels, hardware registers).
  • Packing trades memory savings for extra CPU time spent packing (inserting) and unpacking (extracting) values.
  • Sparse matrix triplet/linked storage is a form of packing applied specifically to matrices with mostly-zero content.
Traps
  • Assuming packing always improves performance — it can slow down access-heavy code because every read/write needs extra mask/shift or index-mapping operations.
  • Using the plain row-major address formula on a packed triangular matrix — the formula must be adjusted for the packed 1-D layout.
  • Confusing "packing" (deliberately reducing storage) with compiler-inserted padding/alignment, which does the opposite by adding bytes for alignment.
Full explanation

Packing exploits redundancy or structure that a plain layout ignores. A classic case is bit-field packing: several small flags or a small-range value can be stored in a few bits of one byte instead of a whole byte or word each, common in hardware registers, colour values, and status flags. Another classic case is packing a symmetric matrix: since A[i][j] = A[j][i], only the upper (or lower) triangle needs to be stored, roughly n(n+1)/2 elements instead of n^2, with a modified address formula to map a logical (i,j) index into the packed 1-D array. Sparse matrix triplet/linked storage (a separate topic) is itself an example of packing applied to matrices dominated by zeros. The trade-off is always the same: packing reduces memory footprint but adds CPU work — masking and shifting to extract a bit-field value, or extra index arithmetic to read a packed triangular element — so it is a direct application of the general space-time trade-off principle to storage layout rather than to algorithm choice.

Strings: representation and basic operations

A string is a sequence of characters handled as a single data item, represented in memory either with an explicit stored length (length-prefixed) or with a special terminator character marking its end (null-terminated).

Key facts & formulas
  • Length-prefixed strings: length lookup is O(1); can contain any byte value including an embedded zero.
  • Null-terminated strings (C convention): length lookup is O(n), since it requires scanning for '\0'.
  • A null-terminated string cannot store an embedded '\0' as data, unlike a length-prefixed string.
  • Concatenating two strings of length n and m by copying takes O(n+m) time.
  • Lexicographic string comparison takes O(min(n,m)) time in the worst case.
Traps
  • Assuming strlen()-style length is O(1) in C — it is O(n) precisely because C strings are null-terminated, not length-prefixed.
  • Forgetting a null-terminated string cannot hold an embedded '\0' as legitimate data, which a length-prefixed string can.
  • Treating "string" purely as its representation rather than as an ADT (sequence of characters plus operations) whose storage is an implementation choice, echoing the ADT-vs-data-structure distinction.
Full explanation

Length-prefixed representation stores an integer count alongside (usually before) the character data. Its length is available in O(1), and because there is no reserved terminator character, the string can contain any byte value, including a literal zero. Null-terminated representation (the C convention) stores just the characters followed by a sentinel '\0'; there is no separate counter, so computing the length means scanning until the terminator is found, an O(n) operation, and an embedded '\0' cannot appear as data since it would be read as the end of the string. Basic string operations and their typical costs: length lookup is O(1) for length-prefixed strings and O(n) for null-terminated ones; concatenating strings of length n and m by copying both into a new buffer is O(n+m); extracting a substring of length k is O(k); and lexicographic comparison of two strings is O(min(n,m)) in the worst case, since it stops at the first mismatching character or when the shorter string ends. Many higher-level languages (Java, Python, Pascal) use length-prefixed-style internal representations even when their syntax resembles C.

String pattern matching: naive and KMP

String pattern matching finds every occurrence of a pattern of length m inside a text of length n; the naive algorithm checks every possible alignment in O(nm) time, while the Knuth-Morris-Pratt (KMP) algorithm avoids re-examining matched characters to run in O(n+m).

Key facts & formulas
  • Naive matching: worst-case time O(n x m); best case close to O(n) when mismatches occur immediately.
  • KMP preprocesses the pattern into an LPS (longest proper prefix that is also a suffix) array in O(m) time.
  • KMP's overall time complexity is O(n + m), and it never re-reads a text character once it has been matched.
  • KMP's key idea: on a mismatch, use the LPS table to know how far to shift the pattern instead of restarting comparison from the next text position.
  • Other classic matchers: Boyer-Moore (bad-character/good-suffix skipping) and Rabin-Karp (rolling hash), each with different average-case behaviour.
Traps
  • Quoting KMP's complexity as O(nm) — that bound belongs to the naive algorithm; KMP's entire purpose is avoiding it.
  • Assuming naive matching is always O(nm) in practice — on typical, non-adversarial text it usually performs close to O(n).
  • Confusing the LPS/failure function with a simple running count of matched characters — it specifically encodes the longest prefix of the pattern that is also a suffix of the portion matched so far.
Full explanation

The naive algorithm slides the pattern over the text one position at a time, comparing characters from scratch at each alignment; its worst case is O(n x m), which occurs on inputs like text "aaaa...a" against pattern "aaa...ab", where many characters match before the final mismatch at every alignment. KMP improves on this by preprocessing the pattern itself: in O(m) time it builds a failure function (also called the LPS array — longest proper prefix of the pattern-so-far that is also a suffix of it). When a mismatch occurs after some characters have matched, this table tells the algorithm exactly how far it can shift the pattern without re-comparing text characters already known to match, because the LPS value captures how much of the pattern's own prefix already lines up. Since the text pointer never moves backward, the whole scan plus preprocessing runs in O(n + m). Other classical matchers worth knowing by name: Boyer-Moore, which skips ahead using bad-character and good-suffix heuristics and can be sub-linear in practice, and Rabin-Karp, which uses a rolling hash to compare substrings quickly on average.

    Which of the following is not a common operation performed on strings?

    2. BComparison
    3. CBitwise manipulation

    Answer: C

    Bitwise manipulation operates on raw binary representations of numbers, not on the character-level operations (concatenation, comparison, substring) that strings support.
