Linked Lists

Asked as trade-off questions (array vs list) far more often than as code — know exactly which operation each wins.

4 concept cards12 past questions

Linear lists: sequential vs linked storage

A linear list is an ordered collection of elements in which every element (except the first and last) has exactly one predecessor and one successor, and it can be stored either sequentially in contiguous memory or as a linked structure of scattered nodes.

Key facts & formulas
  • Sequential storage gives O(1) random access via address = base + i * size.
  • Linked storage needs no contiguous block and can grow or shrink at runtime.
  • Array insertion/deletion in the middle needs shifting up to n elements: O(n).
  • Linked list insertion/deletion at a known node is O(1), but reaching that node is O(n).
  • Every node in a linked representation carries pointer overhead in addition to the data.
  • A linear list has at most one predecessor and one successor per element; trees and graphs do not.
Traps
  • "Linear" is often wrongly equated with "array" — linked lists are linear structures too; the term describes ordering, not memory layout.
  • Exam questions on "access time of element at position i" expect O(1) for arrays but O(n) (or O(i)) for singly linked lists — do not answer O(1) for both.
  • A fixed-size array's capacity limit is a property of the sequential representation, not of the abstract linear list concept itself.
Full explanation

Linearity is a property of order, not of storage. A linear list can be realised two ways. Sequential (array) representation stores elements in contiguous memory locations; the address of the i-th element is computed directly as base + i * element_size, giving O(1) random access, but inserting or deleting in the middle requires shifting up to n elements, and the maximum size must usually be fixed in advance (in languages without dynamic arrays). Linked representation stores each element in a node holding the data plus one or more pointers to neighbouring nodes; nodes need not be contiguous, so insertion and deletion at a known position are O(1) once you are there, memory grows and shrinks as needed, but reaching the k-th element takes O(k) time because you must walk the chain from the start. Neither representation is superior in general — the choice depends on whether the workload is access-heavy (favour arrays) or insert/delete-heavy at arbitrary positions (favour linked lists). Both remain linear structures: a tree or graph, where an element can have multiple successors, is not.

Singly linked list: nodes, insert, delete

A singly linked list is a chain of nodes, each holding a data field and a single pointer to the next node, with the last node's pointer set to NULL and an external head pointer marking the start.

Key facts & formulas
  • Node structure: {data, next}; next of the last node is NULL.
  • Insert/delete at head: O(1).
  • Insert/delete at tail: O(1) with a maintained tail pointer, else O(n).
  • Insert/delete at an arbitrary known predecessor node: O(1) relinking, but O(n) to locate it by traversal.
  • Search/access by position or value: O(n), no random access.
  • Space per node = size of data + one pointer, versus zero pointer overhead in an array.
Traps
  • Deleting a node given only a pointer to that node (not its predecessor) is impossible in a plain singly linked list without a trick (e.g., copying the next node's data over it and deleting the next node) — this is a classic "can you delete in O(1)" trick question.
  • Without a maintained tail pointer, appending to the end is O(n), not O(1) — a common overestimate.
  • Forgetting to update head when inserting/deleting the first node leaves a dangling reference; exam pseudocode questions often test whether you handle the empty-list and single-node edge cases.
Full explanation

Each node is a small record {data, next}. The list itself is just a pointer, head, to the first node (or NULL if empty). Insertion at the head is O(1): create a new node, set its next to the old head, then repoint head to it. Insertion at the tail is O(1) only if a tail pointer is maintained; otherwise it is O(n) because you must traverse to find the last node. Insertion in the middle (after a given node p) is O(1) once p is known: new->next = p->next; p->next = new — but finding p by position or value costs O(n). Deletion mirrors this: deleting the head is O(1) (head = head->next, free old head); deleting an arbitrary node requires a pointer to its predecessor, since a singly linked list cannot move backward, so you must traverse from head to find that predecessor first, making positional deletion O(n) overall even though the pointer-relinking step itself is O(1). This asymmetry — cheap head operations, expensive arbitrary-position operations without extra bookkeeping — is the central complexity story examiners test.

Doubly linked and circular linked lists

A doubly linked list gives each node a pointer to both its next and previous neighbour, enabling traversal in either direction, while a circular linked list makes the last node point back to the first, removing the notion of a fixed end.

Key facts & formulas
  • Doubly linked node: {prev, data, next}; deletion given a node pointer is O(1), no predecessor search needed.
  • Doubly linked lists cost one extra pointer per node versus singly linked.
  • Circular linked list: last node's next points to the first node instead of NULL.
  • Traversal termination in a circular list must test "pointer == start", not "pointer == NULL".
  • Circular lists suit round-robin scheduling and circular buffering; doubly linked lists suit LRU caches and browser back/forward history.
  • A circular doubly linked list allows insertion/deletion from either end in O(1) with no special-casing of head or tail.
Traps
  • Applying a NULL-check loop to a circular list causes an infinite loop — the correct stopping condition is reaching the start node again.
  • Doubly linked lists are not free: every insert/delete touches up to four pointers (prev and next on both neighbouring nodes), doubling the chance of a linking bug compared to singly linked lists' two.
  • "Doubly linked" and "circular" are independent properties — a list can be singly linked and circular, doubly linked and non-circular, or both/neither; exam options often mix these up as if they were the same axis.
Full explanation

A doubly linked list node is {prev, data, next}. Its payoff over a singly linked list: deletion of a given node is now genuinely O(1) without needing a separate search for its predecessor, since node->prev is already known; backward traversal becomes possible; and operations like "insert before this node" are as cheap as "insert after it." The cost is a second pointer per node (more memory) and more pointer updates per operation (four re-links on insertion/deletion versus two), which also means more chances for bugs. A circular linked list — singly or doubly — connects the tail's next pointer back to the head instead of NULL, so traversal can start anywhere and cycle indefinitely; this suits round-robin scheduling, circular buffers, and multiplayer turn rotation. A circular doubly linked list combines both benefits: bidirectional travel and no fixed end, at the highest memory cost. Termination in a circular list must check "back to start node" rather than "pointer is NULL", since NULL never appears — a frequent source of infinite-loop bugs if the loop condition is copied unchanged from a singly linked, NULL-terminated traversal.

Array vs linked list: full trade-off

Arrays offer fast indexed access and compact storage at the cost of expensive insertion/deletion and often a fixed size, while linked lists offer cheap insertion/deletion and dynamic growth at the cost of slow access and per-node pointer overhead.

Key facts & formulas
  • Indexed access: array O(1), linked list O(n).
  • Insert/delete at an arbitrary position: array O(n) due to shifting, linked list O(1) once the node is located (but O(n) to locate it).
  • Memory overhead: linked list pays one pointer per node (two for doubly linked); array pays none beyond the elements.
  • Cache locality favours arrays; linked-list nodes may be scattered, causing more cache misses in practice.
  • Static arrays have a fixed capacity; linked lists grow dynamically node by node.
  • Dynamic arrays (e.g., vector) amortise resizing to O(1) amortised append, narrowing but not eliminating the gap with linked lists.
Traps
  • "Linked lists are always faster for insertion" is only true when you already have a pointer to the insertion point — searching for that point first still costs O(n), same order as an array shift.
  • Big-O comparisons ignore constant factors: array traversal is often faster in wall-clock time than linked-list traversal despite both being O(n), due to cache locality — a subtlety exam MCQs sometimes probe with "which is faster in practice" framing.
  • Confusing "array size is fixed" with "linear lists must have a fixed size" — the limitation belongs to the sequential representation, not the abstract linear list.
Full explanation

Comparing the two side by side: random access by index is O(1) for an array (direct address computation) versus O(n) for a linked list (must walk from head). Insertion or deletion at an arbitrary position is O(n) for an array (elements must shift to close or open a gap) versus O(1) for a linked list once the position is located, though locating it is still O(n) — so the real win of linked lists appears when you already hold a pointer to the relevant node, as in implementing a stack or queue at one end. Memory: an array uses only the space for the elements (plus possibly unused pre-allocated slack), while a linked list additionally stores one pointer per node (two for doubly linked), which for small data types can dominate. Arrays also have better cache locality since elements sit contiguously, making sequential scans faster in practice than the Big-O alone suggests, whereas linked-list nodes may be scattered across memory, causing cache misses. Static arrays have a fixed capacity decided at creation (dynamic arrays like C++ vector or Python list amortise growth); linked lists grow one node at a time with no pre-declared limit, which is why they underlie dynamic structures like adjacency lists, stacks, and queues where size is unpredictable.

Stacks and Expression Evaluation

The single densest cluster in Unit 3 (~25 questions). Infix→ postfix conversion and postfix evaluation are guaranteed marks once the stack drill is automatic.

7 concept cards30 past questions

Worked method

Infix → postfix using a stack
  1. Scan the infix expression left to right.
  2. Operand → append it straight to the output.
  3. ( → push it. ) → pop and output operators until the matching ( is popped and discarded.
  4. Operator → while the operator on top of the stack has *higher or equal* precedence (for a left-associative operator), pop it to the output. Then push the new operator. For right-associative ^, pop only on strictly higher precedence.
  5. At the end, pop everything remaining to the output.
  6. Precedence: ^ > * / > + −.
Worked example

*Convert (A + B) * (C − D).*

( push · A output · + push · B output · ) pop + → output now AB+ · * push · ( push · C output · − push · D output · ) pop −.

Output so far AB+CD−; finally pop *.

Result: AB+CD−*

Infix → prefix (the order that trips people up)
  1. Reverse the infix expression.
  2. Swap every bracket: each ( becomes ) and each ) becomes (.
  3. Convert that modified expression to postfix by the usual method.
  4. Reverse the postfix result. That is the prefix form.
  5. The sequence is therefore: reverse → swap brackets → postfix → reverse. Options that put the bracket swap first are the standard trap.
Evaluating a postfix or prefix expression
  1. Postfix: scan left to right. Push operands. On an operator, pop two operands — the *first* popped is the right-hand operand — apply, and push the result.
  2. Prefix: scan right to left. Push operands. On an operator, pop two — here the *first* popped is the left-hand operand — apply, and push.
  3. Getting the operand order backwards only matters for − and /; check those carefully.
  4. The single value left on the stack is the answer.
Worked example

*Evaluate the prefix expression * + 2 3 − 6 1.*

Scan right to left: push 1, push 6. Operator −: pop 6 (left), pop 1 (right) → 6 − 1 = 5, push 5. Push 3, push 2. Operator +: pop 2, pop 3 → 2 + 3 = 5, push 5. Operator *: pop 5, pop 5 → 25.

Answer: 25

Parenthesis matching and peak stack depth
  1. Push on every ( and pop on every ); the expression is balanced iff you never pop an empty stack and the stack is empty at the end.
  2. For a 'maximum stack size' question, track the running depth: +1 per (, −1 per ).
  3. The answer is the peak the depth reaches, not the total number of brackets — that is the planted distractor.
Worked example

*Peak stack depth while checking (()(())(())).*

Running depth: 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, 0.

Maximum reached is 3, even though there are 6 opening brackets.

Stack ADT: LIFO, push, pop, peek

RememberLIFO = Last In, First Out.

A stack is a Last-In-First-Out (LIFO) abstract data type that supports insertion (push) and removal (pop) only at one end, called the top, plus an operation (peek/top) to inspect the top element without removing it.

Key facts & formulas
  • LIFO: last element pushed is the first popped.
  • Core operations: push, pop, peek/top, isEmpty, isFull — all O(1).
  • Stack overflow: pushing onto a full stack.
  • Stack underflow: popping or peeking an empty stack.
  • A stack is defined by its interface (access restricted to one end), not by its underlying storage.
  • peek() differs from pop() in that it does not remove the element.
Traps
  • Stack overflow (data-structure sense: pushing past capacity) is unrelated to the OS/programming term "stack overflow" from unbounded recursion exhausting the call stack, though the two are often conflated in loosely worded questions.
  • Underflow is triggered by pop/peek on an empty stack, not by push — a common option-swap distractor in MCQs.
  • All stack operations being O(1) assumes the implementation is correct (e.g., a linked-list stack maintaining a head pointer as top); an array-based stack that mistakenly treats index 0 as top and shifts elements on push/pop would wrongly turn this into O(n).
Full explanation

A stack exposes a narrow interface regardless of how it is implemented underneath: push(x) adds x to the top; pop() removes and returns the top element; peek()/top() returns the top element without removing it; isEmpty() and isFull() report boundary state. The defining property is access order: the element most recently pushed is the first one popped — LIFO. Two error conditions are specific to stacks: stack overflow, attempting to push onto a stack that has reached its maximum capacity (relevant mainly to array-based implementations with a fixed size, or to genuinely exhausting memory in a linked implementation); and stack underflow, attempting to pop or peek an empty stack. A correct stack implementation must check for both before performing the operation and signal an error (exception, return code, or sentinel) rather than silently corrupting state or reading invalid memory. All stack operations — push, pop, peek, isEmpty — run in O(1) time regardless of implementation, which is the entire point of restricting access to one end. This is what distinguishes a stack from a general linear list: the ADT is defined purely by its interface and ordering discipline, not by whether it sits on an array or a linked list underneath.

Array vs linked implementation of a stack

A stack can be implemented on top of an array, using an index variable (top) to mark the current top element, or on top of a singly linked list, using the head of the list as the top of the stack.

Key facts & formulas
  • Array stack: top starts at -1; push does top++ then write; pop reads then does top--.
  • Array stack isFull check: top == capacity - 1.
  • Array stack isEmpty check: top == -1.
  • Linked-list stack: top = head; push prepends a node, pop removes the head node.
  • Linked-list stack has no fixed capacity limit (bounded only by available memory).
  • Both implementations give O(1) push, pop, and peek.
Traps
  • Some textbooks initialise top to 0 with the first element at index 0 written before incrementing — always check which convention (top = -1 vs top = 0 as "next free slot") a given pseudocode uses before evaluating isEmpty/isFull conditions.
  • An array-based stack "overflowing" is a real, checkable condition; a linked-list-based stack effectively never overflows except by exhausting system memory — do not treat overflow as impossible for arrays or as impossible in general.
  • Using the tail (not head) of a singly linked list as the stack top would make push/pop O(n) without a tail-and-second-to-last tracking scheme — the head is the efficient end precisely because deletion there needs no predecessor search.
Full explanation

Array implementation: allocate an array of fixed capacity n and an integer top initialised to -1 (empty). push increments top then writes the value; pop reads the value then decrements top; isFull checks top == n-1; isEmpty checks top == -1. This is simple and has excellent cache locality, but the capacity is fixed at creation, so it can genuinely overflow, and resizing (as dynamic arrays do) requires an O(n) copy when it happens. Linked-list implementation: keep a head pointer as the logical top; push creates a new node whose next points to the old head and repoints head to it; pop reads head's data, advances head to head->next, and frees the old node. Every operation touches only the head, so it is naturally O(1) and the stack can grow until memory itself runs out, avoiding artificial overflow at a fixed size. The trade-off mirrors the general array-vs-list comparison: the array version is more memory-compact (no per-element pointer) and faster in practice due to locality, while the linked version avoids a hard capacity limit and never needs a bulk resize copy. Both give identical O(1) push/pop/peek asymptotically; the choice is a systems-level, not an algorithmic, decision.

Infix, prefix and postfix notation

RememberPostfix = operator comes POST (after) its operands; Prefix = operator comes PRE (before).

Infix notation places an operator between its two operands (A + B), prefix (Polish) notation places the operator before its operands (+ A B), and postfix (Reverse Polish) notation places the operator after its operands (A B +).

Key facts & formulas
  • Infix: operator between operands, e.g. A + B.
  • Prefix (Polish notation): operator before operands, e.g. + A B.
  • Postfix (Reverse Polish notation, RPN): operator after operands, e.g. A B +.
  • Prefix and postfix need no parentheses and no precedence rules to be evaluated unambiguously.
  • Standard conversion method: fully parenthesise by precedence/associativity, then relocate each operator to before (prefix) or after (postfix) its operand pair, innermost first.
  • Example: infix A + B * C = postfix A B C * +, and = prefix + A * B C.
Traps
  • For prefix, work outward from the innermost parentheses but the operator moves to the *front* of its group, not the back — mixing up prefix and postfix relocation direction is the single most common error.
  • A + B * C is NOT converted operator-by-operator left to right (that would wrongly give A B + C *); precedence must be respected first (multiplication binds tighter), giving A B C * + instead.
  • Prefix and postfix of the same expression are usually not simple reversals of each other once there are more than two operators or the operators/operands differ in order of appearance — do not assume reversing postfix always gives valid prefix (it does for single binary operators but is not a general algorithm for correctness on all expressions with non-commutative operators).
Full explanation

Infix is how humans normally write expressions, but it needs parentheses and precedence rules to be unambiguous — A + B * C means "add A to the product of B and C" only because of a precedence convention, not because the notation itself makes it explicit. Prefix and postfix encode the same tree structure without any need for parentheses or precedence rules, because the position of each operator relative to its operands fully determines the grouping. To convert infix to prefix/postfix by hand: fully parenthesise the expression respecting precedence and associativity, then for each parenthesised group move the operator to just before (prefix) or just after (postfix) its two operands, working from the innermost parentheses outward. Example: infix A + B * C fully parenthesised is (A + (B * C)); postfix converts the inner group first: (B * C) to "BC*", then the outer: A + "BC*" to "ABC*+". Prefix instead: (B*C) to "*BC", then A+"*BC" to "+A*BC". These forms matter because a computer can evaluate them left-to-right with a single stack and no lookahead for precedence, which is why compilers and calculators translate infix into postfix internally before evaluating.

Infix to postfix via stack (Shunting Yard)

The stack-based algorithm for converting infix to postfix (a form of Dijkstra's Shunting Yard algorithm) scans the infix expression left to right, sending operands directly to output and using an operator stack to hold operators until precedence/associativity rules say they should be popped to output.

Key facts & formulas
  • Operands go straight to output; operators and parentheses use the stack.
  • Pop to output while stack top has higher precedence, or equal precedence and the current operator is left-associative.
  • '(' is always pushed; ')' pops everything up to and discards the matching '('.
  • At end of input, pop all remaining stack operators to output.
  • Right-associative operators (e.g., exponentiation '^') do NOT pop an equal-precedence operator already on the stack.
  • Worked example: infix A + B * C - D converts to postfix A B C * + D -.
Traps
  • Forgetting the associativity exception for '^': A ^ B ^ C is right-associative (means A^(B^C)), so when the second '^' is scanned, the first '^' of equal precedence should NOT be popped — treating all operators as left-associative gives the wrong postfix here.
  • Mishandling parentheses: popping only one operator instead of popping until (and discarding) the matching '(' is a frequent bug in traced-through exam answers.
  • Confusing "higher precedence pops" with "higher precedence pushes" — the rule is to pop stack operators that are AT LEAST as tight-binding as the incoming operator before pushing the incoming one, not the reverse.
Full explanation

Rules: scan left to right. If the token is an operand, append it to output. If it is '(', push it. If it is ')', pop operators to output until '(' is popped (discard both parentheses). If it is an operator op, pop from the stack to output while the stack top has higher precedence than op, or equal precedence with op left-associative (for right-associative operators like '^', do not pop on equal precedence), then push op. At the end, pop all remaining operators to output. Worked example on A + B * C - D: scan A -> output A. '+' -> stack empty, push '+'; stack=[+]. B -> output AB. '*' -> top '+' has lower precedence, don't pop, push '*'; stack=[+,*]. C -> output ABC. '-' -> top '*' has higher precedence than '-', pop '*' to output (output ABC*), top now '+' has equal precedence and left-associative, pop '+' too (output ABC*+), stack empty, push '-'; stack=[-]. D -> output ABC*+D. End of input: pop remaining '-' to output. Final postfix: A B C * + D -. This single left-to-right pass with one stack is exactly why postfix is the form compilers generate before evaluation.

Evaluating postfix expressions with a stack

A postfix expression is evaluated in one left-to-right pass with a single operand stack: push each operand encountered, and on encountering an operator, pop the required number of operands, apply the operator, and push the result back.

Key facts & formulas
  • Operand tokens are pushed; operator tokens pop two operands, compute, and push the result.
  • For binary operator op: pop b first, then a; compute a op b (order matters for '-' and '/').
  • Exactly one value remains on the stack at the end: the final result.
  • Worked example: postfix 6 2 3 * + 5 - evaluates to 7.
  • Postfix evaluation needs no operator-precedence table at evaluation time — that work was already done during conversion.
  • Unary operators pop only one operand.
Traps
  • Popping order for non-commutative operators: the FIRST popped value is the RIGHT operand (b), the SECOND popped is the LEFT operand (a) — computing b op a instead of a op b silently flips subtraction and division results.
  • If more than one value remains on the stack after processing all tokens, the postfix expression is malformed — this is a standard validity check examiners ask about.
  • Evaluating prefix expressions requires scanning RIGHT TO LEFT with the same pop-two-push-one logic, not left to right — mixing up the scan direction between prefix and postfix evaluation is a common error.
Full explanation

Algorithm: scan tokens left to right. If the token is an operand, push its value. If it is a binary operator, pop the top two values as b (popped first) then a (popped second), compute a op b, and push the result — order matters for non-commutative operators like '-' and '/'. At the end of the scan exactly one value remains on the stack: the result. Worked example on postfix 6 2 3 * + 5 -, which represents infix 6 + 2*3 - 5: push 6 -> stack [6]. Push 2 -> [6,2]. Push 3 -> [6,2,3]. Token '*': pop 3, pop 2, compute 2*3=6, push 6 -> [6,6]. Token '+': pop 6, pop 6, compute 6+6=12, push 12 -> [12]. Push 5 -> [12,5]. Token '-': pop 5, pop 12, compute 12-5=7, push 7 -> [7]. End of input, result = 7. This single-stack, single-pass method is exactly why compilers and calculators translate infix to postfix first: postfix needs no precedence lookup or backtracking during evaluation, only a stack and a fixed pop-compute-push step per operator, making it trivial to implement in hardware or a virtual machine.

Stack apps: parens, calls, backtracking

Beyond expression evaluation, stacks underlie balanced-parenthesis checking, the function call stack that manages procedure calls and returns, backtracking algorithms that must undo choices, and undo/redo history in applications.

Key facts & formulas
  • Balanced-parentheses check: push openers, pop-and-match on closers, must end with an empty stack.
  • The function call stack stores activation records: return address, arguments, local variables.
  • Unbounded/very deep recursion exhausts the call stack, causing a runtime stack overflow.
  • Backtracking (DFS, N-Queens, maze solving) uses a stack (implicit via recursion, or explicit) to revert to the last choice point.
  • Undo functionality is a textbook stack application: most recent action undone first.
  • Expression evaluation, parenthesis matching, and call management all rely on the same LIFO discipline.
Traps
  • A string can have equal counts of '(' and ')' yet still be unbalanced (e.g., ")(") — counting brackets is not equivalent to the stack-based matching check, a frequent wrong-shortcut in MCQs.
  • "Stack overflow" from deep recursion is a call-stack memory exhaustion issue, distinct from the data-structure-level "push on a full array-based stack" overflow condition, though both share the name.
  • Backtracking does not require an explicit stack object if implemented via recursion — the language's own call stack plays that role, which is why "which data structure does recursive backtracking use" can validly answer either "the call stack" or "a stack (implicitly)".
Full explanation

Balanced parentheses: scan the string; push each opening bracket; on a closing bracket, check that the stack top is the matching opening bracket, pop it if so, else the expression is unbalanced; at the end the stack must be empty. This directly reuses the LIFO property — the most recently opened bracket must be the next one closed. Function call stack: every function call pushes an activation record (return address, parameters, local variables) onto a runtime call stack; returning pops it and resumes execution at the saved return address. This is why deep or unbounded recursion causes a real stack overflow — each nested call consumes more stack frames until the reserved stack memory is exhausted. Backtracking algorithms (maze solving, N-Queens, DFS) use a stack, explicit or via recursion, to remember the sequence of choices made so that when a path fails, the algorithm can pop back to the last decision point and try an alternative. Undo/redo in editors keeps a stack of past states or operations; undo pops the most recent action and reverses it, redo can push it onto a second stack for reapplication. All four applications exploit the same idea: the most recent unfinished or reversible unit of work should be the first one revisited.

Recursion removal using an explicit stack

Recursion removal is the systematic transformation of a recursive algorithm into an equivalent iterative one by using a programmer-managed explicit stack to hold the state (parameters, local variables, return point) that the runtime call stack would otherwise track automatically.

Key facts & formulas
  • Recursion removal replaces the implicit call stack with a programmer-managed explicit stack.
  • A stack frame typically stores parameters, local variables, and a resume point/state marker.
  • Tail recursion (nothing left to do after the recursive call) can be converted to a simple loop with no explicit stack needed.
  • Non-tail recursion (work pending after the call, e.g. tree traversal) needs an explicit stack to hold multiple pending states.
  • Iterative (explicit-stack) versions avoid the fixed call-stack depth limit that can cause stack overflow in deep recursion.
  • Recursion and iteration-with-explicit-stack are computationally equivalent — recursion is implicit stack use.
Traps
  • Not all recursion needs an explicit stack after removal — tail recursion converts to a plain loop; assuming an explicit stack is always required is a common overgeneralisation.
  • The explicit stack in recursion removal is not simply "the same as the recursive calls" — it must capture enough state (which step within the function to resume) to correctly continue execution, not just the arguments.
  • Recursive tree/graph traversals converted to iterative form usually still need a stack (for DFS-order) or a queue (for BFS-order) — picking a queue when converting a stack-based (DFS-style) recursive traversal changes the traversal order entirely.
Full explanation

Every recursive call implicitly pushes an activation record onto the call stack and every return implicitly pops one. Recursion removal makes this explicit: define a stack of frames, where each frame captures whatever the recursive call needed to resume — typically the function's parameters/local variables and a marker for which step to resume at. The general recipe: replace the recursive call with pushing a new frame (with the new parameters) and jumping back to the top of a loop; replace a return with popping the caller's frame and restoring its state. For simple tail recursion, this reduces to a loop with the parameters simply reassigned — no explicit stack is even needed, since there is nothing left to do after the call returns. For non-tail recursion (where work remains after the recursive call, as in a tree traversal or Fibonacci-style branching calls), an explicit stack is unavoidable because multiple pending computations must be remembered simultaneously. This transformation matters practically because iterative versions avoid call-stack overhead and stack-depth limits, and it matters academically because it demonstrates that recursion and iteration-with-an-explicit-stack are computationally equivalent — recursion is not a separate power, just implicit stack management done by the language runtime.

  1. 1
    Consider the usual algorithm for determining whether a sequence of parentheses is balanced. The maximum number of parentheses that appear on the stack at any one time when the algorithm analyzes: (()(())(())) are:
    1. A1
    2. B2
    3. C3
    4. D4 or more
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Pushing on '(' and popping on ')' over ( ( ) ( ( ) ) ( ( ) ) ) gives stack sizes 1,2,1,2,3,2,1,2,3,2,1,0 — the peak is 3. The tempting '4 or more' comes from miscounting the 12 symbols as more deeply nested than they are.

    Which data structure is used for implementing recursion?
    1. AQueue
    2. BStack
    3. CArray
    4. DList
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Recursion is implemented with the call stack: each invocation pushes an activation record holding parameters, locals and the return address, and returning pops it — strictly LIFO behaviour.

  3. 3
    A data structure in which elements can be inserted or deleted at/from both the ends but not in the middle is?
    1. AQueue
    2. BCircular queue
    3. CDequeue
    4. DPriority queue
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    A deque (double-ended queue, printed here as 'Dequeue') permits insertion and deletion at both front and rear only. A plain or circular queue restricts insertion to the rear and deletion to the front, and a priority queue orders by key rather than by end.

  4. 4
    A normal queue, if implemented using an array of size MAX_SIZE, gets full when
    1. ARear = MAX_SIZE – 1
    2. BFront = (rear + 1)mod MAX_SIZE
    3. CFront = rear + 1
    4. DRear = front
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    In a linear (non-circular) array queue the rear index only advances, so the queue is full once rear reaches the last index, MAX_SIZE - 1. Option B is the fullness test for a circular queue, not a normal one.

    Answer: A

    Popping from an empty stack is stack underflow; overflow is the opposite error, pushing onto a full stack.

    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A*B+C/D groups as (A*B)+(C/D) because * and / bind tighter than +, so postfix is AB* then CD/ then the final + : AB*CD/+.

    1. A600
    2. B350
    3. C650
    4. D588
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Evaluating left to right on a stack: 4+6=10, 5*10=50; then 9/3=3, 4+3=7; finally 50*7=350.

  8. 8
    A stack is a linear data structure whose insertion and deletion of elements follows which discipline?
    1. AFIFO (First In, First Out)
    2. BLIFO (Last In, First Out)
    3. CRandom access, in any order
    4. DPriority-based access only
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A stack only allows access at one end, called the top: the last element pushed onto the stack is the first one popped off — hence Last In, First Out (LIFO). This is why stacks naturally model nested structures such as balanced parentheses, undo histories, and function call chains, where the most recently opened context must be the first one closed or returned from.

  9. 9
    Attempting to perform a POP operation on an already-empty stack results in a condition known as:
    1. AStack overflow
    2. BStack underflow
    3. CA segmentation fault, and nothing else
    4. DDeadlock
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Stack underflow occurs when a program tries to remove (pop) an element from a stack that has no elements left — there is nothing to return. The opposite error, stack overflow, occurs when a push is attempted on a stack that has already reached its maximum capacity (or, in the case of the run-time call stack, when recursion goes too deep and exhausts the stack's memory region).

  10. 10
    A queue is a linear data structure that follows which discipline for insertion and removal?
    1. ALIFO (Last In, First Out), like a stack
    2. BFIFO (First In, First Out) — the element inserted earliest is the first one removed
    3. CElements are always removed in reverse order of insertion
    4. DElements are removed in a completely random order
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A queue allows insertion (enqueue) at one end, called the rear, and removal (dequeue) at the other end, called the front, so the very first element inserted is also the very first one removed — First In, First Out. This models real-world waiting lines and is the basis for scheduling structures such as print job queues, task queues, and breadth-first search traversal.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_STACK_2

  11. 11
    The main limitation of a simple linear queue implemented over a fixed-size array — one that a circular queue is designed to solve — is that:
    1. AIt cannot store more than one data type
    2. BAfter a number of enqueue/dequeue operations, the array slots before the current front become permanently unusable even though they are logically empty, wasting space until the queue is reset
    3. CIt cannot be implemented using an array at all, only a linked list
    4. DIt requires two separate pointers, which a circular queue does not need
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    In a plain linear array-backed queue, dequeuing only advances the front pointer forward; the freed slots behind it are never reused, so the queue can appear 'full' even when there is unused space at the beginning of the array. A circular queue fixes this by wrapping the rear pointer back to index 0 once it reaches the end of the array, reusing freed slots and making full use of the fixed-size buffer.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_STACK_3

  12. 12
    Which postfix expression correctly corresponds to the infix expression (A + B) * (C - D)?
    1. AAB+CD-*
    2. BAB+CD*-
    3. CA+BCD-*
    4. D*+AB-CD
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    To convert infix to postfix, each parenthesized sub-expression's operator moves to immediately after its own two operands: (A + B) becomes AB+, and (C - D) becomes CD-. The outer '*' operator, which combines these two sub-results, then comes last, after both converted operands: AB+ followed by CD- followed by *, giving AB+CD-*. Option D is the prefix form of the same expression, not postfix.

  13. 13
    Evaluate the postfix expression: 6 3 2 * + 5 -
    1. A7
    2. B13
    3. C1
    4. D17
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Scanning left to right using a stack: push 6, push 3, push 2. On '*', pop 2 and 3, compute 3*2=6, push 6 (stack now has 6, 6). On '+', pop both 6's, compute 6+6=12, push 12. Push 5 (stack now 12, 5). On '-', pop 5 and 12, compute 12-5=7, push 7. The final and only value on the stack, 7, is the answer.

  14. 14
    Evaluate the prefix expression: * + 2 3 - 6 1
    1. A25
    2. B11
    3. C30
    4. D17
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    A prefix expression is evaluated by scanning right to left and pushing operands, applying an operator to the two most recently pushed values when encountered. Here, '- 6 1' evaluates to 6 - 1 = 5, and '+ 2 3' evaluates to 2 + 3 = 5; the outer '*' then combines these two results: 5 * 5 = 25. Equivalently, in infix form the expression reads (2 + 3) * (6 - 1) = 5 * 5 = 25.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_STACK_6

  15. 15
    The stack data structure is fundamental to implementing which of the following?
    1. ARound-robin CPU scheduling
    2. BFunction call and return mechanisms, including recursion, via the run-time call stack
    3. CBreadth-first search (BFS) graph traversal
    4. DDisk scheduling algorithms
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Every function call pushes a new activation record (return address, local variables, saved registers) onto the run-time call stack, and returning pops it off, resuming the calling function exactly where it left off — this LIFO discipline is exactly what allows recursion to work correctly, since the most recently called (innermost) function must always return first. BFS traversal instead relies on a queue, not a stack, to explore nodes level by level.

  16. 16
    Which of the following typically uses a queue as its core underlying data structure?
    1. ADepth-first search (DFS) traversal, which relies on a stack
    2. BBreadth-first search (BFS) traversal and round-robin CPU scheduling, both of which process elements strictly in the order they arrive
    3. CBacktracking algorithms in general
    4. DExpression evaluation using operator precedence rules
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    BFS explores a graph level by level by enqueuing each newly discovered neighbor and dequeuing nodes in the order they were found, which is precisely FIFO behavior. Round-robin CPU scheduling similarly cycles through the ready processes in arrival order, giving each a fixed time slice before moving to the next — also a queue-driven mechanism. DFS and backtracking, by contrast, rely on a stack (explicit or via recursion) to explore deeply before backtracking.

    derived · unratedAuthored for Informatics Officer 2026AUTHOR_STACK_8

  17. 17
    In a priority queue, elements are removed based on:
    1. AInsertion order alone, exactly like a normal FIFO queue
    2. BTheir assigned priority value, regardless of the order in which they were inserted — the highest- (or lowest-) priority element is served next
    3. CA purely random selection process
    4. DReverse insertion order, as in a plain stack
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A priority queue associates each element with a priority and always serves the element with the extreme (highest or lowest, depending on convention) priority first, irrespective of when it was inserted — two elements inserted at very different times can be served in any order dictated by their priorities. This differs fundamentally from a plain FIFO queue, where insertion order alone determines service order. Priority queues are commonly implemented using a heap.

  28. 28
    How many stacks are needed to implement a queue? Consider the situation where no other data structure like arrays, linked list is available to you.
    1. A1
    2. B2
    3. C3
    4. D4
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    A queue can be simulated with exactly two stacks: push all enqueues onto stack 1, and when a dequeue is needed, pour stack 1 into stack 2 (reversing order) and pop from stack 2. One stack alone cannot reverse the order twice to restore FIFO behaviour.

    Which is the correct algorithmic sequence for the conversion of an expression from Infix to Prefix? i. Change of every '(' (opening bracket) by ')' (closing bracket) and vice-versa. ii.     4. Div, ii, i, iii

    Answer: C

Queues, Circular Queues and Deques

One formula does most of the work: the (rear+1) mod n == front full test, and the n−1 capacity that follows from it.

5 concept cards10 past questions

Worked method

Circular queue conditions
  1. Fix the array size n and remember that one slot is sacrificed so that full and empty can be told apart.
  2. Advance with modulo arithmetic on both pointers: rear = (rear + 1) % n on enqueue, front = (front + 1) % n on dequeue.
  3. Full when (rear + 1) % n == front. Empty when front == rear.
  4. Maximum elements actually storable is therefore n − 1, not n — this is the single most-asked point.
  5. Current element count is (rear − front + n) % n.
  6. The alternative convention keeps an explicit count variable, and then all n slots are usable. If a question says a count is maintained, the capacity is n.
Worked example

*An array of size 8 is used as a circular queue with the sacrificed-slot method. How many elements can it hold?*

7, not 8. With front = 0, enqueuing until (rear + 1) % 8 == 0 stops at rear = 7, holding indices 0–6.

Queue ADT: FIFO, enqueue, dequeue

RememberFIFO = First In, First Out.

A queue is a First-In-First-Out (FIFO) abstract data type that restricts insertion to one end, called the rear (or tail), and removal to the other end, called the front (or head).

Key facts & formulas
  • FIFO: first element enqueued is the first dequeued.
  • Core operations: enqueue (at rear), dequeue (at front), front/peek, isEmpty, isFull.
  • Queue overflow: enqueue on a full queue.
  • Queue underflow: dequeue or front on an empty queue.
  • All queue operations should be O(1) under a correct implementation.
  • Real-world modelling: print spooling, CPU round-robin scheduling, packet buffering all use FIFO order.
Traps
  • A naive array implementation that shifts every remaining element forward after each dequeue turns an O(1) operation into O(n) — this is precisely the motivation for circular queues, and exam questions probe whether you know this degrade case.
  • Mixing up front and rear: enqueue always happens at the rear, dequeue always at the front — reversing them describes a stack's behaviour, not a queue's.
  • "Queue" without qualification means simple FIFO; do not assume priority ordering or double-ended access unless the question specifies priority queue or deque.
Full explanation

The queue interface mirrors the stack's but with insertion and removal at opposite ends: enqueue(x) adds x at the rear; dequeue() removes and returns the element at the front; front()/peek() inspects the front element without removing it; isEmpty() and isFull() report boundary conditions. The defining property is arrival order: the element that has been waiting longest is the next one served — FIFO, exactly like a real-world queue (a line at a counter). This models real waiting-line scenarios naturally: print jobs are printed in the order submitted, CPU processes in a simple round-robin scheduler are serviced in arrival order, and network packets in a simple buffer are transmitted in the order received. As with stacks, all core operations are conceptually O(1), and correctness depends on the underlying implementation actually achieving that — a naive array-based queue that shifts all elements forward after each dequeue would silently degrade to O(n). The two error conditions are queue overflow (enqueue on a full queue) and queue underflow (dequeue or front on an empty queue), and any correct implementation must guard against both. A queue is defined purely by this FIFO interface, independent of whether it sits on an array, a linked list, or a circular buffer underneath.

Linear queue and the false-overflow problem

A linear queue is a queue implemented on a fixed-size array with separate front and rear index variables that only ever increase, causing the queue to report "full" once rear reaches the array's last index even if earlier slots have since been vacated by dequeues.

Key facts & formulas
  • Linear queue: array of size n with front and rear indices that only increase.
  • enqueue: rear++ then write (after checking rear < n-1); dequeue: read at front, then front++ (after checking not empty).
  • False overflow: rear reaches n-1 and the queue reports full even though slots before front are free/unused.
  • The wasted slots are exactly those with index less than front that were vacated by prior dequeues.
  • The fix for false overflow is the circular queue, using modulo arithmetic to wrap rear (and front) back to index 0.
  • A linear queue never reclaims space once rear hits the last index, regardless of how many dequeues have occurred.
Traps
  • "The queue is full" in a linear array implementation can be a false alarm — always check whether the implementation is linear (no wraparound) or circular before concluding a queue with rear == n-1 truly has no room.
  • Some presentations initialise front and rear to 0 instead of -1, shifting the off-by-one details of the empty/full checks — verify the exact convention given in a problem before applying a memorised check.
  • False overflow is not the same as true overflow: true overflow means every slot is genuinely occupied; false overflow means slots exist but are unreachable under a linear (non-wrapping) index scheme.
Full explanation

Implementation: array of size n, with front and rear typically both initialised to -1 (empty). enqueue increments rear then writes the element (checking rear < n-1 first); dequeue reads the element at front, then increments front (checking front < rear, i.e., not empty, first). The flaw: front and rear only ever move rightward and never wrap back to reuse slots at the beginning of the array that dequeues have already freed. Concretely, in a queue of size 5, if you enqueue 5 elements (rear becomes 4) and then dequeue 3 of them (front becomes 2), slots 0–2 are now free, but rear is still 4 = n-1, so the isFull check (rear == n-1) reports the queue as full even though only 2 of 5 slots are actually occupied. This is the false-overflow (or "false full") problem: capacity is wasted because the linear queue never recycles space behind the front pointer. The standard fix taught alongside this problem is the circular queue, which treats the array as wrapping around so rear can cycle back to reuse freed slots, computing the next position as (rear + 1) % n instead of a plain increment.

Circular queue: wraparound, full/empty test

RememberFull test: (rear+1) mod n == front — one step from colliding with front means no room left.

A circular queue is an array-based queue in which the rear and front indices wrap back to the start of the array using modulo arithmetic, so that slots vacated by dequeues at the front are reused by later enqueues at the rear, eliminating false overflow.

Key facts & formulas
  • Index update: rear = (rear + 1) % n, front = (front + 1) % n — this is the wraparound arithmetic.
  • Full condition (one-slot-sacrificed convention): (rear + 1) % n == front.
  • Empty condition: front == rear (in the initial/no-count convention), or front == -1 in the -1-init convention.
  • A circular queue using the sacrificed-slot method holds at most n - 1 elements out of an n-sized array.
  • Alternative to sacrificing a slot: maintain an explicit count variable, checking count == n (full) or count == 0 (empty).
  • Circular queues eliminate the false-overflow problem of linear queues by reusing freed slots behind the front.
Traps
  • The classic MCQ trap: for an n-slot circular queue with the sacrificed-slot full test, the maximum number of elements it can actually hold is n - 1, not n — students who forget the sacrificed slot answer n and lose marks.
  • front == rear alone is ambiguous between empty and full without additional information (a count, a full flag, or the sacrificed-slot convention) — never state "front == rear means empty" as a universal rule without naming which convention is in use.
  • Applying % n only to rear but forgetting to also wrap front on dequeue (or vice versa) breaks the whole scheme — both pointers must use modulo arithmetic consistently.
Full explanation

With array size n, indices advance as rear = (rear + 1) % n and front = (front + 1) % n, so index n-1 is followed by index 0 again. This solves the linear queue's wasted-space problem: freed slots behind the front pointer are reused instead of stranded. The catch is a genuine ambiguity: after wraparound, both the empty state and the full state can produce front == rear, since the indices are cycling through the same range. The classic textbook resolution is to deliberately leave one slot always unused: the queue is treated as full when (rear + 1) % n == front (i.e., advancing rear one more step would collide with front), and empty when front == rear (or, in the -1-initialised convention, front == -1). This means a circular queue of array size n can hold at most n-1 elements, never all n — a frequently tested MCQ trap. Alternative fixes exist (keeping an explicit count/size variable, or a boolean isFull flag), which avoid sacrificing a slot but add a variable to maintain; the "sacrifice one slot" method is the one most commonly assumed in exam pseudocode unless stated otherwise.

Deque: input- and output-restricted

A deque (double-ended queue) is a linear ADT that allows insertion and deletion at both ends, generalising both stacks and queues; an input-restricted deque allows insertion at only one end (but deletion at both), while an output-restricted deque allows deletion at only one end (but insertion at both).

Key facts & formulas
  • Full deque operations: insertFront, insertRear, deleteFront, deleteRear.
  • Input-restricted deque: insertion allowed at one end only; deletion allowed at both ends.
  • Output-restricted deque: deletion allowed at one end only; insertion allowed at both ends.
  • A deque can emulate both a stack and a queue by restricting which end(s) are used.
  • Doubly linked lists or circular arrays are the natural underlying implementations for O(1) both-end access.
  • A singly linked list is a poor fit for a deque since tail-end deletion needs an O(n) predecessor search.
Traps
  • Input-restricted vs output-restricted are easy to swap in memory: "input" restriction limits where you can PUT things in (one end), "output" restriction limits where you can TAKE things out (one end) — read the question's exact wording rather than guessing from the name alone.
  • A deque is not automatically a priority queue or a sorted structure — it only restricts which ends support insertion/deletion, with no ordering guarantee beyond insertion sequence.
  • Implementing a deque on a singly linked list and claiming O(1) deleteRear is incorrect unless a doubly linked list (or extra bookkeeping) is used — this is a common false-complexity claim in exam distractors.
Full explanation

A full deque supports four operations: insertFront, insertRear, deleteFront, deleteRear, each O(1) under a suitable implementation (a doubly linked list, or a circular array with front and rear indices). Because both ends are active, a deque can emulate a stack (always insert and delete from the same end) or a queue (always insert at one end, delete at the other) simply by restricting which operations you call — this is why a deque is considered the more general structure. The two named restricted variants matter for exam definitions specifically: an input-restricted deque permits insertion at one end only, while deletion is allowed at both ends; an output-restricted deque permits deletion at one end only, while insertion is allowed at both ends. These sit strictly between a plain queue/stack and a full deque in flexibility. Real implementations typically use a doubly linked list (natural O(1) access to both ends) or a circular array with two index pointers, since a singly linked list cannot efficiently support operations at both ends (deleting from the tail would require an O(n) search for the new tail's predecessor). Applications include sliding-window algorithms, palindrome checking, and browser history navigation.

Priority queue: implementations, uses

A priority queue is an ADT in which each element carries a priority and dequeue always removes the highest-priority element first, regardless of arrival order, breaking the plain FIFO discipline of an ordinary queue.

Key facts & formulas
  • Unsorted array/list implementation: O(1) insert, O(n) extract-highest-priority.
  • Sorted array/list implementation: O(n) insert, O(1) extract-highest-priority.
  • Binary heap implementation: O(log n) insert and O(log n) extract-highest-priority — the standard choice.
  • A heap maintains only parent-child order, not full sorted order, which is why it beats a sorted array for insertion cost.
  • BFS uses a plain FIFO queue; Dijkstra's shortest-path algorithm uses a priority queue keyed on tentative distance.
  • Other queue applications: CPU/disk scheduling, print spooling, and buffering between producer and consumer processes.
Traps
  • "Priority queue" does not mean "queue sorted by priority using FIFO order for ties automatically" — tie-breaking behaviour among equal priorities is implementation-defined unless the question specifies a stable priority queue.
  • Confusing which array implementation is O(1) where: unsorted array is cheap to INSERT but expensive to EXTRACT; sorted array is the reverse — swapping these is a frequent distractor pairing.
  • A binary heap is not a fully sorted structure — assuming an in-order traversal of the heap array gives sorted output is wrong; only extracting repeatedly (as in heapsort) yields sorted order.
Full explanation

Implementation options trade off insert cost against extract-highest-priority cost. An unsorted array (or linked list): insertion is O(1) (append anywhere), but extracting the maximum/minimum requires scanning all elements, O(n). A sorted array (or list) kept in priority order: insertion must find the correct position, O(n), but extraction of the highest priority is O(1) (it's always at one end). A binary heap (typically a min-heap or max-heap stored in an array) gives the best balance: both insertion and extraction of the highest-priority element are O(log n), because the heap only maintains partial order (parent-child) rather than full sorted order, needing just a sift-up or sift-down along one root-to-leaf path per operation. This is why heaps are the standard real-world implementation of priority queues, including in the classic heapsort algorithm and in Dijkstra's and Prim's algorithms. Applications of queues generally, beyond simple FIFO buffering: CPU and disk I/O scheduling (priority queues for scheduling by urgency), breadth-first search in graphs (a plain FIFO queue to visit nodes level by level), print/job spooling (FIFO), and buffering data between producer and consumer processes running at different speeds (bounded queues, often circular).

Recursion

Tested as stack behaviour and as recurrence-solving, rarely as code tracing.

1 concept cards4 past questions

Recursion: base case, stack, tail call

RememberRecursion = base case (stop) + recursive case (reduce and call).

Recursion is a technique where a function solves a problem by calling itself on smaller instances of the same problem, guided by a base case that stops the calls and a recursive case that reduces the problem each time.

Key facts & formulas
  • Every correct recursive function needs at least one base case (stops recursion) and at least one recursive case (reduces towards the base case).
  • Each recursive call adds a stack frame; recursion of depth d costs O(d) extra space on the call stack.
  • Direct recursion: a function calls itself; indirect/mutual recursion: function A calls B, which calls back to A.
  • Tail recursion: the recursive call is the last action performed, with no work left to do after it returns — this is what enables tail-call optimisation into a loop.
  • Tail-call optimisation is not guaranteed by every language/compiler (e.g. standard Java and many C compilers by default do not perform it).
Traps
  • Assuming any recursive call written at the end of a function is automatically optimised to O(1) space — tail-call optimisation is a compiler/language feature, not a guarantee from writing tail-recursive code.
  • Misclassifying return 1 + f(n-1); as tail recursion — it is not, since the addition happens after the recursive call returns.
  • Reporting only time complexity for a recursive algorithm while forgetting its call-stack space cost — a recursive function can be O(n) in time yet also O(n) in space.
  • Writing recursion with a missing or unreachable base case, causing infinite recursion and stack overflow — a common code-tracing exam trap.
Full explanation

Every correct recursive function needs a base case, which handles the smallest instance directly without further calls (e.g., factorial(0) = 1), and a recursive case, which expresses the solution in terms of a smaller instance (factorial(n) = n x factorial(n-1)). Each call creates a new stack frame holding its parameters, local variables, and return address; frames are pushed on call and popped on return, so recursion of depth d costs O(d) extra space on the call stack, and excessive depth causes a stack overflow. Direct recursion is a function calling itself; indirect (mutual) recursion is a function A calling B, which in turn calls A (or a longer cycle back to A). Tail recursion is the special case where the recursive call is the very last operation performed, with no pending computation left after it returns — an accumulator-based factorial is tail-recursive, but return 1 + factorial(n-1); is not, because the addition happens after the call returns. Tail-recursive code can, in principle, be rewritten by a compiler into an iterative loop reusing one stack frame (tail-call optimisation), but this optimisation is not universal: many mainstream compilers, including standard Java and typical C compilers by default, do not perform it.

Searching

Binary search's log₂n comparison count and its sorted-input precondition are the two things actually asked.

4 concept cards6 past questions

Sequential (linear) search

Sequential search scans a list element by element from one end until the key is found or the list is exhausted.

Key facts & formulas
  • Unsuccessful sequential search always costs exactly n comparisons.
  • Average successful search costs (n+1)/2 comparisons, assuming each position is equally likely.
  • Best case 1 comparison, worst case n comparisons, average and worst both O(n).
  • Works on unsorted data and on linked lists, unlike binary search.
  • The sentinel version stores the key at a[n] so the loop needs only one test per iteration.
  • The sentinel changes the constant factor only; complexity stays O(n).
  • On a sorted list, linear search can stop early on an unsuccessful search when a[i] > key, but still O(n).
Traps
  • The average of (n+1)/2 holds only for a successful search with uniform probability; an unsuccessful search is n, not n/2. Papers often ask for the unsuccessful case.
  • The sentinel does not improve the asymptotic complexity. An option claiming O(log n) or O(n/2) for sentinel search is wrong; O(n/2) is O(n) anyway.
  • Sequential search does not require sorted input. A question saying 'the list must be sorted' is describing binary search.
  • For a single search on unsorted data, linear search beats sort-then-binary-search: O(n) versus O(n log n) + O(log n). Sorting only pays off over many searches.
  • The sentinel needs an array of size n+1. Applying it to an array with no spare slot overwrites real data.
Full explanation

Linear search makes no assumption about order, so it works on unsorted arrays, linked lists, tapes and streams. The loop is: for i = 0 to n-1, if a[i] == key return i; after the loop return -1 (not found).

Cost is measured in key comparisons. An unsuccessful search always makes n comparisons. A successful search on a key equally likely to be in any position makes (1+2+...+n)/n = (n+1)/2 comparisons on average, so both average and worst case are O(n); best case is 1 comparison when the key is first.

The naive loop actually tests twice per iteration: the bounds test i < n and the key test a[i] == key. The sentinel variant removes the first test. Store the key in one extra slot a[n], then loop i = 0; while a[i] != key; i++. The search must stop, because the sentinel matches. Afterwards, if i == n the key was absent. This roughly halves the work per iteration and is a standard exam point, but it does not change the order: still O(n). It costs one spare array slot and destroys a[n], so it cannot be used on a full array or on a read-only list.

Binary search

RememberSorted, split, discard half.

Binary search finds a key in a sorted array by repeatedly comparing it with the middle element and discarding the half that cannot contain it.

Key facts & formulas
  • Time complexity O(log2 n) worst and average, O(1) best (key at the first midpoint).
  • Recurrence T(n) = T(n/2) + O(1), solving to O(log n).
  • Iterative binary search uses O(1) extra space; the recursive form uses O(log n) stack.
  • Requires a sorted array with O(1) indexing; it is not usable on a singly linked list in O(log n).
  • Halving means n = 2^k elements are covered in about k comparisons.
  • Binary search on n elements can be modelled as a binary search tree of the array's positions.
Traps
  • The array must be sorted. Applying binary search to unsorted data does not merely slow down, it returns wrong answers, including 'not found' for a key that is present.
  • With duplicate keys, plain binary search returns some matching index, not necessarily the first or last. Finding the first occurrence needs the lower-bound variant that continues searching left after a match.
  • On a linked list, finding the middle costs O(n) per step, so 'binary search on a sorted linked list is O(log n)' is false.
  • The log is base 2, but since bases differ by a constant factor, O(log2 n) and O(log n) are the same complexity class. An MCQ distinguishing them on asymptotic grounds is testing notation, not speed.
  • Sorting first to enable binary search costs O(n log n); this is not worth it for one lookup.
Full explanation

Precondition: the array must already be sorted in a known order. Method: with bounds low and high, compute mid = (low + high) / 2 (integer division). If a[mid] == key, done. If key < a[mid], search low..mid-1; else search mid+1..high. Stop when low > high, meaning not found.

This is textbook divide and conquer: divide the problem into one sub-problem of half the size, conquer it, and no combine step is needed. The recurrence is T(n) = T(n/2) + O(1), which solves to T(n) = O(log2 n). By the Master theorem with a = 1, b = 2, f(n) = O(1): n^(log_b a) = n^0 = 1, matching f(n), giving Theta(log n).

Trace, array 11 22 33 44 55 66 77 (n = 7), key = 22. low=0, high=6, mid=3, a[3]=44 > 22, so high=2. low=0, high=2, mid=1, a[1]=22, found in 2 comparisons.

Because each step halves the interval, n elements need about log2 n steps: 1000 elements in 10 comparisons, a million in 20. The cost is the sorted-order precondition, and the requirement of O(1) random access, which rules out linked lists.

Binary search: comparisons and pitfalls

Rememberlow + (high - low)/2 — never (low+high)/2.

The worst-case comparison count for binary search on n elements is floor(log2 n) + 1, and the classic implementation bugs are midpoint overflow and a non-shrinking interval.

Key facts & formulas
  • Worst-case comparisons = floor(log2 n) + 1 = ceil(log2(n+1)) for a three-way comparison.
  • n = 1000 needs at most 10 comparisons; n = 1,000,000 needs at most 20.
  • Safe midpoint: mid = low + (high - low) / 2, which cannot overflow when low <= high.
  • With while (low <= high), the updates must be low = mid + 1 and high = mid - 1.
  • With while (low < high) and high = mid, the low update must still be mid + 1, or the loop hangs.
  • The search space sizes follow n, n/2, n/4, ..., 1, so the number of iterations is the number of halvings.
Traps
  • floor(log2 n) + 1 counts three-way comparisons. A two-way (<) implementation that always runs to low == high does about the same number of iterations but a different comparison count; read which the question asks for.
  • mid = (low + high) / 2 is not wrong mathematically, only in fixed-width arithmetic. The bug never appears on small test arrays, which is why it survives review.
  • Off-by-one: high must start at n-1, not n, in the inclusive-bounds version. Starting at n reads past the array.
  • Setting low = mid (not mid + 1) is an infinite loop, not merely slow, because floor rounds the midpoint down onto low.
  • Do not quote log2 n by itself as the worst case. For n = 1000, log2 1000 is about 9.97, and the answer is 10, not 9.
Full explanation

Comparison count. Model the search as a decision tree whose internal nodes are the n array positions. Any binary tree on n nodes has height at least floor(log2 n), so the deepest path visits floor(log2 n) + 1 nodes. With a three-way comparison (<, =, >) at each node, the worst-case number of comparisons is floor(log2 n) + 1, which equals ceil(log2(n+1)). Examples: n = 7 gives 3; n = 1000 gives 10; n = 1,000,000 gives 20. Successful and unsuccessful searches are both bounded by this.

Overflow. Writing mid = (low + high) / 2 can overflow a fixed-width signed integer once low + high exceeds the maximum, producing a negative index. The safe form is mid = low + (high - low) / 2. This bug famously sat in widely used library binary searches for years before being publicised in 2006, and it is a favourite short-note question.

Termination. The interval must shrink every iteration. With floor division, mid == low whenever high == low + 1, so writing low = mid instead of low = mid + 1 loops forever. The two safe skeletons are while (low <= high) with high = mid - 1 and low = mid + 1, or while (low < high) with high = mid and low = mid + 1.

Interpolation and Fibonacci search

RememberInterpolation guesses where, binary always guesses middle.

Interpolation search estimates the key's position from its value instead of always probing the midpoint, and Fibonacci search splits the array at Fibonacci-number boundaries using only addition and subtraction.

Key facts & formulas
  • Interpolation probe formula: pos = low + ((key - a[low]) * (high - low)) / (a[high] - a[low]).
  • Interpolation search: average O(log log n) on uniformly distributed sorted data, worst case O(n).
  • Interpolation search requires sorted data whose keys are numeric and can be arithmetically interpolated.
  • Fibonacci search runs in O(log n) and uses only addition and subtraction to compute probe positions.
  • Fibonacci search splits in the golden ratio (about 0.618/0.382), not into equal halves.
  • All three of binary, interpolation and Fibonacci search require the array to be sorted.
Traps
  • O(log log n) for interpolation search is an average under the uniform-distribution assumption only. Its worst case is O(n), worse than binary search's O(log n); an MCQ offering O(log log n) as the worst case is wrong.
  • Interpolation search is not simply 'better than binary search'. On clustered or exponential data it is worse.
  • If a[high] == a[low] the formula divides by zero; a real implementation must special-case it.
  • Fibonacci search does not beat binary search asymptotically. Its advantage is avoiding division and improving locality of reference, not order of growth.
  • Neither method works on unsorted data, and neither gives O(1) search; that is hashing.
Full explanation

Interpolation search is how a person uses a telephone directory: a name starting with 'B' is looked for near the front, not in the middle. On a sorted array of uniformly distributed numeric keys, it probes

pos = low + ((key - a[low]) * (high - low)) / (a[high] - a[low])

and then narrows exactly like binary search. On uniformly distributed data the average cost is commonly quoted as O(log log n), a large improvement. But the estimate can be badly wrong: on strongly skewed data such as 1, 2, 3, ..., 100, 100000, the probe crawls one element at a time and the worst case degrades to O(n). Guard against division by zero when a[high] == a[low].

Fibonacci search uses the Fibonacci numbers F(k) to split the interval into parts of size roughly F(k-1) and F(k-2), the golden-ratio split, instead of exact halves. Its appeal is arithmetic: the next probe is obtained by additions and subtractions of Fibonacci numbers, with no division or multiplication, which mattered on machines where division was expensive, and it touches elements closer to the previous probe, which suits sequential or paged storage. Its complexity is still O(log n), with a slightly larger comparison count than binary search.

    Assume a binary search tree is constructed using the integer values and searched for the number 55. Identify the INCORRECT sequence from the list given. Justify your answer.

    3. C9, 85, 47, 68, 43, 57, 55
    4. D79, 14, 72, 56, 16, 53, 55
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Tracking the shrinking (low,high) bound while searching for 55: 9 -> (9,inf) -> 85 -> (9,85) -> 47 -> (47,85) -> 68 -> (47,68) -> next node 43 violates the established lower bound of 47, so this path could never occur in a real BST search; the other three sequences all respect their bounds at every step.
