Sorting

Second-densest cluster. The comparison table (time / space / stable / in-place) answers most of it; the rest is pass-tracing.

11 concept cards38 past questions

Worked method

Tracing sorting passes (the 'after k passes' question)
  1. Bubble sort, ascending: each pass walks the whole unsorted prefix comparing *adjacent* pairs and swapping when out of order. After pass k, the k largest elements are parked at the right end, in place.
  2. Selection sort, ascending: pass k scans the unsorted remainder for the minimum and swaps it into position k. After pass k, the k smallest are fixed at the left.
  3. Insertion sort: after pass k, the first k + 1 elements are sorted among themselves but not necessarily in final position.
  4. To answer quickly: identify which end fills up, and how many elements are settled — then check that against the options before simulating the whole thing.
  5. Count of passes to sort n elements is n − 1 for both bubble and selection.
Worked example

*42 29 75 11 65 58 60 18, bubble sort ascending, after 2 passes.*

Pass 1 bubbles the largest (75) to the end: 29 42 11 65 58 60 18 | 75.

Pass 2 bubbles the next largest (65) into place: 29 11 42 58 60 18 | 65 75.

Result: 29 11 42 58 60 18 65 75 — note the two largest are settled at the right, which alone eliminates most options.

Bubble sort

RememberHeavy elements sink, largest bubbles to the end each pass.

Bubble sort repeatedly walks the list comparing adjacent pairs and swapping those out of order, so the largest remaining element bubbles to the end on each pass.

Key facts & formulas
  • Worst and average time O(n^2); best case O(n) only with the swapped-flag early exit, otherwise O(n^2).
  • Total comparisons without early exit are exactly n(n-1)/2.
  • Number of swaps equals the number of inversions; maximum n(n-1)/2 on a reverse-sorted array.
  • Stable: equal elements are never swapped past one another because the test is strictly greater-than.
  • In-place, O(1) auxiliary space.
  • n-1 passes suffice in the worst case; with early exit a sorted array needs one pass.
Traps
  • Best case O(n) requires the flag. Plain textbook bubble sort with two fixed for-loops is O(n^2) even on sorted input.
  • Stability depends on using a[j] > a[j+1]. Changing it to >= makes bubble sort unstable, and it is a standard trick question.
  • Bubble sort's comparison count is input-independent (without early exit); only the swap count varies. Questions asking for minimum comparisons on sorted input are testing this.
  • After k passes exactly k elements are final, at the high end. An option claiming the array is sorted after k passes for k < n-1 is wrong in general.
  • Do not confuse bubble sort with selection sort: bubble swaps adjacent elements many times, selection makes at most one swap per pass.
Full explanation

Pass structure: for i = 1 to n-1, scan j = 0 to n-i-1 and swap a[j] with a[j+1] if a[j] > a[j+1]. After pass i the last i elements are in final position, which is why the inner loop shortens each time.

Trace on 5 1 4 2. Pass 1: 1 5 4 2, 1 4 5 2, 1 4 2 5. Pass 2: 1 4 2 5, 1 2 4 5. Pass 3: no swap. Sorted.

Comparison count is fixed at (n-1) + (n-2) + ... + 1 = n(n-1)/2, which is O(n^2). Swaps range from 0 on sorted input to n(n-1)/2 on reverse-sorted input; the number of swaps equals the number of inversions in the array.

The early-exit optimisation adds a boolean 'swapped' flag, set whenever a swap occurs in a pass. If a full pass makes no swap the array is sorted and the algorithm stops. This turns the best case, already-sorted input, into a single pass with n-1 comparisons and no swaps: O(n). Without the flag the best case is also O(n^2), which is the single most common exam distinction. Bubble sort is stable, in-place with O(1) extra space, and remains O(n^2) on average and in the worst case.

Selection sort

RememberSelect the smallest, swap it home — one swap per pass.

Selection sort repeatedly finds the minimum of the unsorted portion and swaps it into the next position of the sorted prefix.

Key facts & formulas
  • Best = average = worst = Theta(n^2); selection sort is not adaptive.
  • Comparisons are always exactly n(n-1)/2, independent of input order.
  • Swaps are at most n-1, the fewest of the elementary sorts; this is its main selling point.
  • In-place, O(1) auxiliary space.
  • The standard array implementation is not stable.
  • It can be made stable by shifting elements to insert the minimum instead of swapping, at the cost of more moves.
  • On a linked list, or with an insertion instead of a swap, the stability problem disappears.
Traps
  • Selection sort is unstable. Example 4a 4b 1: swapping the minimum 1 into position 0 throws 4a behind 4b, so the equal keys are reordered. This is the single most-asked stability distractor.
  • There is no best case of O(n). Sorted input still costs n(n-1)/2 comparisons; only insertion and flagged bubble sort get O(n) there.
  • Do not confuse comparisons with swaps: comparisons are O(n^2) but swaps are only O(n). A question asking for the number of swaps expects n-1, not n(n-1)/2.
  • Selection sort is not heap sort. Heap sort is the same 'select the extreme' idea accelerated by a heap to O(n log n); plain selection sort scans linearly.
  • Fewest swaps does not mean fastest. It is still quadratic in comparisons and loses to insertion sort on nearly sorted data.
Full explanation

For i = 0 to n-2: scan a[i..n-1] to find the index min of the smallest element, then swap a[i] with a[min]. After iteration i the first i+1 positions hold the i+1 smallest elements in order.

Trace on 29 10 14 37 13. i=0: min is 10, swap gives 10 29 14 37 13. i=1: min is 13, swap gives 10 13 14 37 29. i=2: min is 14, no swap. i=3: min is 29, swap gives 10 13 14 29 37.

Comparisons are (n-1) + (n-2) + ... + 1 = n(n-1)/2 in every case, because the scan for the minimum cannot stop early. Hence best, average and worst are all Theta(n^2), with no adaptive behaviour whatever: a sorted array costs the same as a reverse-sorted one.

Its distinguishing property is the swap count: exactly n-1 swaps in the standard form, or at most n-1 if you skip the swap when min == i. That is the minimum among the simple sorts, so selection sort is the right choice when a write or a swap is far more expensive than a comparison, for example on flash memory or when moving large records. It is in-place with O(1) extra space, and it is not stable.

Insertion sort

RememberSorting a hand of cards: pick the next card, slide it left into place.

Insertion sort builds a sorted prefix one element at a time, shifting larger elements right to open a slot for each new key.

Key facts & formulas
  • Best case O(n) on already-sorted input, n-1 comparisons and zero shifts.
  • Worst and average case O(n^2); reverse-sorted input costs n(n-1)/2 comparisons and shifts.
  • Number of shifts equals the number of inversions in the input.
  • If no element is more than k positions from its sorted place, insertion sort runs in O(nk).
  • Stable and in-place, O(1) auxiliary space.
  • It is online: it can sort data as it arrives, unlike selection, merge, quick or heap sort.
  • Used as the small-array base case inside hybrid sorts because of its low constant factor.
Traps
  • Insertion sort shifts rather than swaps in its standard form; a swap-based version does the same number of element moves but roughly three times the writes. Questions asking for 'element movements' need care.
  • Its O(n) best case does not make it O(n) generally; the average is still quadratic.
  • Binary insertion sort reduces comparisons to O(n log n) but the shifting still costs O(n^2) moves, so the overall time stays O(n^2). This is a favourite trap.
  • Stability requires the strict test a[j] > key, which stops shifting at an equal key and leaves the new element after it. Writing a[j] >= key keeps shifting past equal keys and places the new element before the older one, making insertion sort unstable.
  • Insertion sort is stable and adaptive, selection sort is neither. Do not carry over one's properties to the other.
Full explanation

For i = 1 to n-1: key = a[i]; j = i-1; while j >= 0 and a[j] > key, a[j+1] = a[j] and j--; then a[j+1] = key. It is how most people sort a hand of playing cards.

Trace on 5 2 4 6. i=1: key 2 shifts past 5, giving 2 5 4 6. i=2: key 4 shifts past 5, giving 2 4 5 6. i=3: key 6 stays, one comparison. Done.

The running time is proportional to the number of inversions. Precisely, the number of shifts equals the number of inversions I, and comparisons are I + (n-1) at most. On sorted input each key needs one comparison and no shift, so the best case is n-1 comparisons, that is O(n). On reverse-sorted input every key travels the full distance, giving n(n-1)/2 comparisons and shifts, O(n^2), and the average is about n^2/4.

This inversion-proportional cost is why it is the best of the simple sorts on nearly sorted data: if every element is at most k places from its home, the cost is O(nk), which is linear for constant k. It is stable, in-place, and online (it can sort a stream as items arrive). Real libraries use it as the base case of quick sort and merge sort for small sub-arrays, typically under about 10 to 32 elements.

Merge sort

RememberSplit to single elements, then merge upward.

Merge sort splits the array in half, sorts each half recursively, and merges the two sorted halves into one sorted sequence.

Key facts & formulas
  • Best = average = worst = Theta(n log n); merge sort's running time is input-independent.
  • Recurrence T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Theta(n).
  • Merging two sorted lists of sizes p and q needs at most p + q - 1 comparisons.
  • Array merge sort uses Theta(n) auxiliary space, so it is not in-place.
  • Stable if, on a tie, the element from the left run is taken first.
  • For linked lists it needs only O(1) or O(log n) extra space, since merging is pointer manipulation.
  • It is the standard algorithm for external sorting of data that does not fit in memory.
Traps
  • Merge sort is stable but NOT in-place; quick sort and heap sort are in-place but NOT stable. This pairing is the classic table distractor.
  • It has no bad input. Answers offering O(n^2) as merge sort's worst case are confusing it with quick sort.
  • Auxiliary space is Theta(n), not Theta(n log n): the buffer is reused level by level, not allocated per level.
  • In-place merge sort variants exist but are complicated and lose either stability or the clean n log n constant; the standard algorithm is not in-place.
  • Stability breaks if the merge takes from the right run on equality. If a question shows a merge that prefers the right list, it is unstable.
Full explanation

Divide is trivial (compute the midpoint); the work is in the merge. Merging two sorted runs of lengths p and q uses two pointers, repeatedly copying the smaller front element to the output, then draining whatever remains. It needs at most p + q - 1 comparisons and exactly p + q moves.

Trace on 38 27 43 3. Split to (38 27) and (43 3). Sort to (27 38) and (3 43). Merge: 3, 27, 38, 43.

The recurrence is T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Theta(n), which by the Master theorem gives Theta(n log n). Crucially this holds for the best, average and worst case alike: the split does not depend on the data, so merge sort never degrades. It performs about n log2 n comparisons, and the recursion depth is log2 n.

The price is space. The array version needs a Theta(n) auxiliary buffer for merging, so it is not in-place; the recursion adds O(log n) stack. It is stable provided the merge takes from the left run when the two fronts are equal.

On linked lists merge sort is the natural choice: merging relinks nodes with O(1) extra space, and a bottom-up implementation avoids recursion entirely. It is also the basis of external sorting of files too large for memory.

Quick sort

RememberPivot, partition, recurse — worst case when the pivot is always extreme.

Quick sort picks a pivot, partitions the array so that smaller elements lie left of the pivot and larger ones right, then sorts the two partitions recursively.

Key facts & formulas
  • Average and best case O(n log n); worst case O(n^2).
  • The worst case arises when partitioning is maximally unbalanced, i.e. the pivot is always the extreme element: with a first/last-element pivot this occurs on sorted or reverse-sorted input.
  • Worst-case recurrence T(n) = T(n-1) + Theta(n) = Theta(n^2).
  • Not stable in its usual in-place partition forms.
  • In-place for data movement, but recursion needs O(log n) stack on average and O(n) in the worst case.
  • Recursing on the smaller partition first bounds the stack at O(log n) even in the worst case.
  • Median-of-three and randomised pivots make the worst case improbable; introsort switches to heap sort to guarantee O(n log n).
  • With Lomuto partitioning, an array of all-equal keys triggers the O(n^2) worst case.
Traps
  • 'Quick sort is O(n log n)' is only the average. Its worst case is O(n^2), and it is the only common O(n log n)-average sort with a quadratic worst case.
  • Already-sorted input is quick sort's WORST case with a naive pivot, while it is insertion sort's and flagged bubble sort's BEST case. Papers exploit this reversal constantly.
  • Quick sort is in-place but not stable; merge sort is stable but not in-place. Do not swap these.
  • Randomising the pivot does not remove the O(n^2) worst case; it only makes it vanishingly unlikely. The expected time becomes O(n log n) for every input.
  • The partition step is O(n) and the pivot lands in final position after ONE partition, not after the recursion; a question asking 'how many elements are in final position after the first pass' expects one.
  • Quick sort is faster than merge sort in practice because of cache behaviour and no auxiliary array, not because of a better asymptotic bound.
Full explanation

The work is in the partition; there is no combine step, because after partitioning the pivot is already in its final position. Lomuto's scheme takes the last element as pivot and sweeps once, keeping a boundary index; Hoare's scheme runs two pointers inward and does fewer swaps.

Trace with pivot 4 on 7 2 1 8 6 3 5 4: elements below 4 go left (2 1 3), 4 sits at index 3, larger ones go right (7 8 6 5); recurse on each side.

A balanced split gives T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Theta(n) = Theta(n log n), the average case, with a small constant that makes quick sort the fastest general-purpose in-memory sort in practice.

The worst case comes from maximally unbalanced partitions: if the pivot is the smallest or largest element every time, one side is empty and the other has n-1 elements, so T(n) = T(n-1) + Theta(n) = Theta(n^2). Concretely, with a fixed first- or last-element pivot this happens on data that is already sorted or reverse-sorted, and with Lomuto partitioning it also happens when all elements are equal. That is the standard exam answer: quick sort's worst case is on already-sorted input under naive pivot choice.

Remedies: random pivot, median-of-three, or introsort, which switches to heap sort once recursion gets too deep.

Heap sort

RememberBuild once in O(n), then extract n times at O(log n).

Heap sort builds a max-heap from the array, then repeatedly swaps the root with the last unsorted element and re-heapifies, growing a sorted suffix.

Key facts & formulas
  • Best = average = worst = O(n log n).
  • Bottom-up heap construction (Floyd's method) is O(n), not O(n log n).
  • The extraction phase performs n-1 delete-max operations, each O(log n).
  • In-place with O(1) auxiliary space.
  • Not stable.
  • A max-heap is used to sort ascending; the maximum is swapped to the end each time.
  • For 0-based indexing, node i has children at 2i+1 and 2i+2 and parent at floor((i-1)/2).
  • Heapify starts at index floor(n/2) - 1, the last internal node; leaves need no sifting.
Traps
  • Building the heap is O(n), but the whole sort is O(n log n). Answering O(n) for heap sort because heap construction is O(n) is a standard error.
  • Heap sort is in-place but not stable. Merge sort is the reverse. Selection sort is also unstable, and heap sort is essentially selection sort with a heap.
  • Use a MAX-heap for ascending output. A min-heap in an in-place heap sort produces descending order.
  • Heap sort has no worst-case degradation, so it is not the answer to 'which O(n log n) sort can become O(n^2)' — that is quick sort.
  • A heap is only partially ordered. The array after phase 1 is a valid heap but is NOT sorted, and a heap's in-order traversal is not sorted either.
Full explanation

Phase 1, build the heap. Treat the array as a complete binary tree where node i has children 2i+1 and 2i+2. Apply heapify (sift-down) to nodes from index n/2 - 1 down to 0. Although each heapify is O(log n), building bottom-up costs only O(n), because most nodes are near the leaves and sift down very little. Building instead by n successive insertions costs O(n log n).

Phase 2, extract. Swap a[0] (the maximum) with a[n-1], shrink the heap by one, and sift down the new root, O(log n). Repeat n-1 times, costing O(n log n). Total: O(n) + O(n log n) = O(n log n).

Because both phases are data-independent in their bounds, best, average and worst case are all O(n log n). Unlike quick sort there is no bad input, and unlike merge sort there is no auxiliary array: heap sort is in-place with O(1) extra space, and iterative sift-down needs no recursion stack.

It is not stable: sifting moves elements over long distances and can reorder equal keys. In practice it is slower than quick sort on typical data because its access pattern jumps around the array and defeats the cache, but its guaranteed bound makes it the fallback inside introsort.

Shell sort

RememberInsertion sort with long-distance jumps, gap shrinking to 1.

Shell sort is insertion sort applied to elements spaced a gap apart, with the gap shrinking to 1 so that the final pass is an ordinary insertion sort on nearly sorted data.

Key facts & formulas
  • Shell sort is a generalisation of insertion sort using diminishing gaps ending at 1.
  • Worst case with Shell's original n/2 halving sequence is Theta(n^2).
  • Hibbard's gaps 2^k - 1 give a worst case of Theta(n^(3/2)).
  • Knuth's sequence h = 3h + 1 (1, 4, 13, 40, ...) is the common practical choice, usually quoted around O(n^(3/2)).
  • In-place, O(1) auxiliary space.
  • Not stable, because comparisons and moves span non-adjacent positions.
  • The last gap must be 1, otherwise the array is not guaranteed sorted.
  • The average-case complexity of Shell sort for a general gap sequence is still an open problem.
Traps
  • Shell sort is NOT stable, even though insertion sort is. Long-distance moves reorder equal keys. This is the commonest Shell sort MCQ.
  • Its complexity is not a single fixed value. Quoting 'O(n log n)' or 'O(n^2)' without naming the gap sequence is what the question is testing; match the answer to the stated sequence.
  • Gaps must decrease to exactly 1. Stopping at gap 2 leaves the array unsorted.
  • Shell sort is not a divide-and-conquer algorithm and involves no recursion.
  • Best case is often quoted as O(n log n) rather than O(n); the gap passes still traverse the array once per gap.
  • The halving sequence performs badly precisely because its gaps are not relatively prime; a question about 'why is n/2 a poor choice' wants that reason.
Full explanation

Insertion sort is slow because an element far from its home must be shifted one place at a time. Shell sort (D. L. Shell, 1959) fixes this by first sorting sub-sequences of elements h positions apart, letting items jump long distances cheaply. When h reaches 1 the array is already nearly sorted, so the closing insertion pass is close to linear.

Algorithm: for each gap h in a decreasing sequence ending at 1, run insertion sort over a[i], a[i-h], a[i-2h], ... for i = h to n-1. An h-sorted array stays h-sorted after later, smaller-gap passes, which is why the work never has to be redone.

Complexity depends entirely on the gap sequence, and this is the examinable part. Shell's original halving sequence n/2, n/4, ..., 1 has worst case Theta(n^2), because the gaps share factors and even-indexed and odd-indexed elements never compare until the last pass. Hibbard's 2^k - 1 (1, 3, 7, 15, ...) gives Theta(n^(3/2)). Knuth's 1, 4, 13, 40, ... (h = 3h + 1) is commonly quoted at about O(n^(3/2)) and is the usual practical choice. Sedgewick's sequences reach O(n^(4/3)). The average case for general gap sequences is not known in closed form.

Counting sort

RememberCount, prefix-sum, place from the right.

Counting sort sorts integer keys drawn from a small known range by counting occurrences of each key value and using the running totals as output positions.

Key facts & formulas
  • Time O(n + k) and space O(n + k), where k is the size of the key range.
  • Linear only when k = O(n); for k much larger than n it is worse than a comparison sort.
  • Stable, provided the output pass scans the input from right to left after prefix-summing.
  • Not in-place: it needs an output array of size n plus a count array of size k.
  • It is not comparison-based, so the Omega(n log n) bound does not apply to it.
  • Requires keys that are (or map to) non-negative integers in a known range.
  • It is the standard stable subroutine used inside LSD radix sort.
Traps
  • Counting sort is O(n + k), not O(n). If a question sets k = n^2, the answer is O(n^2).
  • It is stable only in the right-to-left implementation. Scanning left to right after prefix sums reverses equal elements and breaks radix sort built on it.
  • It is not in-place, so 'linear time and constant space' is wrong.
  • It cannot sort arbitrary comparable data such as floats or strings by direct counting; it needs integer keys in a bounded range.
  • Counting sort does not violate the Omega(n log n) lower bound, because that bound only constrains comparison-based algorithms.
Full explanation

Let the keys be integers in 0..k-1. Three steps: (1) count[v] = number of elements with key v, one pass over the input, O(n); (2) turn count into a prefix sum, so count[v] is the number of elements with key <= v, O(k); (3) walk the input from RIGHT to LEFT, placing each element at output[--count[key]]. Total O(n + k) time and O(n + k) space.

Trace with keys in 0..3 on 2 0 2 1. Counts: 1 1 2 0. Prefix: 1 2 4 4. Scanning right to left: 1 goes to index 1, 2 goes to index 3, 0 goes to index 0, 2 goes to index 2. Output 0 1 2 2.

It beats the Omega(n log n) comparison bound because it never compares two keys; it uses each key as an array index. That is only legal when keys are small non-negative integers (or map to them). It is linear only while k is O(n): sorting a million 32-bit integers by direct counting would need a table of four billion entries.

The right-to-left final pass is what makes it stable, and that stability is precisely what radix sort depends on.

Radix sort and bucket sort

RememberRadix: least significant digit first, stably. Bucket: scatter, sort, concatenate.

Radix sort sorts numbers digit by digit using a stable sort at each digit position, while bucket sort scatters elements into ranges, sorts each bucket, and concatenates them.

Key facts & formulas
  • LSD radix sort: O(d(n + b)) time for d digits in base b; O(n + b) auxiliary space.
  • Radix sort requires the per-digit sort to be STABLE; counting sort is the usual choice.
  • LSD radix sort must process digits from least significant to most significant.
  • Radix sort is stable and is not in-place.
  • Bucket sort has expected O(n) time under a uniform-distribution assumption, and O(n^2) worst case when insertion sort is used inside a single overloaded bucket.
  • Bucket sort is stable if the per-bucket sort is stable; it is not in-place.
  • Increasing the radix b reduces the number of passes d but enlarges the count array; b is a time-space trade-off.
Traps
  • Radix sort is not unconditionally O(n). With d = O(log n) digits, as for distinct integers, it is O(n log n); calling it 'linear' regardless of d is wrong.
  • If the per-digit sort is not stable, LSD radix sort produces a wrong result. This is the most-asked radix sort question.
  • MSD radix sort exists but works differently: it partitions recursively by the most significant digit and does not need each pass to be globally stable in the same way.
  • Bucket sort's O(n) is an average under the uniform-distribution assumption, not a worst case. Skewed input destroys it.
  • Neither algorithm is in-place, and neither contradicts the Omega(n log n) comparison lower bound.
  • Radix sort on plain negative numbers needs an offset or sign handling; the digit extraction assumes non-negative keys.
Full explanation

Radix sort, LSD form: for each digit position from least significant to most significant, stably sort the whole array on that digit, usually with counting sort. After processing all d digits the array is fully sorted. With base b, cost is O(d * (n + b)) time and O(n + b) space. For n numbers of at most d digits in base 10, that is O(d(n + 10)). If d is constant, this is linear.

Trace on 170 45 75 90. Units digits 0 5 5 0 give 170 90 45 75. Tens digits 7 9 4 7 give 45 170 75 90 (170 stays before 75 because the previous pass placed it there and the sort is stable). Hundreds digits 0 1 0 0 give 45 75 90 170. The stability of each pass is what preserves the ordering established by the previous, less significant digits.

Bucket sort: with n keys assumed uniformly distributed over an interval, create about n buckets, scatter each key into its bucket in O(1), sort each bucket (usually with insertion sort), and concatenate. If the keys really are uniform the expected cost is O(n); if they all land in one bucket the cost degenerates to whatever the inner sort costs on n items, O(n^2) with insertion sort.

Both beat O(n log n) only by exploiting key structure, never by comparing better.

Sorting algorithm comparison table

RememberStable set = {bubble, insertion, merge, counting, radix, bucket}; everything else reorders equal keys.

A single reference table of best, average and worst-case time, auxiliary space, stability and in-place status for the standard sorting algorithms.

Key facts & formulas
  • Stable sorts: bubble, insertion, merge, counting, radix, bucket (if its inner sort is stable).
  • Unstable sorts: selection, shell, quick, heap.
  • Not in-place: merge sort, counting sort, radix sort, bucket sort. All others are in-place.
  • Only quick sort among the common O(n log n)-average sorts has an O(n^2) worst case.
  • Merge sort and heap sort are O(n log n) in all three cases; heap sort adds O(1) space, merge sort needs O(n).
  • O(n) best case belongs to insertion sort and to bubble sort with the early-exit flag only.
  • Selection sort's best case is still O(n^2), the only simple sort with no adaptive behaviour.
Traps
  • The single most common distractor pair: merge sort is stable but not in-place; quick sort and heap sort are in-place but not stable. Reversing either half is a wrong answer.
  • Selection sort is unstable while bubble and insertion are stable. Candidates assume all three simple sorts behave alike; they do not.
  • Shell sort is unstable although it is built from insertion sort, which is stable.
  • Quick sort's O(log n) space is recursion stack, not data movement. It is still called in-place; do not mark it 'not in-place' because of the stack.
  • Bubble sort's O(n) best case is conditional on the swapped flag. Without it, bubble sort has no O(n) case at all.
  • Heap sort is O(n log n) in the worst case; only heap CONSTRUCTION is O(n).
Full explanation

Columns: Best | Average | Worst | Auxiliary space | Stable | In-place.

Bubble: O(n) with flag, else O(n^2) | O(n^2) | O(n^2) | O(1) | Yes | Yes Selection: O(n^2) | O(n^2) | O(n^2) | O(1) | No | Yes Insertion: O(n) | O(n^2) | O(n^2) | O(1) | Yes | Yes Shell: depends on gaps, often quoted O(n log n) | gap-dependent | O(n^2) with n/2 gaps, O(n^1.5) with Hibbard | O(1) | No | Yes Merge: O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(n) | Yes | No Quick: O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(n^2) | O(log n) stack average, O(n) worst | No | Yes Heap: O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(n log n) | O(1) | No | Yes Counting: O(n+k) | O(n+k) | O(n+k) | O(n+k) | Yes | No Radix (LSD): O(d(n+b)) in all cases | | | O(n+b) | Yes | No Bucket: O(n+k) | O(n+k) | O(n^2) | O(n+k) | Yes if inner sort is | No

Read the pattern rather than memorising cells. Stable: bubble, insertion, merge, counting, radix, bucket. Unstable: selection, shell, quick, heap. In-place: all the simple sorts plus shell, quick and heap. Not in-place: merge and all three non-comparison sorts. Quick sort is the only common sort whose average and worst case differ in order.

Lower bound for comparison sorting

Remembern! outcomes need log2(n!) yes/no answers.

Any sorting algorithm that orders elements only by comparing pairs of keys must make Omega(n log n) comparisons in the worst case.

Key facts & formulas
  • Worst-case comparisons for any comparison sort >= ceil(log2(n!)) = Omega(n log n).
  • The proof uses a decision tree that must have at least n! leaves and therefore height at least log2(n!).
  • Stirling gives log2(n!) approximately n log2 n - 1.44n, so the bound is Theta(n log n).
  • Merge sort and heap sort meet the bound asymptotically, so Theta(n log n) is optimal for comparison sorts.
  • The information-theoretic minimum is 3 comparisons for n = 3, 5 for n = 4 and 7 for n = 5.
  • Counting, radix and bucket sort are not comparison-based, so the bound does not bind them.
  • The same decision-tree argument gives the Omega(log n) lower bound for comparison-based searching in a sorted array.
Traps
  • The bound is on COMPARISONS in a COMPARISON-based model. Saying 'no sorting algorithm can be faster than O(n log n)' is wrong; radix sort can be linear.
  • Non-comparison sorts do not break the theorem; they fall outside its assumptions by requiring bounded integer keys or a distribution assumption.
  • It is a worst-case bound. It does not forbid an O(n) best case, which insertion sort attains on sorted input.
  • Omega(n log n) is a lower bound, O(n log n) an upper bound. Merge sort is Theta(n log n) because both hold; using Omega and O interchangeably loses the mark.
  • ceil(log2(n!)) is a lower bound and is not always achievable: it is met for small n, but no comparison sort achieves exactly ceil(log2 n!) for every n.
Full explanation

Model any comparison sort as a decision tree. Each internal node is one comparison a[i] <= a[j], each of the two edges is an outcome, and each leaf is one permutation that the algorithm can output. To sort correctly the tree must have at least n! leaves, one for every possible input ordering. A binary tree with L leaves has height at least log2 L, and the height is exactly the number of comparisons on the worst-case input. Therefore

worst-case comparisons >= log2(n!) = ceil(log2 n!).

By Stirling's approximation log2(n!) is approximately n*log2(n) - n*log2(e), which is Theta(n log n). Hence the bound Omega(n log n). Merge sort and heap sort achieve it, so it is tight.

Small cases: n = 3 needs ceil(log2 6) = 3 comparisons; n = 4 needs ceil(log2 24) = 5; n = 5 needs ceil(log2 120) = 7.

Why counting, radix and bucket sort escape it: they never compare two keys against each other. They use a key as an array index, or as a bucket number, extracting information from the key's internal structure. The decision-tree model does not describe them, so the theorem simply does not apply. That is not a loophole; it is a different, stronger assumption about the input, namely that keys are small bounded integers or drawn from a known distribution.

    What is the average case complexity of bubble sort?
    1. AO(nlogn)
    2. BO(logn)
    3. CO(n)
    4. DO(n^2)
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    Bubble sort compares adjacent pairs across roughly n passes of n comparisons, giving O(n^2) in both the average and worst case; only the best case on already-sorted input drops to O(n) with the early-exit optimisation.

    1. AQuick Sort
    2. BHeap Sort
    3. CMerge Sort
    4. DInsertion Sort
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    Insertion sort's inner loop does almost no shifting on nearly sorted data, so it runs in O(n) (about n comparisons plus the few needed for the misplaced elements). Merge and heap sort remain Theta(n log n) regardless of input, and quicksort with a first/last-element pivot actually degrades to O(n^2) on sorted input.

  4. 4
    ___________ comparisons required to sort the list 1, 2, 3…….n using insertion sort.contested key
    1. A(n^2 + n + 2) / 2
    2. B(n^3 + n – 2) / 2
    3. C(n^2 + n – 2) / 2
    4. D(n^2 – n – 2) / 2
    Show answer
    Do not memorise this answer

    The list 1, 2, 3, …, n is already sorted, so insertion sort makes only n − 1 comparisons. The keyed (n² + n − 2)/2 is insertion sort's worst case, which this input is the opposite of.

    Bank's answer: C

    (n^2 + n - 2)/2 = 2 + 3 + ... + n, the count you get when pass i costs i comparisons (i-1 element comparisons plus the loop-boundary test) — the maximum for insertion sort. The question as printed is defective: the list 1,2,3,...,n is already sorted, which insertion sort handles in just n-1 comparisons, and no printed option equals n-1.

    official keyProgrammer under Public Health Engineering Department, July 2018 — Technical Paper IPROG2018_P1_032

    List obtained in third pass of selection sort for list 3, 5, 4, 1, 2 is ___________

    3. C1,5,4,3,2
    4. D3,5,4,1,2
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Selection sort on 3,5,4,1,2: pass 1 swaps min 1 to front giving 1,5,4,3,2; pass 2 swaps min 2 giving 1,2,4,3,5; pass 3 swaps min 3 of {4,3,5} giving 1,2,3,4,5. The options match the list after passes 0/1/2/3, confirming that 'third pass' means the state after pass 3.

    4. DNone of the above.
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Quicksort's worst case arises when every partition is maximally unbalanced (one side empty), so the recursion tree degenerates into a chain with one node per level, giving O(n^2). The phrasing 'single node' per level is the intended description of that degenerate tree.

    Let P be a QuickSort program to sort numbers in ascending order using the first element as pivot. Let t1 and t2 be the number of comparisons made by P for the inputs {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} and {4, 1, 5, 3, 2} respectively. Which of the following holds?

    2. Bt1 < t2
    3. Ct1 > t2
    4. Dt1 = t2
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    For the already-sorted input, first-element-pivot QuickSort hits its worst case: t1 = 4+3+2+1 = 10 comparisons. Simulating {4,1,5,3,2} gives t2 = 7 comparisons. So t1 > t2.

    The best average behaviour is shown by
    1. AMerge Sort
    2. BInsertion Sort
    3. CQuick Sort
    4. DHeap Sort
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Quicksort has the best average-case behaviour in practice among common comparison sorts, thanks to small constants and good cache locality, despite its O(n^2) worst case.

    3. C115

  28. 28
    List the complexity of sorting algorithms in worst case from lower to higher order.
    1. AMerge sort == Heap Sport < Quick sort == Selection Sort == Bubble Sort == Insertion Sort
    2. BMerge Sort < Heap Sort < Quick Sort < Selection sort < Bubble Sort < Insertion Sort
    3. CMerge Sort <= Heap Sort <= Quick Sort < Selection Sort < Bubble Sort < Insertion Sort
    4. DMerge Sort > Heap Sort < Quick Sort < Selection Sort < Bubble Sort < Insertion Sort
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    Worst-case complexities: Merge Sort and Heap Sort are both O(n log n) (equal), while Quick Sort, Selection Sort, Bubble Sort and Insertion Sort are all O(n^2) in the worst case (equal to each other). That is exactly option A's chain once 'Heap Sport' is read as the OCR-mangled 'Heap Sort'.

    1. A5 and 4

    3. C0 and 4

    Show answer

    Answer: D

    Selection sort has no early-exit optimization, so it always performs n-1 = 4 outer-loop passes for 5 elements, sorted or not. A flag-optimized bubble sort, given an already-sorted array, makes exactly one pass, finds zero swaps, and terminates immediately. That gives 4 (selection) and 1 (bubble) — option D.

    For an array containing 8 elements as : 42 29 75 11 65 58 60 18 what will be the result of sorting in ascending order using bubble sort after 2 passes have completed?

    2. B29 42 11 65 58 60 18 75
    3. C11 29 42 58 18 60 65 75
    4. D29 11 42 58 60 18 65 75
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    Tracing bubble sort on 42 29 75 11 65 58 60 18 with adjacent swaps: pass 1 bubbles the largest remaining value to the end each time, giving 29 42 11 65 58 60 18 75. Pass 2 sweeps again from the left: 29,42 no swap; 42,11 swap; 42,65 no swap; 65,58 swap; 65,60 swap; 65,18 swap — landing on 29 11 42 58 60 18 65 75, which is option D, not option C.

  37. 37
    Which of the following is the tightest upper bound that represents the number of swaps required to sort n numbers using selection sort?
    1. AO(log n)
    2. BO(n)
    3. CO(n log n)
    4. DO(n²)
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    Selection sort makes exactly one swap per pass — it finds the minimum of the unsorted remainder and swaps it into place — so it performs n−1 swaps in total, which is O(n). The O(n²) figure that makes (d) tempting is the COMPARISON count: every pass scans the whole unsorted remainder. The stem asks specifically about swaps, and minimising swaps is the one thing selection sort is genuinely good at — it is why the algorithm gets chosen when writes are expensive, as on flash memory.

Trees, BSTs, AVL and B-Trees

Height/node-count formulas plus the traversal-reconstruction drill. The 2^(h+1)−1 vs 2^h−1 off-by-one is the classic trap.

10 concept cards29 past questions

Worked method

Rebuilding a tree from two traversals
  1. You need inorder plus one of preorder/postorder. Preorder with postorder alone does not determine a binary tree.
  2. Preorder: the *first* element is the root. Postorder: the *last* element is the root.
  3. Find that root inside the inorder sequence. Everything to its left is the left subtree; everything to its right is the right subtree.
  4. Split the other traversal into blocks of the same two sizes, and recurse on each half.
  5. Stop when a block has one element — that is a leaf.
Worked example

*Inorder D B E A F C, postorder D E B F C A. Find the preorder.*

Postorder's last element A is the root. In the inorder, D B E lies left of A and F C lies right — so those are the two subtrees.

Left subtree: inorder D B E, postorder D E B → root B, with D left and E right. Right subtree: inorder F C, postorder F C → root C with F as its left child.

Preorder (root, left, right) = A B D E C F

Spotting an impossible BST search path
  1. Track a shrinking interval (low, high) as you walk the given sequence. Start with (−∞, +∞).
  2. Each time you move to a larger value you have gone right, so low rises to the node you just left.
  3. Each time you move to a smaller value you have gone left, so high falls to the node you just left.
  4. The path is impossible the moment a node falls outside the current interval — a BST search could never reach it.
Worked example

*Is 9, 85, 47, 68, 43, 57, 55 a valid BST search path for 55?*

From 9 we go right to 85, so low = 9. From 85 we go left to 47, so high = 85. From 47 we go right to 68 → low = 47. From 68 we go left → high = 68. The interval is now (47, 68).

The next node is 43, which is below the lower bound 47. Impossible — the search had already committed to values above 47.

Answering 'maximum nodes / minimum height' questions safely
  1. First settle the height convention, because the two readings differ by one and the exam has used both.
  2. If height counts levels (a single node has height 1), then max nodes = 2ʰ − 1.
  3. If height counts edges (a single node has height 0), then max nodes = 2^(h+1) − 1.
  4. Use the options to disambiguate: for h = 10, 1023 means levels and 2047 means edges. Whichever appears tells you what the examiner intended.
  5. Minimum height for n nodes is ⌈log₂(n+1)⌉ levels — achieved by a complete tree.
Worked example

*'A binary tree has height 10. Maximum number of nodes?'* with options 1000 / 1023 / 1024 / 1002.

1023 = 2¹⁰ − 1, so the examiner is counting levels. Answer 1023. Had 2047 been offered instead, the edge convention would have been intended.

Tree terminology: nodes, height, depth

A tree is a hierarchical, non-linear data structure of nodes linked by edges, with exactly one root and no cycles, where each non-root node has exactly one parent.

Key facts & formulas
  • Depth of root = 0 (some texts use 1 -- check the paper's stated convention before answering).
  • Height of a leaf = 0; height of an empty tree is conventionally defined as -1.
  • Height of the tree = height of the root = length of the longest root-to-leaf path in edges.
  • Degree of a node = number of its children; degree of the tree = max degree over all nodes.
  • A binary tree has degree at most 2 for every node.
  • The number of edges in a tree with n nodes is always n-1.
Traps
  • Height counts edges, not nodes -- a single-node tree has height 0, not 1, a common off-by-one trap.
  • Depth and height of the SAME node are different: depth counts down from the root, height counts up from leaves.
  • 'Degree of tree' vs 'degree of node' -- a question asking for tree degree wants the maximum, not any one node's value.
  • Some textbooks start level numbering at 1 (root = level 1) instead of 0 -- read the question's stated convention first.
Full explanation

Every non-empty tree has a root node with no parent. A node's parent is the node directly above it; its children are nodes directly below. A leaf (terminal node) has no children. Siblings share the same parent. The degree of a node is its number of children; the degree of the tree is the maximum degree among all nodes. Ancestors of a node are all nodes on the path from it up to the root; descendants are all nodes reachable going down from it. Depth (or level) of a node is the number of edges on the path from the root to that node -- the root itself is at depth 0. Height of a node is the number of edges on the longest downward path from it to a leaf; a leaf has height 0. Height of the tree equals the height of the root. A subtree rooted at any node is itself a valid tree consisting of that node and all its descendants. A forest is simply a collection of disjoint trees. Example: root A with child B, B with child C (a leaf) -- depth of C is 2, height of A is 2, height of C is 0, degree of A is 1.

Binary tree types: full, complete, perfect

Binary trees are classified by shape: full (every node has 0 or 2 children), complete (levels filled left-to-right, last level may be partial), perfect (all internal nodes have 2 children and all leaves at the same level), and skewed (every node has only one child).

Key facts & formulas
  • Max nodes at height h = 2^(h+1) - 1 (h counted in edges from root).
  • Min nodes at height h = h + 1 (a skewed tree, one node per level).
  • Minimum height for n nodes = ceil(log2(n+1)) - 1; maximum height for n nodes = n - 1 (fully skewed).
  • A perfect binary tree of height h has exactly 2^(h+1) - 1 nodes, of which 2^h are leaves.
  • Every perfect tree is both full and complete; a complete tree need not be full, and a full tree need not be complete.
  • A complete binary tree of n nodes has height floor(log2 n).
Traps
  • 'Full' and 'complete' are used loosely in conversation but are precisely different in exams -- full is about child count (0 or 2), complete is about level-filling order.
  • If height is defined as number of levels (nodes) instead of edges, every formula shifts by one -- always check which convention the question uses.
  • A tree can be full without being complete (e.g. a root with two children, only the left of which has two more children).
  • Skewed trees are still valid binary trees, not degenerate/invalid ones -- this trips up true/false questions.
Full explanation

Full (proper/strict) binary tree: no node has exactly one child -- every node is either a leaf or has two children. Complete binary tree: every level is fully filled except possibly the last, which fills left to right with no gaps -- exactly the shape a heap requires for its array representation. Perfect binary tree: every internal node has two children AND every leaf sits at the same depth; a perfect tree is automatically both full and complete. Skewed tree: every node has at most one child, degenerating into a linked list (left-skewed or right-skewed) -- the worst case for BST height. For height h (root at height 0, edges counted), the maximum number of nodes is 2^(h+1) - 1 (achieved only by a perfect tree), and the minimum number of nodes is h+1 (achieved by a skewed tree, one node per level). Equivalently, a tree with n nodes has minimum possible height ceil(log2(n+1)) - 1 and maximum possible height n-1.

Binary tree representation: array vs linked

A binary tree can be stored implicitly in an array using index arithmetic, or explicitly as linked nodes each holding data plus left/right child pointers.

Key facts & formulas
  • 1-based array indexing: left child = 2i, right child = 2i+1, parent = floor(i/2).
  • 0-based array indexing: left child = 2i+1, right child = 2i+2, parent = floor((i-1)/2).
  • Array representation is space-efficient only for complete (or near-complete) trees; a skewed tree of n nodes needs an array of size 2^n - 1.
  • Linked representation needs no pre-known size and handles arbitrary tree shape without wasted slots.
  • Heaps almost always use the array representation because heaps are always complete binary trees.
  • Linked nodes typically carry 2 pointers (left, right) plus data; threaded and parent-linked variants add more.
Traps
  • Mixing up 0-based and 1-based index formulas is the single most common MCQ error on this topic -- always confirm which indexing the question states.
  • Array index arithmetic assumes the tree is stored as if complete; applying it to a genuinely sparse tree wastes space, it does not simply 'not work'.
  • A node's index in the array does not equal its value -- index arithmetic gives structural position, unrelated to key ordering.
Full explanation

In the array representation, node positions map directly to indices: for a node at index i, using 1-based indexing, its left child is at 2i, right child at 2i+1, parent at floor(i/2); using 0-based indexing, left child is at 2i+1, right child at 2i+2, parent at floor((i-1)/2). This works efficiently only when the tree is complete (or close to it), since a sparse tree still needs slots for every possible position up to its height, wasting up to 2^(h+1)-1-n cells. It is the standard choice for heaps, which are always complete. The linked representation stores each node as a separate record with a data field and pointers to its left and right children (null if absent); some variants add a parent pointer for upward traversal. It handles any tree shape without wasted space, at the cost of pointer storage overhead (commonly 2-3 extra words per node) and no O(1) random access to an arbitrary node by position. The choice is a classic space/access-pattern trade-off: array for dense, position-addressable trees (heaps); linked for general, dynamically-changing trees (BSTs, general search trees).

Traversals: inorder, preorder, postorder

Preorder (root-left-right), inorder (left-root-right), and postorder (left-right-root) are the three depth-first orderings of a binary tree; level-order is the breadth-first ordering, and any two of these (one of which must be inorder) can reconstruct the original tree uniquely.

Key facts & formulas
  • Inorder on a BST always produces keys in ascending sorted order.
  • Preorder + inorder, or postorder + inorder, uniquely reconstruct a binary tree.
  • Preorder + postorder alone reconstruct a tree only if it is a full binary tree (every node has 0 or 2 children).
  • All DFS traversals are O(n) time; recursive stack depth is O(h).
  • Level-order traversal is implemented with a queue (BFS), not a stack.
  • Iterative inorder traversal without recursion needs an explicit stack (or threads).
Traps
  • Preorder+postorder without inorder is a favorite MCQ distractor implying reconstruction is always possible -- it is not, unless the tree is full.
  • Inorder alone never determines the tree shape, even though it gives sorted order -- many different trees share the same inorder sequence.
  • Level-order is breadth-first (queue-based); confusing it with a DFS variant that just 'visits root first' like preorder is a common mistake.
  • Postorder's last element is always the root -- students sometimes assume this is preorder's property instead.
Full explanation

Take the tree: A is root, B is A's left child, C is A's right child, D and E are B's left and right children. Preorder: A B D E C. Inorder: D B E A C. Postorder: D E B C A. Level-order (via a queue): A B C D E. Inorder on a binary SEARCH tree always yields keys in sorted order -- its main practical use. To reconstruct a tree, preorder or postorder alone only fixes the root but not how remaining nodes split between subtrees; inorder is needed alongside one of them, because inorder tells you which keys lie left of the root and which lie right, letting the split be found by locating the root's position in the inorder sequence and recursing. Preorder+postorder together can reconstruct a tree only if it is known to be a full binary tree (every node has 0 or 2 children) -- otherwise the split is ambiguous. All four traversals take O(n) time; recursive DFS traversals use O(h) stack space (O(log n) balanced, O(n) worst case skewed), while level-order needs O(w) queue space where w is the tree's maximum width.

Binary search tree: search, insert, delete

A binary search tree (BST) is a binary tree where every node's left subtree contains only smaller keys and its right subtree only larger keys, enabling O(h) search, insert, and delete.

Key facts & formulas
  • BST property: left subtree keys < node key < right subtree keys, applied recursively at every node.
  • Search/insert/delete are all O(h): O(log n) balanced, O(n) worst case (skewed).
  • Deleting a two-children node uses the inorder successor (min of right subtree) or inorder predecessor (max of left subtree).
  • The inorder successor of a two-children node is guaranteed to have at most one child, so its own deletion is simple.
  • Inserting keys in already-sorted order into a plain BST produces a fully skewed tree -- worst-case O(n) height.
  • Inorder traversal of a BST always yields keys in sorted ascending order.
Traps
  • Using the predecessor instead of the successor (or vice versa) is equally valid for BST delete -- a question specifying one is testing whether you follow the specified method, not just the final value.
  • Forgetting that after copying the successor's value in, you must still delete the successor node from its original position -- stopping one step early is a common error.
  • BST worst-case complexity is O(n), not O(log n) -- a question about worst case specifically is testing for the skewed-tree scenario.
  • Confusing 'delete a node with one child' with 'delete a leaf' -- the splice step differs (one relinks a subtree, the other just removes).
Full explanation

Search compares the target with the current node: equal stops, smaller recurses left, larger recurses right, until found or a null pointer is hit. Insert follows the same path to find the correct null spot and attaches a new leaf there. Delete has three cases. Case 1, deleting a leaf: simply remove it. Case 2, deleting a node with one child: splice the node out by linking its parent directly to its single child. Case 3, deleting a node with two children: find its inorder successor (smallest key in its right subtree -- go right once, then left as far as possible) or equivalently its inorder predecessor, copy that value into the node being deleted, then delete the successor/predecessor from its original position -- guaranteed to fall under case 1 or 2, since a leftmost node can only have a right child. Example: delete 30 from a BST where 30 has two children 20 and 50, and 50's subtree minimum is 40 -- replace 30's value with 40, then delete the original 40 node. All three operations run in O(h): O(log n) for a balanced tree, but O(n) worst case for a skewed tree, exactly the motivation for AVL trees.

AVL trees: balance factor and why balance

An AVL tree is a self-balancing BST in which every node's balance factor -- the height of its left subtree minus the height of its right subtree -- is restricted to -1, 0, or +1.

Key facts & formulas
  • Balance factor = height(left subtree) - height(right subtree); must be in {-1, 0, +1} for every node.
  • A balance factor of +2 or -2 after insertion/deletion triggers a rotation.
  • AVL height is O(log n), bounded near 1.44 log2(n+2) -- close to the theoretical minimum.
  • Search, insert, delete are all O(log n) worst case in an AVL tree, unlike O(n) worst case in a plain BST.
  • AVL trees are named for inventors Adelson-Velsky and Landis (1962).
  • Height/balance-factor bookkeeping and rotation overhead make AVL inserts/deletes slower per-operation than a plain BST, in exchange for a far better worst case.
Traps
  • Balance factor is left height MINUS right height -- reversing the subtraction flips the sign convention and answers rotation-direction questions backwards.
  • A balance factor of +-1 is fine and needs no rotation -- only +-2 triggers rebalancing; students sometimes rotate unnecessarily at +-1.
  • AVL guarantees O(log n) WORST case, stronger than a plain BST's O(log n) AVERAGE case -- a question contrasting 'average' vs 'worst' is testing this distinction.
  • Height is measured in edges (leaf = height 0); a nodes-based height convention shifts every balance-factor computation by one.
Full explanation

A plain BST's performance depends entirely on its shape: inserting keys in sorted order produces a skewed, linked-list-like tree with O(n) search/insert/delete instead of the O(log n) a balanced shape would give. AVL trees (named for inventors Adelson-Velsky and Landis) fix this by enforcing a balance invariant at every node after every insertion or deletion: balance factor = height(left subtree) - height(right subtree), and this value must always be -1, 0, or +1. If an operation pushes any node's balance factor to +2 or -2, the tree performs a rotation (see AVL rotations) to restore the invariant before the operation is considered complete. Because the invariant holds everywhere, an AVL tree's height is provably O(log n) -- bounded near 1.44 log2(n+2), only marginally worse than the ideal floor(log2 n) of a perfectly balanced tree. This guarantees worst-case O(log n) for search, insert, and delete, unlike a plain BST's O(n) worst case. The cost is bookkeeping: each node stores or computes its subtree height or balance factor, and inserts/deletes may trigger rotations to re-establish balance, adding constant-factor overhead over an unbalanced BST.

AVL rotations: LL, RR, LR, RL worked

AVL rotations -- single rotations LL and RR, and double rotations LR and RL -- are the four local restructuring operations used to restore the balance-factor invariant after an insertion or deletion pushes it to +-2.

Key facts & formulas
  • RR imbalance leads to a single left rotation; LL imbalance leads to a single right rotation.
  • LR imbalance: right-rotate the left child, then left-rotate the node (double rotation).
  • RL imbalance: left-rotate the right child, then right-rotate the node (double rotation).
  • Each rotation is O(1) time -- a constant number of pointer updates.
  • A single insertion into an AVL tree requires at most one rotation (single or double) to restore the whole tree's balance.
  • A deletion, unlike an insertion, may require rotations at multiple ancestors up to the root, not just one.
Traps
  • The naming (LL/RR/LR/RL) refers to the path of the imbalance from the unbalanced node downward, not the rotation direction -- an LL imbalance is fixed by a RIGHT rotation, which reverses the naming intuition.
  • Confusing which double rotation (LR vs RL) applies requires tracing exactly where the new node landed -- easy to swap under exam time pressure.
  • Insertion needs at most one (possibly double) rotation total; deletion can need one rotation at every level up to the root -- assuming delete behaves 'just like insert' is a common wrong assumption.
  • After a rotation, balance factors of the involved nodes must be recomputed, not assumed to become 0.
Full explanation

Which rotation to apply depends on where the imbalance came from. RR case: imbalance caused by an insertion into the right subtree of the right child -- fix with a single LEFT rotation. Worked example: insert 10, 20, 30 in that order. After inserting 30, node 10 has balance factor -2 (right-heavy, and the extra height is on the right side of 10's right child). Left-rotate around 10: 20 becomes the new root, 10 becomes its left child, 30 its right child -- balanced. LL case is the mirror: imbalance from the left subtree's left child, fixed with a single RIGHT rotation (inserting 30, 20, 10 imbalances at 30; right-rotate to make 20 the root). LR case: imbalance from the right child of the LEFT subtree -- first left-rotate the left child, then right-rotate the node (a double rotation). RL case: imbalance from the left child of the RIGHT subtree -- first right-rotate the right child, then left-rotate the node. Each rotation is O(1), re-linking a constant number of pointers, so a single insertion triggers at most one rotation (single or double) to restore balance across the whole tree.

Threaded binary trees: iterative inorder

A threaded binary tree replaces a node's null child pointers with 'threads' pointing to its inorder predecessor and/or successor, allowing inorder traversal without recursion or an explicit stack.

Key facts & formulas
  • A thread replaces a null pointer with a direct link to the inorder predecessor (left) or successor (right).
  • A tag bit per pointer distinguishes a 'thread' from a normal child link.
  • Single-threading only threads null right pointers (enables forward inorder traversal).
  • Double-threading threads both null left and right pointers (enables traversal in both directions).
  • An n-node binary tree has n+1 null pointers, all candidates for threading.
  • Threaded traversal is O(n) time with O(1) extra space, versus O(h) stack space for ordinary recursive/iterative inorder traversal.
Traps
  • Threads only replace NULL pointers -- a node with a real child never gets a thread on that side, so checking 'is this a thread' always needs the tag, not just non-null-ness.
  • Single-threaded trees only make one traversal direction stack-free; reverse-inorder or other traversals may still need a stack unless double-threaded.
  • Insertion and deletion in a threaded tree are more complex than in a plain BST because thread pointers must be relinked, not just child pointers.
  • The rightmost node's right thread and the leftmost node's left thread have no real predecessor/successor -- these typically point to a dummy header node, not left dangling.
Full explanation

In an ordinary binary tree, roughly half of all pointers in an n-node tree are null (n+1 out of 2n). Threading reclaims these: a null right pointer is redirected to point to the node's inorder successor, and, in a double-threaded tree, a null left pointer points to the inorder predecessor. Each pointer needs an accompanying tag bit to distinguish 'this is a real child link' from 'this is a thread', since both look like ordinary pointers otherwise. A single-threaded tree only threads the right pointers (enough to walk inorder forward); a double-threaded tree threads both directions, also enabling reverse-inorder traversal. To traverse inorder iteratively: from any node, if it has a left child, follow left pointers to the leftmost node; after visiting a node, if its right pointer is a thread, follow it directly to the successor; if it is a real right-child link, descend to that subtree's leftmost node. This removes the need for the stack (or recursion's implicit call stack) that ordinary inorder traversal requires, trading O(h) auxiliary space for a small constant per-node tag overhead -- useful in memory-constrained or embedded settings.

B-trees: order, occupancy, splitting

A B-tree is a self-balancing multi-way search tree, each node holding multiple sorted keys and children, designed so its height (and thus disk accesses) stays very small even for huge datasets.

Key facts & formulas
  • Order-m B-tree: at most m children per node, at most m-1 keys per node.
  • Minimum occupancy: non-root internal nodes need at least ceil(m/2) children; root needs at least 2 unless it's a leaf.
  • All leaves of a B-tree sit at the same depth -- always perfectly height-balanced.
  • Insertion always happens at a leaf; overflow triggers a split at the median key, pushed up to the parent.
  • B-trees grow in height only from the root splitting, never from the leaves independently.
  • Search/insert/delete are all O(log_m n); a large branching factor m keeps height very small.
Traps
  • Order m gives at most m-1 keys per node, not m keys -- an off-by-one that changes the overflow threshold.
  • The minimum-children requirement (ceil(m/2)) applies to internal nodes, not leaves, and the root is exempt if it is the only node.
  • B-trees always grow upward (root splits create a new root), never by adding a leaf level independently, unlike a BST which can grow at any leaf.
  • Large branching factor matters specifically because each node read equals one disk block read -- the benefit is minimizing disk I/O, not reducing in-memory comparisons.
Full explanation

A B-tree of order m allows each node at most m children and at most m-1 keys. Every non-root, non-leaf node must have at least ceil(m/2) children (minimum occupancy, so no node becomes too sparse), and the root must have at least 2 children unless it is itself a leaf. Every leaf sits at exactly the same depth, keeping the tree perfectly height-balanced. Insertion always starts at a leaf: the key is inserted in sorted position within that leaf. If the leaf now holds m keys (overflow, exceeding the m-1 limit), it splits into two nodes at the median key, and that median is pushed up into the parent; if the parent overflows too, the split propagates upward, potentially to the root, which is how the tree grows in height, always from the top, keeping all leaves level. Because each node maps to one disk block and can hold many keys, a B-tree's branching factor is deliberately large (often in the hundreds), keeping the tree height, and therefore the disk seeks per search, very small: O(log_m n), the entire point of the structure for external, disk-resident data.

B+ trees vs B-trees; external search

A B+ tree is a B-tree variant where all data records live only in leaf nodes (internal nodes store just routing keys), and leaves are chained together for fast sequential access -- the standard index structure for disk-based ('external') search.

Key facts & formulas
  • B+ tree: data records only in leaves; internal nodes hold routing keys only.
  • B-tree: data can be in internal nodes or leaves.
  • B+ tree leaves are linked in a chain, enabling efficient sequential/range scans.
  • Every B+ tree search descends all the way to a leaf, even for an exact-match lookup.
  • External search means searching disk-resident data; the goal is minimizing disk block reads, not in-memory comparisons.
  • Databases and file systems overwhelmingly use B+ trees, not plain B-trees, specifically for the range-query benefit.
Traps
  • Assuming B-trees and B+ trees are interchangeable terms -- the data-location difference (leaves-only vs anywhere) is specific and exam-testable.
  • Thinking B+ tree's extra leaf-descent makes it slower overall -- the trade-off wins on range queries and sequential scans, which dominate real workloads.
  • External search performance is measured in disk accesses (I/O), not comparison count -- a question about 'efficiency' here usually means I/O efficiency.
  • Believing internal nodes in a B+ tree store real data 'for faster access' -- they deliberately do not; that is the defining difference from B-trees.
Full explanation

In a plain B-tree, a key's associated data can live in any node, internal or leaf, so a search may stop partway down the tree. In a B+ tree, internal nodes hold only copies of keys used purely to guide the search; actual records exist only at the leaf level, so every search walks all the way to a leaf. This costs a slightly deeper traversal per lookup but pays off for range queries: leaves are linked left-to-right in a chain, so once a starting leaf is reached, scanning a range is a simple linear walk along leaf links, with no need to jump back up the tree. This is why real database indexes and file systems use B+ trees rather than plain B-trees. External search refers to searching data too large for memory, residing on disk; since a disk access is orders of magnitude slower than memory access, the goal is to minimize disk block reads. Because a B/B+ tree node is sized to match one disk block and has a large branching factor, its height, and thus the disk reads per operation, stays very small: typically 3-4 levels even for millions of records.

    If a node having two children is deleted from a binary tree, it is replaced by itscontested key
    1. APreorder predecessor
    2. BInorder predecessor
    3. CInorder Successor
    4. DPreorder Successor
    Show answer
    Do not memorise this answer

    Both replacements are standard and correct: the inorder successor (keyed) and the inorder predecessor are equally valid for deleting a two-child BST node. Circulating keys for this question pick option B.

    Bank's answer: C

    Deleting a two-child node in a BST means promoting the nearest value in sorted order, i.e. the inorder successor (smallest node of the right subtree) as in the standard CLRS/Tanenbaum treatment. Note the inorder predecessor (largest node of the left subtree) is equally valid and some circulating answer keys for this exact question mark option A, so treat this pairing as genuinely ambiguous.

    Both derivations chose the inorder successor, but the inorder predecessor (option b) is the exact symmetric mirror of it and some textbooks key that instead. The question as printed does not distinguish them; learn the successor convention for the exam but treat (b) as defensible.

    official keyProgrammer under Public Health Engineering Department, July 2018 — Technical Paper IPROG2018_P1_027

    Data Structure: Preorder and in order of a tree is given as Preorder : A B D H E C F I G J K Inorder : D H B E A I F C J G K What will be the postorder?

    Answer: A

    Preorder gives root A; inorder splits into left {D H B E} and right {I F C J G K}. Left: root B with subtree D (right child H) and right child E; right: root C with left child F (left child I) and right child G (children J and K).     1. A3

    3. C5
    4. Dnone of these
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    The minimum-node AVL tree of height h follows N(h)=N(h-1)+N(h-2)+1 with N(0)=1, giving N(1)=2, N(2)=4, N(3)=7, so 7 nodes is exactly enough to reach height 3 but not height 4 (which needs 12), making 3 the maximum achievable height.

Heaps and Priority Queues

Index arithmetic (2i / 2i+1 / ⌊i/2⌋) and the build-heap O(n) vs heap-sort O(n log n) distinction.

2 concept cards7 past questions

Worked method

Inserting into a heap (the 'array after insertion' question)
  1. Append the new key at the next free array slot — the end of the array. The tree stays complete by construction.
  2. Compare it with its parent: index ⌊(i−1)/2⌋ if the array is 0-based, ⌊i/2⌋ if 1-based. Check which convention the question uses before you start.
  3. In a max-heap, swap up while the new key is greater than its parent; in a min-heap, while it is smaller. This is *sift-up*.
  4. Stop as soon as the parent dominates, or you reach the root. At most log₂ n swaps.
  5. Read the final array left to right — that is the answer. Do not re-sort it; a heap is not a sorted array.
Worked example

*Insert 35 into the max-heap 40, 30, 20, 10, 15, 16, 17, 8, 4 (0-based).*

Append 35 at index 9. Parent = ⌊(9−1)/2⌋ = 4, holding 15. 35 > 15 → swap; 35 is now at index 4.

Parent = ⌊(4−1)/2⌋ = 1, holding 30. 35 > 30 → swap; 35 is now at index 1.

Parent = index 0, holding 40. 35 < 40 → stop.

Result: 40, 35, 20, 10, 30, 16, 17, 8, 4, 15

Heaps: max-heap, min-heap, index math

A heap is a complete binary tree satisfying the heap-order property -- every parent is >= both children in a max-heap, or <= both children in a min-heap -- and is stored compactly as an array.

Key facts & formulas
  • Max-heap: parent >= both children, everywhere. Min-heap: parent <= both children, everywhere.
  • 0-based indexing: left = 2i+1, right = 2i+2, parent = floor((i-1)/2).
  • 1-based indexing: left = 2i, right = 2i+1, parent = floor(i/2).
  • The root (index 0 or 1) always holds the maximum (max-heap) or minimum (min-heap) element.
  • A heap is always a complete binary tree -- this is what makes the array representation waste-free.
  • A heap is NOT a sorted structure; only the vertical parent-child relation is guaranteed, siblings can be in any order.
Traps
  • Mixing 0-based and 1-based index formulas is the single most common heap MCQ trap -- always check which the question states before computing an index.
  • Assuming a heap's inorder or level-order traversal gives sorted output -- only heap sort's repeated extraction produces sorted output.
  • Confusing 'complete' (required for heaps) with 'full' or 'perfect' -- a heap need not have every internal node with 2 children, only level-filling left-to-right.
  • Assuming the second-largest element in a max-heap is identifiable as 'the other root child' without comparison -- it is only guaranteed to be one of the root's two children.
Full explanation

Because a heap is always a complete binary tree, it can be stored in an array with no wasted space, using pure index arithmetic instead of pointers. For 1-based array indexing (index starts at 1): a node at index i has left child at 2i, right child at 2i+1, parent at floor(i/2). For 0-based array indexing (index starts at 0, the common convention in C/Java/Python): a node at index i has left child at 2i+1, right child at 2i+2, parent at floor((i-1)/2). In a max-heap, the maximum element is always at the root (index 0 or 1, per convention); a min-heap's minimum is likewise always at the root. A heap only guarantees the parent-child ordering vertically -- it says nothing about the relative order of sibling subtrees, so a heap is NOT sorted and inorder traversal of a heap does not yield sorted order, unlike a BST. Heaps back priority queues, heap sort, and graph algorithms like Prim's and Dijkstra's that repeatedly need the current minimum (or maximum) element.

Heap insert, delete-max, build-heap, sort

RememberBuild-heap is O(n): most nodes are near the leaves and barely move.

Insert (sift-up) adds a new element and bubbles it toward the root; delete-max/extract-root (sift-down) removes the root and restores order from the top; build-heap converts an arbitrary array into a heap in linear time; heap sort repeatedly extracts the root to produce sorted output.

Key facts & formulas
  • Insert (sift-up): O(log n); place at end, bubble toward root.
  • Delete-max/extract-root (sift-down): O(log n); replace root with last element, then bubble down via the larger child.
  • Build-heap from an unsorted array: O(n) total, not O(n log n) -- a tighter level-weighted amortized bound.
  • Heap sort overall: O(n log n) time, O(1) auxiliary space (in-place), not a stable sort.
  • Heap sort = build-heap O(n) + n extractions each O(log n) = O(n log n) dominant term.
  • Sift-down compares with the LARGER child in a max-heap (smaller child in a min-heap) -- comparing with the wrong child breaks the heap property.
Traps
  • Assuming build-heap is O(n log n) because it 'calls sift-down n times' -- the correct bound is O(n), a classic exam trick relying on the amortized level-weighted argument.
  • Heap sort is NOT stable -- a question asking for a stable O(n log n) sort should point to merge sort, not heap sort.
  • Confusing sift-up (used on insert) with sift-down (used on delete/build-heap) -- they move in opposite directions and are not interchangeable.
  • Extracting the root does not simply remove it and shift everything -- the LAST element is moved into the root's place first, then sifted down, to keep the tree complete.
Full explanation

Insert: place the new element at the next free array slot (end of the array, keeping the tree complete), then compare it with its parent and swap upward (sift-up) until the heap property holds -- O(log n) worst case, one swap per level. Delete-max (on a max-heap): save the root's value to return, move the LAST array element into the root position, shrink the array, then sift-down: repeatedly swap the node with its LARGER child until it is >= both children or becomes a leaf -- O(log n). Build-heap: rather than inserting n elements one by one (O(n log n)), start from the last non-leaf node (index floor(n/2)-1 for 0-based) and sift-down each node moving backward to the root; a tighter level-weighted amortized analysis shows this costs O(n) total, not O(n log n). Heap sort: build a max-heap in O(n), then n times swap the root with the last unsorted array element and sift-down the reduced heap -- total O(n log n), in-place with O(1) extra space, but not a stable sort.

Hashing

Compute h(k) = k mod m for a given list and spot the collisions — that is essentially the whole cluster.

1 concept cards2 past questions

Hashing: functions, load factor, collisions

Hashing maps keys to array bucket indices using a hash function, giving average O(1) search/insert/delete; when two keys map to the same bucket (a collision), it is resolved by chaining or by open addressing.

Key facts & formulas
  • Load factor alpha = n/m (entries / table size); performance degrades as alpha increases.
  • Chaining: each bucket is a list; alpha can exceed 1; average cost O(1+alpha).
  • Open addressing: all entries stored in-table; requires alpha < 1 strictly.
  • Linear probing: slot+1, slot+2, ... -- suffers primary clustering.
  • Quadratic probing: slot+1^2, slot+2^2, ... -- less clustering, but can fail below full-table coverage unless table size/alpha are chosen carefully (often kept below 0.5).
  • Double hashing: step size = second hash function h2(key); best distribution among open-addressing schemes, minimal clustering.
Traps
  • Open addressing requires load factor strictly less than 1; chaining has no such hard limit -- a question assuming both behave the same at alpha=1 is wrong.
  • Linear probing's weakness is 'primary clustering'; quadratic probing's residual weakness is 'secondary clustering' (keys with the same initial hash follow the same probe sequence) -- a different named phenomenon, often confused with the first.
  • Quadratic probing does not guarantee finding an empty slot even if one exists, unless table size is prime and load factor is kept low -- assuming it always succeeds like linear probing is a trap.
  • Double hashing needs its second hash function to never evaluate to 0 and ideally to be relatively prime to the table size.
Full explanation

A hash function h(key) should distribute keys as uniformly as possible across the m slots of a table, minimizing collisions while being cheap to compute. Load factor alpha = n/m, where n is the number of stored entries, directly governs performance: as alpha grows, collisions become more frequent and operations slow down. Chaining resolves collisions by making each table slot a linked list of all entries hashing there; alpha can exceed 1, and average search cost is O(1+alpha). Open addressing keeps every entry inside the table itself, probing for the next open slot on collision: linear probing tries slot, slot+1, slot+2... (simple, but causes 'primary clustering', long runs of occupied slots that slow future probes); quadratic probing tries slot+1^2, slot+2^2... (reduces clustering but can fail to find an empty slot unless table size and load factor are chosen carefully, commonly kept below alpha=0.5); double hashing uses a second hash function to determine step size (slot + i*h2(key)), giving the most uniform probe sequences and least clustering, provided the table size and h2's range are chosen, often prime, so all slots are reachable. Open addressing requires alpha < 1 always, since there is no overflow structure.

    Answer: A

    The whole point of a hash table is average-case O(1) lookup, unlike the O(log n) or O(n) costs of tree- or list-based searches.
