Graphs, Greedy Methods and Disjoint Sets

Unit 3's angle on the Unit 1 algorithms: representation cost, traversal data structure (DFS→stack, BFS→queue), and complexity.

5 concept cards10 past questions

Graph representation: matrix vs list

An adjacency matrix stores a graph as a V-by-V grid marking edge presence/weight between every vertex pair; an adjacency list stores, for each vertex, only its actual neighbors -- the choice trades memory for edge-lookup speed depending on graph density.

Key facts & formulas
  • Adjacency matrix: O(V^2) space, O(1) edge lookup, best for dense graphs.
  • Adjacency list: O(V+E) space, O(degree) edge lookup, best for sparse graphs.
  • DFS/BFS: O(V+E) time with adjacency list, O(V^2) time with adjacency matrix.
  • Most real-world graphs are sparse (E much smaller than V^2), favoring adjacency lists in practice.
  • Adjacency matrix trivially supports directed, undirected, and weighted graphs by adjusting symmetry and cell values.
  • Prim's simple O(V^2) array-based implementation pairs naturally with an adjacency matrix; the O(E log V) heap-based version pairs with an adjacency list.
Traps
  • Assuming adjacency list is always 'better' -- for dense graphs the matrix's O(1) lookup and simpler code can outperform the list in practice, despite worse asymptotic space.
  • Forgetting the O(V) cost to scan one row of a matrix when iterating a single vertex's neighbors -- this is why full traversal is O(V^2) with a matrix even though a single lookup is O(1).
  • Confusing space complexity (fixed O(V^2) for matrix, variable O(V+E) for list) with time complexity for a specific operation -- they answer different questions.
  • For a directed graph, the adjacency matrix need not be symmetric -- assuming symmetry as in undirected graphs is a common error.
Full explanation

Adjacency matrix: a V-by-V 2D array where entry (i,j) is 1 (or the weight) if an edge exists from i to j, else 0 (or infinity for shortest-path use). It always uses O(V^2) space regardless of how many edges exist, but checking whether edge (i,j) exists or reading its weight is O(1). It suits dense graphs (E close to V^2) or frequent edge-existence queries. Adjacency list: an array of V lists, where list[i] contains all vertices adjacent to i, with weights for weighted graphs. Space is O(V+E), proportional to what actually exists, far smaller than V^2 for sparse graphs -- most real-world graphs (road networks, social graphs) are sparse. Checking if edge (i,j) exists costs O(degree(i)) worst case, since the list must be scanned, rather than O(1). This trade-off directly affects algorithm complexity: DFS/BFS run in O(V+E) with adjacency lists but O(V^2) with adjacency matrices, because visiting all neighbors of a vertex means scanning its whole matrix row (O(V)) versus just its list (O(degree)).

Graph traversal: DFS (stack) vs BFS (queue)

Depth-first search (DFS) explores as far as possible along each branch before backtracking, implemented with a stack or recursion; breadth-first search (BFS) explores all neighbors at the current distance before moving farther out, implemented with a queue.

Key facts & formulas
  • DFS uses a stack (explicit or via recursion's call stack); BFS uses a queue.
  • BFS gives shortest paths (fewest edges) in an unweighted graph; DFS does not guarantee this.
  • Both DFS and BFS are O(V+E) with adjacency lists, O(V^2) with adjacency matrices.
  • DFS is the basis for cycle detection, topological sort, and connected components/articulation points.
  • BFS processes vertices in strictly non-decreasing order of distance from the source.
  • Both algorithms mark vertices visited to avoid revisiting/infinite loops in graphs with cycles.
Traps
  • BFS gives shortest paths only for UNWEIGHTED graphs -- for weighted graphs, Dijkstra's or Bellman-Ford is needed, not plain BFS.
  • Recursive DFS implicitly uses the call stack -- a question asking to implement DFS without recursion expects an explicit stack, and neighbor order can differ depending on push order.
  • A single DFS/BFS call only covers one connected component of a disconnected graph, requiring an outer loop over all vertices for full coverage.
  • Both have the same time complexity O(V+E), but a question testing use-case (shortest unweighted path vs cycle detection vs topological order) is testing which algorithm fits which purpose, not complexity.
Full explanation

DFS: from a source vertex, mark it visited, then recursively (or via an explicit stack) visit an unvisited neighbor, going as deep as possible before backtracking to try other branches. Used for cycle detection, topological sorting (DAGs), connected components, and articulation points. BFS: from a source, mark it visited and enqueue it; repeatedly dequeue a vertex, visit all its unvisited neighbors, mark and enqueue them. This processes vertices in increasing order of distance from the source, which is why BFS finds shortest paths in an UNWEIGHTED graph -- the first time a vertex is reached is guaranteed to be via a shortest path. Both algorithms visit every vertex and edge exactly once in the standard formulation, giving O(V+E) time with an adjacency list. With an adjacency matrix, both degrade to O(V^2), since finding a vertex's neighbors means scanning a matrix row of length V rather than just its list of actual neighbors. Space: DFS uses O(h) for recursion/stack depth (up to O(V) worst case); BFS uses O(w) for the queue, where w is the maximum frontier width, up to O(V) worst case.

Disjoint sets: UNION, FIND, compression

A disjoint-set (union-find) data structure maintains a partition of elements into non-overlapping sets, supporting FIND (which set does an element belong to) and UNION (merge two sets), and is the standard structure for cycle detection in Kruskal's algorithm.

Key facts & formulas
  • FIND(x): follow parent pointers to the root, which identifies x's set.
  • UNION(x,y): merge the trees containing x and y by linking one root under the other.
  • Union by rank/size: attach the smaller/shorter tree under the larger/taller root, bounding height at O(log n).
  • Path compression: during FIND, re-point every visited node directly to the root, flattening future lookups.
  • Union by rank plus path compression together give amortized O(alpha(n)) per operation -- alpha is inverse Ackermann, effectively constant in practice.
  • Kruskal's algorithm uses union-find specifically to detect whether adding an edge would create a cycle (both endpoints already in the same set).
Traps
  • Without either optimization, naive union-find can degrade to O(n) per FIND -- 'near constant time' is a property of the OPTIMIZED structure, not union-find in general.
  • Alpha(n), the inverse Ackermann function, is not literally O(1) in the strict theoretical sense, even though it is always small enough to treat as constant -- a precise answer says 'O(alpha(n)), effectively constant', not flatly 'O(1)'.
  • Union by rank alone, or path compression alone, each already improve the bound to O(log n) amortized -- the O(alpha(n)) bound specifically requires BOTH together.
  • 'Rank' in union by rank is an upper bound on height, not an exact height once path compression starts flattening trees.
Full explanation

Each set is represented as a tree, every element pointing toward a parent, with the root serving as that set's representative. FIND(x) walks up parent pointers from x to the root and returns it; two elements are in the same set iff FIND returns the same root for both. UNION(x,y) finds each one's root and makes one root point to the other, merging the trees. A naive implementation can degenerate into a long chain, making FIND take O(n) worst case. Two independent optimizations fix this. Union by rank (or size): when merging, always attach the root of the smaller/shallower tree under the root of the larger/taller one, rather than arbitrarily -- this keeps tree height bounded at O(log n). Path compression: during a FIND call, after locating the root, re-point every node visited along the way directly to that root, flattening the tree for future FIND calls on those nodes. Used together, the amortized cost per operation is O(alpha(n)), where alpha is the inverse Ackermann function, a value that grows so slowly it is effectively at most 4 or 5 for any practically conceivable input size, so the structure is described as 'near-constant time' per operation, though the true worst case for a single operation is not strictly O(1).

Greedy method: Kruskal's and Prim's MST

The greedy method builds a solution step by step, always making the choice that looks best at the moment without reconsidering it -- provably optimal for problems like minimum spanning tree (MST) that have the greedy-choice and optimal-substructure properties, but not for all optimization problems.

Key facts & formulas
  • Kruskal's: sort edges by weight, add if no cycle forms (via union-find), stop at V-1 edges -- O(E log E), equal to O(E log V).
  • Prim's: grow one tree from a start vertex, always adding the cheapest edge to an outside vertex -- O(E log V) with a binary heap, O(V^2) with a simple array.
  • Both Kruskal's and Prim's are correct because MST satisfies the greedy-choice property and optimal substructure.
  • Kruskal's is edge-centric (suits sparse graphs); Prim's is vertex-centric (its O(V^2) form suits dense graphs).
  • Greedy is not universally correct -- 0/1 knapsack is a classic example where greedy fails to find the optimum, unlike fractional knapsack.
  • A graph has a unique MST if all edge weights are distinct; with ties, multiple equal-weight MSTs can exist.
Traps
  • Assuming greedy always gives the optimal answer for any optimization problem -- it is only provably correct for problems with the greedy-choice property (MST, Huffman, activity selection), and fails for 0/1 knapsack.
  • Kruskal's complexity is often stated as O(E log V) from memory without knowing it comes from the sort step O(E log E), which equals O(E log V) only because E is at most V^2.
  • Prim's O(V^2) array-based version can outperform the O(E log V) heap version on dense graphs, since E approaches V^2 and the heap's log factor stops paying off.
  • Kruskal's builds a forest that only becomes one tree at the end, unlike Prim's, which always maintains a single connected growing tree from the start.
Full explanation

A greedy algorithm is guaranteed correct only when the problem has the greedy-choice property (a locally optimal choice leads to a globally optimal solution) and optimal substructure (an optimal solution contains optimal solutions to subproblems). MST construction satisfies both, which is why two different greedy strategies both provably produce a correct MST. Kruskal's algorithm: sort all E edges by weight ascending; process them in order, adding an edge to the growing forest only if its endpoints are currently in different components, checked via union-find, since adding it otherwise would create a cycle; stop once V-1 edges are added. Complexity is dominated by the sort: O(E log E), equal to O(E log V) since E is at most V^2. Prim's algorithm: start from an arbitrary vertex and grow a single tree by repeatedly adding the cheapest edge connecting the current tree to a vertex not yet in it; implemented efficiently with a min-priority queue keyed on each outside vertex's cheapest connecting edge, giving O(E log V) with a binary heap plus adjacency list, or O(V^2) with a simple array scan, which can be faster for dense graphs. Greedy is not universal: 0/1 knapsack is a classic case where local optimal choices fail to give a global optimum.

Shortest path: Dijkstra, Bellman-Ford, F-W

Dijkstra's algorithm finds single-source shortest paths greedily using a priority queue for non-negative weights; Bellman-Ford handles negative edge weights and detects negative cycles via repeated edge relaxation; Floyd-Warshall computes all-pairs shortest paths by dynamic programming; Huffman coding applies the same greedy principle to build optimal prefix-free codes.

Key facts & formulas
  • Dijkstra's: O((V+E) log V) with a binary heap; requires all edge weights non-negative.
  • Bellman-Ford: O(VE); handles negative edges; detects negative-weight cycles via a Vth relaxation pass.
  • Floyd-Warshall: O(V^3) time, O(V^2) space; computes ALL-PAIRS shortest paths via dynamic programming over an intermediate vertex k.
  • Dijkstra and Prim's are structurally similar (greedy plus priority queue) but solve different problems: shortest path tree vs minimum spanning tree.
  • Huffman coding builds an optimal prefix-free code by greedily merging the two lowest-frequency nodes via a min-heap.
  • A shortest path in a graph with V vertices has at most V-1 edges -- this bounds Bellman-Ford's required relaxation passes.
Traps
  • Dijkstra's gives WRONG answers, not an error, on graphs with negative edges -- it finalizes vertices too early rather than detecting the problem; this is why Bellman-Ford is preferred with negative weights.
  • Bellman-Ford needs exactly V-1 relaxation passes for correctness on a graph with no negative cycle; the Vth pass is purely a negative-cycle detector, not part of normal operation.
  • Floyd-Warshall's O(V^3) can beat running Dijkstra V times on dense graphs -- which is faster for all-pairs depends on graph density, not a fixed universal answer.
  • Huffman coding is optimal specifically among prefix-free (uniquely decodable) codes -- it does not mean all codeword lengths are equal or that it beats every other compression scheme.
  • Prim's and Dijkstra's look almost identical in pseudocode, but Prim's compares to the EDGE weight alone while Dijkstra's compares to the cumulative PATH distance from the source -- swapping this is a classic bug.
Full explanation

Dijkstra's: maintain a tentative distance for every vertex, infinity except the source at 0; repeatedly extract the unvisited vertex with smallest tentative distance via a min-heap and relax its outgoing edges. Complexity O((V+E) log V) with a binary heap and adjacency list. It fails with negative edges because once a vertex is finalized, a later negative edge could still improve its distance, but Dijkstra never revisits it. Bellman-Ford: relax every one of the E edges, repeated V-1 times -- this guarantees convergence since a shortest path has at most V-1 edges; a Vth pass that still finds an improvement signals a negative-weight cycle. Complexity O(VE), slower than Dijkstra but correct with negative edges. Floyd-Warshall: dynamic programming over all vertex pairs, considering each vertex k as a possible intermediate point: dist[i][j] = min(dist[i][j], dist[i][k]+dist[k][j]), for all i,j, for each k. Gives all-pairs shortest paths in O(V^3) time, O(V^2) space, a simple triple-nested loop. Huffman coding: repeatedly extract the two lowest-frequency nodes from a min-heap and merge them into a new internal node, until one tree remains -- frequent symbols get shorter codes, proven optimal among prefix-free codes by a greedy exchange argument.

    4. DIn-order Traversal

    Loose. Preorder is the usual answer, but inorder and postorder are also depth-first orders — only level-order is not. The distinguishing fact worth learning is DFS→stack, BFS→queue.

    The recursive DFS of a graph, which visits a node and then descends into each unvisited neighbour, maps onto pre-order traversal of a binary tree. Strictly speaking in-order and post-order are also depth-first orders, so options B and D are defensible in a pedantic reading, but pre-order is the direct structural match and the intended key.

  3. 3
    Time Complexity of DFS is? (V – number of vertices, E – number of edges)
    1. AO(V + E)
    2. BO(V)
    3. CO(E)
    4. DO(V * E)
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    DFS visits every vertex once and traverses every edge once (twice in an undirected adjacency-list representation), giving O(V + E) with adjacency lists. An adjacency-matrix implementation would be O(V^2), which is not among the options.

    official keyProgrammer under Public Health Engineering Department, July 2018 — Technical Paper IPROG2018_P1_021

  4. 4
    If we choose Prim's Algorithm for uniquely weighted spanning tree instead of Kruskal's Algorithm, then
    1. Awe'll get a different spanning tree.
    2. Bwe'll get the same spanning tree.
    3. Cspanning will have less edges.
    4. Dspanning will not cover all vertices.
    Show answer

    Answer: B

    When all edge weights are distinct the minimum spanning tree is unique, so Prim's and Kruskal's must both return that same tree (they may add edges in a different order).

    official keyProgrammer under Public Health Engineering Department, July 2018 — Technical Paper IPROG2018_P1_026

  5. 5
    The time required to find shortest path in a graph with n vertices and e edges iscontested key
    1. AO(e)
    2. BO(n)
    3. CO(e2)
    4. DO(n2)
    Show answer
    Do not memorise this answer

    O(n²) holds for Dijkstra with an adjacency matrix. With a binary heap it is O((V+E) log V). The question never states the representation.

    Bank's answer: D

    The classic textbook figure for a shortest-path algorithm (e.g. Dijkstra) using an adjacency-matrix implementation is O(n^2); with an adjacency list and a priority queue it would instead be closer to O(e log n), but O(n^2) is the answer this style of question is built around.

    Only the Paper-I-syllabus questions from this paper were imported (88 of 149).

    derived · mediumInspector of Legal Metrology, March 2010 · Paper IIILM2010_P2_020

  6. 6
    In which algorithm or problem-solving technique is Union-Find commonly used?contested key
    1. ADepth-First Search (DFS)
    2. BBinary Search
    3. CQuick Sort
    4. DBreadth-First Search (BFS) a a ee ee eee FO
    Show answer
    Do not memorise this answer

    Union–Find's canonical use is Kruskal's MST (cycle detection), which is not among the options. The keyed DFS is not where Union–Find belongs.

    Bank's answer: A

    Union-Find (disjoint set) is most often paired with depth-first search as an alternative technique for detecting cycles/connected components in a graph (it's also central to Kruskal's MST algorithm, which isn't one of the options here); I'm not fully confident this is the pairing intended, since none of the four options is its most standard textbook use.

    Only the Paper-I-syllabus questions from this paper were imported (97 of 98).

    derived · lowInspector of Legal Metrology, November 2023 · Paper IILM2023_P1_094

  7. 7
    Which of the following requires less memory?
    1. ADFS
    2. BBFS
    3. CBoth (a) and (b)
    4. DNone of these
    Show answer

    Answer: A

    DFS memory usage is bounded by the depth of the tree/graph, while BFS must hold an entire frontier level, generally larger.

    derived · unratedJr. Grade of MES, November 2015MES2015_PAPER1_008

  8. 8
    A union find data-structure is commonly applied while implementing
    1. AA depth-first search traversal of a graph
    2. BA breadth-first search traversal of a graph
    3. CThe computation of the minimum spanning tree of a graph
    4. DThe computation of the all-pairs shortest path in a graph
    Show answer

    Answer: C

    Kruskal's MST algorithm uses union-find (disjoint set union) to detect cycles efficiently while adding edges.

    Recovered from page 5 of the source PDF -- this question and Q42-50 were entirely missing from the OCR text supplied for this task (it stopped at Q40).

    derived · highJr. Grade of MES, November 2015MES2015_PAPER1_041

  9. 9
    An adjacency matrix representation of a graph cannot contain information of
    1. AEdges
    2. BDirection of Edges
    3. CNodes
    4. DParallel Edges
    Show answer

    Answer: D

    A basic adjacency matrix stores one value per vertex pair, so it can't represent multiple (parallel) edges between the same two vertices without extending it to store counts/weights.

    Only the Paper-I-syllabus questions from this paper were imported (50 of 51).
